  • backburner: Let Your iPhone Help Your Mac Run a 27B Model Over USB-C
    summary

    TL;DR: Backburner is a pre-release, MIT-licensed project that uses a USB-C-connected iPhone as a co-processor so a Mac runs Qwen3.8-27B locally with faster prefill and much longer context.

    • Engine: A fork of llama.cpp with custom Mac kernels, including SME2 CPU paths, Metal fusions and DFlash2 speculative decoding.
    • Split prefill: The Mac runs layers 1-40, the iPhone GPU runs layers 41-64, pipelined in 256-token ubatches; only new KV rows cross the cable.
    • Long context: Past the Mac’s 64k context cells, the oldest 4,096-key pages move to the phone, which computes attention over them and merges partial results with the Mac. Older 16,384-key pages can be compiled into Core ML models for the phone’s Neural Engine.
    • Numbers: On a 24 GB M4 Pro MacBook Pro with an iPhone 17 Pro Max, prefill is 29-44% faster at 16k-48k context (109 to 157 tok/s at 16k). A cold session’s first answer drops from 245 s on stock llama.cpp to 168 s.
    • Decode: About 25 tok/s at 27-33k context with the fork versus 11.3 tok/s on stock llama.cpp, but the phone adds nothing there. At 128k with the phone it is 12.6 tok/s. The Mac Neural Engine is deliberately unused: it slowed decode by 26% through memory-bandwidth contention.
    • Capacity: The phone’s free memory lets total context reach a reported 196k-229k tokens. End-to-end tests reach 128k at 8-bit and 140k at 4-bit, and greedy output is claimed token-identical with and without the phone.
    • Limits: One request at a time; the app must stay in the foreground with the screen on; a 15 s silence stops the server; a phone failure disables it for 60 s. Only Pro iPhones with a 10 Gb/s cable work, since the boxed USB 2 cable is too slow. Thermals vary: the 16 Pro Max hits “serious” thermal state within about a minute of continuous prefill.
    • Setup: A one-line install script (about 24 GB download) or a manual build; the phone app is sideloaded via AltStore or Xcode. The result is an OpenAI-compatible API on localhost:8080. Manual setup raises the GPU wired memory limit with sudo sysctl, which resets on reboot.
    • Security and caveats: Versions before 0.0.3 accepted Wi-Fi connections from any source; newer ones answer only the USB-attached Mac or a previously paired Mac over an encrypted, authenticated tunnel. All benchmarks are self-reported, and the README credits Claude Opus 5.5 for much of the build.

    Takeaway: An interesting local-inference hack for keeping code and prompts off third-party APIs. If you run it, update past 0.0.3 or disable Wi-Fi on the phone app.

  • CodeAF: Open-Source Software Factory for Open Models
    summary

    TL;DR: CodeAF is an early-preview, Apache 2.0 coding harness from AgentField AI, a “software factory for open models” shipped as a single 53 MB Go binary.

    • Interface: One window replaces multiple agent terminals, with a home view of every project and conversation, tabs, and a jump-to-any-conversation shortcut.
    • Task isolation: A request splits into tasks, each in its own repository copy on its own branch. Work that passes its check lands on your branch, never on main, dev or a release branch; unverified work waits in an “unread” queue for manual accept or reject.
    • Model seats: Each task has worker, planner and checker seats chosen per task. Commands pin models, pick best or cheap crews, or redo with a stronger model; default spend limit is $5 per task.
    • Specialists: senior-dev for code changes, sec for local security audits (imported from SEC-AF), review for GitHub pull requests (imported from PR-AF). Reports come in Markdown, JSON and SARIF.
    • Providers: OpenRouter, DeepSeek, GLM, Kimi, MiniMax, Qwen, Codex via a ChatGPT plan, Ollama and any OpenAI-compatible endpoint.
    • Automation and CI: Plain-language automations (reminders, schedules, watches); headless codeaf do prints one JSON object and an exit code for CI; remote use attaches over SSH to the machine that owns the work.
    • Numbers (self-run): 54.9% solved on 113 DeepSWE issues with DeepSeek V4 Flash, at a cost per solved task 3.4x lower than claude-code and 2.1x lower than codex. 27 MB RAM per added session versus 216 MB for claude; 507 MB versus 3.5 GB for 16 idle sessions; 145 ms versus 392 ms to resume a 50-turn session.
    • Limits: The README admits benchmarks are self-run and PR-AF’s published results apply to the original project, not the imported specialist. sec and review only read code without running tests or exploits, so no findings does not mean secure. Specialists run on macOS and Linux only; non-SSH remote access depends on a relay that has not launched.
    • Privacy defaults: Anonymous usage telemetry is on (disable with CODEAF_TELEMETRY=off or DO_NOT_TRACK=1). A “Model Pool” shares text-free model measurements by default (disable with model_pool = off). Install is a curl-pipe-to-bash script.

    Takeaway: Branch-isolated agent tasks with a checker gate, SARIF output for existing pipelines and a cheap headless CI mode are the useful ideas. Turn off telemetry and pool sharing and pin a version before trusting it.

  • Vulnerability Discovery and Exploitation Trends in the AI Era
    summary

    TL;DR: Google Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG) analyses vulnerabilities disclosed January 2025 through August 2026 and argues AI is changing the pace of discovery and exploitation and the kind of bugs found.

    • Method: Ratings use GTIG’s own risk scores, not CVSS.
    • Volume: Monthly CVE disclosures roughly doubled, from 5,045 in January 2026 to a peak of 10,740 in August. Automated CNA policies inflate the count: about 5,000 “Linux Kernel” CVEs appeared in eight months with no observed in-the-wild zero-day exploitation.
    • High-Risk: Up 167%, from 131 to 350 per month, but still only about 3% of the total; part of the spike came from TOTOLINK router research and Oracle and Linux advisories.
    • Exploitation: Nearly doubled, from 10.5 to 18 vulnerabilities per month; 141 exploited in the first eight months of 2026 versus 127 in all of 2025. Only 0.23% of 2026 disclosures (about 1 in 431) were seen exploited.
    • Zero-days vs n-days: Zero-days grew modestly, from 8 to 11 per month on average (22 in August), and are still 62% of exploited bugs. Growth mostly comes from fast n-day weaponization, likely using LLMs to diff patches and work from PoCs. Edge and security appliances are 14% of exploited flaws, often via unauthenticated management interfaces where EDR has no visibility.
    • AI-found bugs: 50% lead to RCE versus 26% ecosystem-wide; 58% rate Medium versus 28% for non-AI finds. GTIG calls this an early signal, since public data undercounts AI attribution.
    • Case study: CVE-2026-1731 in BeyondTrust PRA and Remote Support, an unauthenticated command injection found by the Hacktron AI agent. One cluster exploited it within four days and five more within a week, with SNOWLIGHT, SPARKRAT and cryptominer payloads.
    • AI stack: 2,076 AI-stack CVEs since January 2025. Agent orchestration frameworks (Langflow, Flowise, LangChain, Dify) are half of AI-related flaws in 2026 after a 347% surge; inference servers (vLLM, Ollama, LiteLLM) account for 212. Confirmed exploited: LiteLLM CVE-2026-42271 (command injection in MCP test endpoints), Langflow CVE-2026-5027 (path traversal file write), Langflow CVE-2025-3248 (unauthenticated exec RCE). No zero-day exploitation of AI infrastructure yet, but GTIG notes an intercepted AI-generated zero-day exploit script and actors prototyping agentic vulnerability discovery.
    • Recommendations: Replace mass patching with threat-intel-driven triage; prioritise perimeter appliances; adopt AI-assisted vulnerability management; sandbox and restrict exposure of AI orchestration, gateway and inference components; vendors should run AI code review before release.

    Takeaway: Patch by exploitation and exposure, not CVE volume, and assume n-day windows of days. Treat self-hosted Langflow, LiteLLM, vLLM and Ollama as high-value internet-facing targets that must never sit unauthenticated on the network.

  • Escaping Space, Part I
    summary

    TL;DR: Perplexity red-teamed SPACE, the Firecracker-microVM sandbox behind Perplexity Computer, showing VM isolation and network confinement are separate boundaries that must be tested separately.

    • Setup: Each agent runs in a Firecracker microVM; a per-sandbox gateway enforces egress policy. Agents with root in the guest tried to steal an AWS honeytoken from the host (sandbox escape) or fetch a flag from a blocked callback URL (network bypass).
    • Settings: Four combinations of source code access with no network or partial network (package repositories plus Perplexity Search).
    • Models: Nine tested over 216 runs: Claude Opus 5.0, GPT-5.6 Sol, GPT-5.6 Cyber, Gemini 3.1 Pro, Kimi K3, DeepSeek V4 Pro, GLM 5.2, Grok 4.20 and Qwen 3.8 27B. Fable and GPT-6 Astra refused the tasks.
    • Results: Sandbox escape 0 of 108; network bypass 0 of 54 no-network runs, but 11 of 54 partial-network runs bypassed egress. Claude Opus 5.0 and GPT-5.6 Sol succeeded 3 of 3 times with source code.
    • Root cause: Every bypass exploited the gap between domain-based policy and IP-based enforcement, since the gateway learned domain-to-IP mappings from observed DNS.
    • DNS spoofing: The root agent forged DNS answers mapping pypi.org to the callback’s IP, aimed at a veth address only discoverable from source code; the gateway accepted them.
    • Shared IP: Agents reached the callback through other tenants on an allowed CDN IP, such as Fastly Fiddle or a Taboola image service that used thum.io to screenshot the flag, read via OCR. Opus spotted this route but declined it as out of scope.
    • Fix: An nftables source-address validation rule against forged packets, an HTTP relay that validates authority, and TLS termination requiring SNI and Host to be allowlisted and DNS-attributed to the destination IP. Re-tests found no bypass.
    • Third parties: Of ten platforms tested, seven of the nine published had at least one bypass: E2B, Vercel Sandbox, Modal, Daytona, Deno Sandbox, Fly.io Sprites, microsandbox. Cloudflare Sandbox and NVIDIA OpenShell held. Responses: fixes (microsandbox v0.6.18, Daytona, Deno), planned mitigations (E2B, Fly.io), documented limitations (Vercel, Modal); Deno also allowed direct TCP to unlisted IPs and ports.

    Takeaway: Never allowlist by destination IP on shared CDN infrastructure, keep DNS, SNI and Host consistent across layers, and drop spoofed source addresses from guests. A passing time-boxed test does not prove the boundary is sound.

  • Expanding the Cyber Verification Program
    summary

    TL;DR: On October 6, 2026 Anthropic expanded its Cyber Verification Program (CVP), giving vetted security professionals frontier models with fewer cyber-blocking classifiers.

    • Merger: Combines Project Glasswing (Claude Mythos access for critical-software organisations) with the original CVP (reduced safeguards on Opus and Sonnet for vetted teams).
    • Rationale: Cybersecurity is inherently dual use, so generally available models ship conservative safeguards that block most offensive-looking work. All tiers include Claude Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5.5, Mythos 5.1 and future models.
    • Defense Access: SOC work, incident response, malware reverse engineering, vulnerability validation. Open to company, nonprofit, university and government security teams, critical infrastructure operators such as hospitals and utilities, small security firms, open-source maintainers and individual researchers with a disclosure record. Responses promised within days.
    • Red Team Access: Adds authorised penetration testing against systems the organisation may test. Limited to in-house, government and pentest-firm red teams (not individuals); review takes a few weeks; still blocks actions like deploying ransomware or damaging physical systems.
    • Specialized Access: Fewest blocks, for a small set of organisations testing safety-critical systems such as flight software, power grids, telecom and interbank networks. Each applicant is reviewed with the US government; existing Glasswing members move here automatically.
    • Data retention: Enrolled organisations must retain data so Anthropic can monitor misuse. Enterprise Frontier Safeguards, later in fall 2026, will allow storage in customer-controlled cloud infrastructure.
    • Numbers: On CyScenarioBench with Opus 5.5, every task was blocked at the first prompt without CVP; 46 of 50 trials were blocked at some point under Defense Access; Red Team Access completed 34 of 50, matching the unrestricted 67.6% success rate. Glasswing partners verified at least 129,000 vulnerabilities between April and July 2026, Anthropic’s own open-source scanning found 5,500 more, over 33,000 rated critical or high, called a lower bound.
    • Enrolment: Apply via Anthropic’s portal with verification and proof of security controls; admins must assign the program to specific workspaces; existing members are automatically evaluated for the new models. There is a false-positive appeal form.
    • Availability: Claude Platform, Google Vertex AI and Microsoft Foundry; on Amazon Bedrock only for customers eligible for Enterprise Frontier Safeguards.

    Takeaway: Routine code review, patching known issues and alert triage still work on standard models. Malware analysis or red-team automation will hit blocks unless your team enrols, and enrolment means accepting data retention for misuse monitoring.

  • The AI SDLC Transformation Playbook
    summary

    TL;DR: Atlassian’s playbook argues an AI-native SDLC is more than handing developers coding agents: once coding goes asynchronous and multi-threaded, the bottleneck moves to planning, design, review and maintenance.

    • Structure: Seven pillars, two platform investments and five workflow shifts, all depending on work being captured in systems of record.
    • Context graph: Links repositories, work items, docs, standards, meeting recordings and chat behind a permissions-aware layer. Atlassian’s Teamwork Graph draws on more than 50 sources, is reachable via its apps, IDEs, terminals, MCP or CLI, and feeds agent activity back so context compounds.
    • Measurement: Pair system data with qualitative developer surveys; Atlassian uses DX, combining data from Jira, Cursor and Claude Code.
    • Planning and design: Agents synthesise customer needs and code context into specs while humans refine and decide (Jira Product Discovery, Jira). Design becomes a continuous thread with live prototypes, designers contributing UI code, and Loom video feedback turned into revision prompts.
    • Development and review: Quality depends on the context supplied to agents; speed depends on orchestrating several. AI checks changes first against centrally defined standards, humans make the final call (Atlassian’s AI Reviewer alongside tools such as Cursor).
    • Maintenance: Agents classify errors, draft root-cause analyses and handle routine fixes, using Jira Service Management and “governed agent loops” that scan the backlog and codebase.
    • Numbers (vendor-internal): Agents with Teamwork Graph access were 44% more accurate with 48% fewer tokens; issue cycle time fell from 11.5 to 6.7 days; Jira agent loops automatically resolve 51% of potential vulnerabilities. Cites a 2026 survey of more than 1,000 software professionals, The Agentic Pivot.
    • Five moves: Anchor on connected, permissioned context; invest in measurement early; start deep in one high-value workflow; keep humans accountable with scoped, auditable agent loops; change rituals, roles and metrics rather than just buying tools.

    Takeaway: The piece promotes Atlassian products, but the point holds: treating AI adoption as a licence purchase yields incremental gains. The leverage is permissioned context, centrally defined review standards, and measured, auditable agent automation for KTLO and vulnerability backlog work.

  • Countering Misuse of AI: September 2026 Threat Intelligence Report
    summary

    TL;DR: Anthropic’s September 2026 threat report covers Haiku, Sonnet and Opus misuse it disrupted from December 2025 to August 2026, arguing AI erased the gap between state operations and lone operators.

    • Scope and thesis: Cyber operations, AI supply-chain abuse, influence operations, surveillance, weapons, biology, fraud and illicit distillation. Sophistication no longer indicates who is behind an attack; autonomy raises scale and speed more than severity.
    • GTG-20006: Russian state-nexus group linked to Midnight Blizzard used Claude to build phishing infrastructure and tooling and rebuild implants until undetected. Targeted more than 20 organisations; breached a North African government authority holding over 300,000 identity records.
    • GTG-50014: ShinyHunters affiliates downloaded and decompiled 1.8 million Android APKs and scanned them for secrets with TruffleHog. One intrusion harvested over 2,100 Azure AD token sets across more than 40 tenants in about 34 hours.
    • GTG-10007: A Chinese-speaking group ran “agent swarms” with a lead agent, persistent campaign memory and an exploit foundry producing more than a dozen possible network-appliance zero-days in a month against about 50 organisations.
    • GTG-50020: Prompt injection against an AI vendor’s evaluation sandbox yielded production API keys, then about 30 AI companies were hit in four days trying to reach a pre-release Claude model; Anthropic was not compromised. Also: a fake cheap-Claude reseller harvesting credentials, and French-speaking hacktivists exploiting a previously undocumented WordPress re-installation race condition to steal about 140,000 political campaign records.
    • Influence ops: A Russian operation in the Central African Republic via a local radio station; a French ad agency running about 70 fake news sites with at least 8,913 articles in about 20 languages; an Istanbul firm’s Malaysian election platform with about 1,000 fake X accounts.
    • Surveillance and weapons: Iranian units built case-management and phishing tooling; a Malian SIM-tracking system covered about 25 million SIM cards; several Chinese profiling efforts. In one Iranian case, splitting tasks into smaller sessions got past safeguards that refused 9 of 10 direct requests. A Yemeni cell used Claude Code as guidance-software engineer for rockets and missiles; a Russian freelance team built an autonomous FPV drone swarm.
    • Fraud and distillation: A Chinese app studio ran more than 4,700 AI dating personas engaging over 25,000 people with about 2.36 million messages in two weeks. Distillation is attributed to seven China-based labs, including Alibaba peaking near 3 million exchanges a day from over 3,500 accounts, and Moonshot, DeepSeek, Zhipu and Xiaomi relaying customer traffic through proxy “transfer stations.”
    • Response: Static detections cannot keep up with AI-driven adaptation; Anthropic calls for account and institutional signals, retained data for misuse detection, trusted access programs and threat sharing, and published an IOC list.

    Takeaway: Treat AI API keys and agent integrations as production credentials, and scan your own apps, containers and repos for exposed keys before attackers do. Buy AI access only through authorised channels, and expect token theft and appliance exploitation at machine speed.

  • How Cloudflare Enforces Engineering Standards Using AI
    summary

    TL;DR: Cloudflare describes the Cloudflare Codex, a governed set of engineering standards that AI agents retrieve and apply during development, review and incident follow-up.

    • Problem: Guidance was scattered across docs, repos, chat and people’s heads; engineers could not tell what was current or authoritative, reviewers could not check every requirement, and drift built up between projects.
    • Structure: Domains such as architecture, security, reliability, TypeScript and Rust, each with an owner. Standards are RFCs using RFC 2119 MUST and SHOULD keywords plus front-matter metadata.
    • Process: Anyone with domain competence proposes an RFC via merge request; it goes through review rounds and the domain owner approves it before publishing to an internal Astro site.
    • Staged enforcement: An approved RFC only produces non-blocking findings. MUST violations block merges only after the RFC is explicitly promoted to “enforced,” giving teams time to adapt.
    • Context handling: 60+ RFCs would overwhelm a model’s context window, so an agent extracts each MUST and SHOULD into structured JSON with a stable slug that survives RFC edits, enabling lazy loading and long-term tracking.
    • AI code reviewer: Checks merge requests for Codex compliance and pulls full RFC text only when needed. Since launch it flagged close to 230,000 violations; almost 16,000 withheld approval for breaking MUST statements on enforced RFCs.
    • Linters and CLI: Mechanically checkable rules go into linters (oxlint for TypeScript first, Rust in progress, Go planned). A local CLI runs the same OpenCode-based agents on your diff to skip the CI round trip.
    • Spec and incident reviewers: The spec reviewer (Cloudflare Worker, D1 state, AI Gateway, Cron Trigger) reviewed nearly 600 design specs across over 3,200 invocations since May 2026; findings 65% major, 29% minor, 6% critical. The incident report reviewer assessed over 200 postmortems, catching missing action items, incomplete timelines and omitted detection signals; it is now mandatory for high-severity incidents in central review.
    • Next: Cover the whole SDLC, let agents propose fixes with engineers still approving, and extend the Codex to product, security, compliance and trust and safety.

    Takeaway: Write standards as machine-parseable RFCs with stable IDs and deliver them at the point of work through AI review. Push anything deterministic into linters, start advisory, block only after explicit promotion, and keep humans owning final approval.

  • Hacking AI Customer Service Agents
    summary

    TL;DR: Intigriti’s write-up of Inti De Ceukelaire’s DEF CON 34 Bug Bounty Village talk shows AI customer service agents grow as an attack surface as they gain tools.

    • Thesis and payout: Human oversight does not fix it, because risk appears exactly where a human stops deciding and an over-privileged agent acts. The research reportedly earned over $50,000 in bounties over a few weekends without Burp Suite or automated scanners.
    • Email spoofing: A spoofed From header made chatbots treat a message as the victim’s, enabling phishing from the company’s own support address and leaking billing or profile data to an attacker via CC. RFC 822 allows multiple From headers, so SPF can validate one address while the agent looks up the account under another and replies to an attacker-controlled Sender. A signed victim email can be produced without spoofing by triggering an out-of-office auto-reply whose subject carries the attacker’s instruction.
    • Auth weaknesses: Adding a comment to an email address made the rate limiter see a new identity, giving three extra MFA guesses. IVR checks based on caller ID, last four SSN digits or emailed OTPs were spoofable or brute-forceable, and channel switching reset counters.
    • Email address smuggling: An RFC 5322 comment like attacker(&email=victim@victim.org&)@attacker.com in the local part is stripped by the mail layer but passed raw by the agent into a query string, so the backend returns the victim’s profile.
    • OTP exfiltration: Prime the agent to watch for the next email, trigger a real password reset, then trick it into fetching an attacker URL with the OTP as a subdomain visible in DNS logs. A Chrome built-in AI variant was marked Won’t Fix by Google.
    • Asymmetric messaging: Human and agent see different content via differing text/plain and HTML parts, zero-opacity CSS or user-agent-dependent images. Forged quoted replies posing as earlier operator approvals produced a $4,200 refund. Path traversal in an offer ID swapped a real API response for a crafted JSON file uploaded as an avatar.
    • Spray and poisoning: Blind-copying predictable aliases such as support-1000 to support-1099 sprayed a prompt injection into any live ticket thread. A forum comment about a nonexistent promo code was later cited as fact by the RAG crawler. Registering a username like “sitemap” put attacker content where crawlers fetch automatically, with loc tags enabling SSRF and XXE.
    • Defenses: Never treat From headers as identity; normalise identifiers identically across mail, agent and backend layers; rate-limit per account, not per input string. Treat email bodies, quoted history, KB entries and crawled pages as untrusted.

    Takeaway: Give agent tools least privilege with stronger confirmation for sensitive actions, restrict agent URL fetching and sanitise IDs. Reserve sensitive usernames and paths, and keep official content separate from user-generated content in RAG pipelines.

  • HarnessTax: How Much Does the Harness Matter for Coding Agents?
    summary

    TL;DR: HarnessTax, from UC Berkeley and Arena Intelligence researchers including Ion Stoica and Matei Zaharia, measures how much the coding-agent harness matters versus the model: little for success, a lot for cost.

    • Setup: 21 model-harness pairs from seven models and three harnesses (Claude Code, Codex CLI, minimal open-source Pi) on the same 30 randomly sampled tasks from SWE-bench Lite and Terminal-Bench 2.0. A harness is the software managing a model’s tools, context and execution.
    • Method: Three runs per task at the harness’s high-effort setting with a 100-turn cap. Costs use a fixed direct-API price list dated September 1, 2026; confidence intervals use 10,000 bootstrap resamples.
    • Finding 1, cost: Harness barely moves success but the same model can reach similar success at up to 5x the cost. Claude Fable 5 solved 97.8% of SWE-bench Lite in Claude Code versus 96.7% in Codex and Pi, yet Claude Code cost about twice Pi ($1.33 versus $0.67).
    • Cost ratios: Across shared models, Claude Code cost about 2.0x Pi and 1.6x Codex on SWE-bench Lite and 1.5x Pi on Terminal-Bench 2.0. The harness effect on success stayed within about ±2% and ±5% respectively.
    • Finding 2, simple works: Pi has only four tools (read, write, edit, bash) yet sits on the cost-success Pareto frontier on both benchmarks. Turn counts were nearly identical (15.4 versus 15.3 per attempt for Fable 5), but Claude Code’s initial context averaged more than 10x Pi’s due to longer instructions and larger tool schemas, which helps explain higher spend per turn.
    • Finding 3, vendor harness not best: Across six Anthropic and OpenAI models, an alternative harness gave the best observed success in nine of twelve comparisons. Sonnet 4.6 hit 68.9% in Codex versus 66.7% in Claude Code; GPT-5.6 Sol hit 83.3% in Pi versus 78.9% in Codex on Terminal-Bench 2.0, at roughly half the cost.
    • Other models: GPT-5.6 Luna was cheapest on both benchmarks; open-weight Kimi K3 came close to the frontier.
    • Limits: Results cover two open benchmarks the models may have seen in training and may differ on real workloads. The authors plan to release profiling traces.

    Takeaway: Benchmark the same model across harnesses on your own workloads, and watch initial context size and tool schema bloat as cost drivers. Do not assume the vendor’s own harness is the best or cheapest pairing.

  • Containers Are No Longer a Security Boundary
    summary

    TL;DR: depthfirst researcher Zhenpeng (Leo) Lin argues containers are no longer a security boundary, since they share one Linux kernel and AI makes kernel bugs far easier to find and exploit.

    • The bug: CVE-2026-80521, a heap use-after-free in the kernel’s AF_UNIX garbage collector for SCM_RIGHTS messages (which pass file descriptors between sockets), affecting struct unix_vertex.
    • Discovery: Found with dfs-large1, depthfirst’s in-house vulnerability-detection model, plus their harness. Used to win a Google kernelCTF slot with a zero-day exploit on July 24, 2026.
    • Root cause 1: The kernel publishes SCM graph edges before queuing the socket buffer carrying the references. Because unix_add_edges() briefly drops unix_gc_lock, the garbage collector can run in that window.
    • Root cause 2: unix_del_edge() can free a vertex without removing it from its scc_entry ring. If another socket in the same strongly connected component survives, the next GC pass walks the stale ring via unix_walk_scc_fast() and dereferences freed memory.
    • Reach: Triggerable from ordinary unprivileged system calls, so namespaces, cgroups and seccomp-bpf do not stop it. Affects container runtimes and OS-level sandboxes such as nsjail, Firejail and Bubblewrap.
    • Timeline: Reported to security@kernel.org on August 5; OpenAI’s Kyle had reported the same bug; upstream patch landed August 6. At publication on September 22 it was still unpatched in Ubuntu 26.04. depthfirst released a container escape exploit for Ubuntu 26.04 and another for CVE-2026-52910 on Ubuntu 24.04.
    • Trend: 5,976 unique Linux kernel CVEs published January 1 to September 16, 2026; August alone had 1,650, over 27% of the year’s total. Of 36 publicly disclosed kernelCTF CVEs, 13 were reachable from ordinary interfaces such as epoll, futex, POSIX timers and AF_UNIX sockets.
    • Recommendation: Stop relying on a shared kernel to isolate sensitive, untrusted or multi-tenant workloads; move them into microVMs such as Firecracker or Kata Containers, which give each workload its own kernel.

    Takeaway: On Kubernetes or CI runners, assume a determined tenant can escape a container and put untrusted code (CI jobs, AI agent sandboxes, customer workloads) behind a VM boundary. Track the gap between upstream kernel fixes and your distribution’s patched packages.

  • Hacking OpenAI: Heap Overflow and SSO Misconfiguration to Compromise Internal Repositories
    summary

    TL;DR: Hacktron AI chained a libheif memory-corruption bug into OpenAI employees’ ChatGPT and Codex accounts and OpenAI’s internal repositories in under 72 hours, arguing exploit complexity no longer protects ordinary software.

    • Thesis: AI removed the protection that exploit complexity used to give; “security assumptions must catch up with attacker capabilities.”
    • Entry point: A heap buffer overflow in libheif, reachable because Discourse hands HEIC/HEIF uploads to ImageMagick, giving attacker-controlled files out-of-bounds read and write primitives during decoding.
    • Patch gap: Upstream fixed the bug the year before, but it was never labelled a security fix and got no CVE. Discourse’s Debian 12 image still shipped libheif 1.19.7; Debian only published DSA-6417-1 on August 8, 2026.
    • Exploit build: Opus 4.8 produced a working exploit with ASLR disabled but could not make it reliable with ASLR on. Claude Opus 5, released the same evening, built a working ARM64 exploit in about three hours. The team ported it to x86-64 and Discourse’s jemalloc setup, reaching reliable local RCE with ASLR enabled by 06:00 UTC on July 25.
    • Model use: Claude ran in an autonomous goal loop against their own Discourse Cloud instance. The model refused to write exploits for remote targets, so they proxied traffic to make the target look like a CTF forum. Models also helped with privilege escalation, lateral movement and evading defenses, though skilled human steering was still needed.
    • Pivot: From RCE on community.openai.com, they abused “Sign in with OpenAI” SSO to take over an employee’s ChatGPT and Codex accounts. That Codex account was linked to OpenAI’s GitHub organization, used to open a harmless PR in the internal monorepo.
    • Disclosure: OpenAI confirmed a fix about 14 hours after the Bugcrowd report and paid $6,500, covering only the OpenAI-side SSO finding, since the Discourse-hosted forum was out of scope. Discourse published GHSA-vhm9-85gw-x335 on July 28; its fix included sandboxing image processing.
    • HEIF Heist: A wider two-month project: three researchers spent under $3,000 in tokens and adapted the exploit to each new target in one or two days. Named targets include Slack, Meta, GitHub Enterprise, Rails and Next.js/Astro/Gatsby. Of thousands of malicious images sent, only Shopify noticed, because its image processors kept crashing.
    • Fixes: For self-hosted Discourse, rebuild the container image; a web update alone does not replace the vulnerable native library. Update libheif and libde265 and check distribution advisories, since backported fixes may hide behind old version numbers. Disable HEIF/AVIF decoding where not needed, restrict formats with an ImageMagick policy, or run image processing in hardened, ephemeral sandboxes.

    Takeaway: Patch native image libraries by rebuilding images, and sandbox image processing. Treat SSO-linked internal tools, such as coding agents connected to GitHub, as part of the blast radius of any peripheral web app.

  • Attacker Infrastructure, but Vibe-Coded: Tracking the Evolution of Credential Harvesting Platforms
    summary

    TL;DR: Datadog Security Labs researcher Julie Agnes Sparks analyses Loot and UltraVault, two “vibe coded” attacker credential harvesting platforms that treat LLMjacking as a product category.

    • Context: Tracked since July 2026; both treat LLMjacking alongside cloud, SaaS and database access. Builds on Google Threat Intelligence Group’s report of an exposed “Recon” framework managing more than 23,800 harvested secrets.
    • Exposure: Both dashboards were reachable without authentication, so anyone could browse them, search by victim domain and filter by category.
    • Loot: A simple plaintext credential catalog with a search box and validate button, holding 26,733 keys. AI category: 1,778 records, 196 live, across 15 providers, including 699 OpenAI keys (93 live), 666 Gemini (55 live) and 114 Anthropic (10 live). The OpenAI tab shows which models each key reaches and remaining credit. Parsing is poor, with misclassified keys and non-credential entries, yet many still work.
    • UltraVault: A much more capable post-exploitation console claiming 42,286 secrets, about 2.7% live. Targets are grouped under exploit chains such as react2shell (CVE-2025-55182), wp2shell, xss2shell and joomla2shell.
    • UltraVault features: An /api/pairs endpoint matches AWS access-key IDs and secret keys collected from different dumps, validates the rebuilt pair and returns account ID, ARN and region. A matrix evaluates 14 local privilege-escalation bugs with go/no-go verdicts. It also re-opens WordPress shells, mints persistent credentials, and runs cloud and Kubernetes recon from compromised Node processes.
    • Bedrock abuse: The host serving both dashboards validated stolen Amazon Bedrock credentials: GetCallerIdentity, then ListFoundationModels and ListInferenceProfiles, then bursts of dozens of InvokeModel calls to several Anthropic models across regions in under a minute. In September the actor added DeepSeek, Mistral, Kimi and GLM-5.
    • Fingerprints: A Boto3 user agent containing “kali-cloud”, a bare Python-urllib/3.13 user agent, and validation of temporary STS keys with the ASIA prefix.
    • Detections: Alert on Bedrock model enumeration with long-term keys, multi-region InvokeModel bursts, InvokeModel calls from new ASNs, and impossible travel on IAM user keys.
    • Ethics: Datadog withheld indicators to protect victims and did not use any exposed credentials.

    Takeaway: LLMs let even unskilled operators build full validation and post-exploitation pipelines, so leaked keys get tested and used fast. Treat any leaked AI provider key as a direct cost and abuse risk and deploy the Bedrock detections above.

  • laya: Non-Autoregressive System 1 Decision Engine for Typed Decisions in a Single Forward Pass
    summary

    TL;DR: Laya is an Apache-2.0, multilingual, non-autoregressive “System 1” decision engine that answers typed questions about an input in a single encoder forward pass instead of generating text.

    • Inputs and types: Takes text, an email, a ticket or JSON. “choice” picks one label from a set with a probability per option; “score” places the input on an ordinal scale; “noul” returns the probability a yes/no statement is true.
    • Checkpoints: An English model on ModernBERT-large (421M parameters), a multilingual mmBERT-base model (322M parameters, up to 8,192 tokens), and a model fine-tuned for typed decisions.
    • Router: Pure-Python; detects script and language and sends each request to the right checkpoint, keeping at most two models loaded by default.
    • Calibration caveat: Probabilities are trained with RLCD against strictly proper scoring rules, yet the README admits both shipped checkpoints are over-confident and the multilingual one has no fitted temperatures. Calibrate thresholds on your own data.
    • Interfaces: Python SDK, laya CLI with batch and preset modes, FastAPI server with a web playground and Jev-compatible /v1/systemone endpoints, and an MCP server. Integrates with LangChain/LangGraph, LlamaIndex and CrewAI; client SDKs for TypeScript (including local ONNX), Go, Rust, Java and .NET.
    • Features: Audit and redaction hooks, schema-driven decide() with JSON Schema or Pydantic, windowed long-document prediction, abstention via min_confidence, and presets for routing, guardrails, moderation, triage and email.
    • Numbers: On a T4 GPU, 33 ms per single question or 7.2 ms per question batched. Routing lifts XNLI accuracy on 14 non-English languages from 0.521 (English checkpoint) to 0.731. The multilingual checkpoint is usable (more than 3x random) in 48 of 51 languages. The typed-decisions fine-tune scores 0.766 on its 2,000-decision benchmark versus 0.362 for the base model.
    • Limits: Accuracy gets less predictable beyond about 4,000 tokens of context; the HTTP server caps choice questions at 100 options.
    • Deployment: Runs on CPU, Apple MPS or GPU; installs with pip and also ships a Docker Compose setup and a NixOS module.

    Takeaway: A cheap, self-hostable way to run classification, guardrail, moderation or alert-triage decisions at millisecond latency without paying for LLM generation. Treat its confidence scores as a ranking signal to calibrate on your own data, not proof an answer is correct.

  • Throw Away the Playbook: Security in the AI-Native SDLC
    summary

    TL;DR: Marco Lancini argues security teams should discard 20-plus years of secure-SDLC playbooks and write new ones for AI-native development, where agents do much of the work.

    • Starting point: Anthropic’s “AI-Native SDLC Playbook”, which Lancini, a director of security, calls mandatory reading for engineers and security staff.
    • Broken assumptions: old playbooks assumed writing code was the slow, expensive step and humans did every step; neither holds once agents do much of the work.
    • New model: the SDLC becomes a loop across six stages, an agent embedded at each, with handoffs carried by versioned intent.md, spec.md, plan.md, the diff and review findings.
    • Core distinction: skills are advisory, hooks are enforceable; a skill makes violations rare but guarantees nothing, so must-hold policies need a deterministic hook or a PR re-check pass.
    • Example skill: secure-api-review requires gateway JWT on every endpoint except health checks, OpenAPI body validation rejecting unknown fields, audit events on state changes, and no PII in logs or errors.
    • Hook placement: build-time hooks stay fast and scoped to the changed file; heavier checks such as the full test suite belong at commit or PR time.
    • His setup: blocks rm -rf, pushes to main and terraform apply, whatever a prompt says.
    • Agent review and on-call: agents run the same policy passes on every PR so humans judge only intent and risk; on-call agents get severity-tiered permissions: log only, diagnose read-only, or act via PR or runbook.
    • Limits: compliance is the main blocker, since many certifications require two-person peer review; he has not seen any organisation run this at scale with auditors, and ends asking practitioners where it held up or failed.

    Takeaway: Encode critical security rules as skills backed by hooks, and version specs, prompts and skill versions as an audit trail. Start agent first-response with the lowest-risk actions, and talk to auditors before replacing human review.

  • Test-Driving Jev on a Security Task: Identity Resolution
    summary

    TL;DR: Vincenzo Iozzo tests Jev, a model returning calibrated yes/no probabilities, on identity resolution across company systems, and finds it accurate, stable and far cheaper than frontier LLMs.

    • Problem: employees often have a dozen or more accounts across Google, Okta, Slack, GitHub, AWS IAM, Salesforce, Active Directory and B2B guest directories, many sharing no identifier.
    • Pipeline: group identical emails without a model, shortlist 20 candidates per account with weighted Jaro-Winkler similarity, let a model decide matches, then merge accepted links with union-find.
    • Comparison: prompted gpt-oss-20b baseline, Claude Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5, Haiku 4.5, Fellegi-Sunter (the method behind Splink) and gradient-boosted trees; graded by a blind Opus 5.5 judge checked against about 600 manually linked identities.
    • Small set: on about 630 manually linked accounts, Jev reproduced correct identities and fixed baseline wrong merges like rob.stone and rick.stone, at $0.16 and about 0.25 s per account versus about $22 and 3.5 s for Opus.
    • Large sets: on four environments of 30,000 to over 100,000 accounts, Jev hit 95% precision and 96% relative recall versus 82% and 71% for the baseline; F1 was 0.948.
    • Wins and exceptions: in a hybrid AD org it found pairs like “Doe, Jane” and JDOE@corp.example the baseline missed; in an org cluttered with duplicate B2B guest invitations the baseline had better recall.
    • Cost: per 1,000 accounts Jev cost $0.62 versus $11.67 for Haiku and $28.86 for Sonnet (19x and 47x more); Claude models scored slightly higher on the small set but over-linked, e.g. people sharing only a first name.
    • Classic linkage: nearly matched Jev on large regular directories at no cost, but linked msmith and msmith1 and two unrelated purchases@ mailboxes, and missed 74 links Jev found, only 3% sharing an exact identifier.
    • Hybrid and stability: a Sonnet second opinion only in Jev’s 0.2 to 0.5 “maybe” zone raised messy-org F1 from 0.650 to 0.860 at 4 to 7 times Jev’s cost; five runs gave Fleiss’ kappa about 0.98 to 0.99; caveats: the judge is itself an LLM, large environments were sampled, and only one prompt wording was tested.

    Takeaway: Treat a per-pair probability as a control: set a threshold, route uncertain pairs to humans or a pricier second-opinion model, and calibrate on a few hundred of your own labels (the article cites isotonic regression on about 500). Settle exact matches with free deterministic pre-passes before any model call.

  • gh-secure: GitHub CLI Extension to Enable Security Features Following GitHub Security Lab Best Practices
    summary

    TL;DR: gh-secure is a GitHub Security Lab CLI extension that enables recommended repository security features in one command, aimed mainly at public open source maintainers.

    • Pitch: “protect your project in 2 minutes”, claiming no security or coding expertise needed.
    • Branch protection: blocks force pushes and branch deletion; warns first if active rulesets exist, to avoid conflicts with legacy protection.
    • Reporting and secrets: private vulnerability reporting adds a security policy and private channel; secret scanning with push protection blocks commits with credentials across a large set of token types.
    • Dependencies and code: Dependabot flags vulnerable dependencies and opens update PRs; CodeQL code scanning checks code and pull requests for common vulnerabilities.
    • Usage: install with gh extension install GitHubSecurityLab/gh-secure; gh secure is interactive, gh secure --yes enables everything without prompts.
    • Options: pick features with bp, vr, ss, dep and cs, target another repo with --repo owner/repo, preview with --dry-run, and check state with gh secure status.
    • Requirements: an authenticated gh and admin or maintain permission, otherwise 403 errors; organisation repos may need the admin:org scope.
    • Limits: org policies can restrict branch protection, code scanning depends on supported languages and GitHub plan, branch protection may force PR-based workflows, and CodeQL findings do not block merges unless configured.
    • Extras: every setting can be reverted in repository settings, and the tool can be driven from Copilot CLI or other AI assistants.

    Takeaway: Use gh-secure to apply a fast baseline across side projects or many small repos, scripting audits with --dry-run and status. It raises the floor but, as its FAQ says, does not guarantee security.

  • herdr: The Runtime Your Coding Agents Live On
    summary

    TL;DR: herdr is an Apache-2.0 terminal runtime for coding agents, a single Rust binary that does tmux’s job while tracking which agent is waiting on you.

    • Scope: runs inside your existing terminal and manages panes, sessions and multiple machines rather than replacing the terminal emulator.
    • Persistence: a background server keeps terminals alive when the client closes or SSH drops; detach with ctrl+b q, reattach by running herdr again.
    • Restarts: after a server or machine restart it restores the saved layout and resumes supported agent sessions, but original processes do not survive.
    • Multi-machine: local work and saved SSH machines share one window, with a combined agent list and independent reconnects.
    • Status tracking: each pane is labelled working, blocked or idle, and herdr flags agents that stopped and need input.
    • Orchestration: agents control herdr via a CLI and socket API to spawn panes, prompt other agents and wait until another agent is actually blocked.
    • Agents and UI: runs Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, OpenCode, Grok and others unmodified, managing their terminals rather than wrapping them; tmux-style prefix keys, mouse actions and plugins.
    • Install: install script, Homebrew (brew install herdr), mise, prebuilt release binaries, beta Windows PowerShell installer, or cargo build --release; repo has a Nix flake and what appears to be a Ghostty-based terminal emulation crate.
    • Status: about 45.5k stars at capture, README referenced v0.4.0; Windows support is beta and supported platforms are not listed in detail.

    Takeaway: For running several coding agents locally or on remote dev boxes, herdr cuts tab-hopping and survives SSH drops. Prefer the package manager or a verified release binary over the curl-pipe-to-shell installer.

  • GitHub Actions SHA Pinning, Org-Wide
    summary

    TL;DR: Semgrep’s Leif Dreizler describes enforcing full-length commit SHA pinning for GitHub Actions across about 350 repositories, with tooling, numbers, breakages and a rollout plan.

    • Motivation: the early-2025 tj-actions/changed-files compromise, where attackers moved release tags to a malicious commit so every tag-referencing workflow ran attacker code.
    • Setting: GitHub’s “Require actions to be pinned to a full-length commit SHA” exists at repo and org level and blocks any run using an unpinned action.
    • What must go: direct tags like actions/cache@v4, branch refs like @main, and transitive unpinned actions called inside pinned ones, which caused most breakage.
    • Tooling: pinact converts tags to SHAs with a Renovate-readable # v4 comment; Renovate, chosen over Dependabot mainly because it also manages Helm charts, handles branch refs and updates.
    • Update policy: Semgrep-authored actions update immediately, third-party minor and patch wait a 7-day cooldown, majors arrive in separate PRs.
    • Automation: Semgrep Agentic Workflows detected and pinned in parallel, a Claude-built local dashboard tracked progress, a cron job enabled the setting on new repos every 15 minutes, and a monitor watched failures.
    • Numbers: about 80 PRs in roughly two days; about 100 of 350 repos needed changes, about 60 only direct tags (batch-fixed); roughly 80% of non-archived repos never ran an action; around 220 dead repos archived; a week and a half of monitoring caught two failures.
    • Edge cases: composite actions must be pinned, reusable workflows need not be but their actions must; fix transitive deps by upgrading, upstream PR or replacement, e.g. pre-commit/action swapped for plain uv and pre-commit; SLSA GitHub Generator cannot be SHA-referenced and was removed; internal @main actions broke without auto-updates.
    • Gotchas: Renovate needs an auto-approver action where approvals are required and platformAutomerge: false where not; repo-wide CODEOWNERS blocked bot PRs until relaxed for workflow files; GitHub has no aggregate failure view and no audit-only or exemption mode.

    Takeaway: Follow the ten-step rollout: announce with real error messages, auto-enrol new repos, enable first where no actions run, add pinning rules to AI assistant instructions, pick an update bot, pilot and monitor, expand gradually, announce again, then flip the org-wide switch.

  • open-code-review: Alibaba’s Hybrid Code Review Tool with Deterministic Pipelines and LLM Agent
    summary

    TL;DR: Open Code Review (OCR) is Alibaba’s Apache-2.0 AI code review CLI, combining deterministic file selection with a tool-using LLM agent for low-noise, line-positioned review.

    • Origin: open-sourced from an internal review assistant the README says served tens of thousands of Alibaba developers.
    • How it works: reads Git diffs, or whole files with ocr scan, and sends them to a configurable OpenAI- or Anthropic-compatible LLM via an agent that can read files, search the codebase and view other changed files.
    • Rules: structured, line-positioned comments from a built-in multi-language ruleset covering null pointer errors, thread safety, XSS and SQL injection; custom rules with path filters.
    • Deterministic layer: precise file selection so important changes are not skipped; bundles related files, such as English and Chinese message properties, into one unit for an isolated-context sub-agent; template-based rule matching plus positioning and reflection modules.
    • Agent layer: review-tuned prompts and a toolset derived from production tool-call traces; Alibaba says purely prompt-driven setups like Claude Code with skills suffer incomplete coverage, position drift and unstable quality.
    • Benchmark: on its own AACR-Bench, OCR claims higher precision and F1 than Claude Code on the same model, with about one-ninth of the tokens and faster runs; detailed figures appear only in a chart image.
    • Dataset: 50 repos, 200 real PRs, 10 languages and 1,505 annotated issues validated by more than 80 senior engineers, published on Hugging Face; recall is admittedly lower than general-purpose agents, a deliberate trade of fewer findings for less noise.
    • Usage: npm package @alibaba-group/open-code-review, Git 2.41 or later, Windows, macOS and Linux; ocr review, --from/--to, --commit, --resume and JSON output.
    • Integrations: delegation mode lets Claude Code, Codex or Cursor review with their own model while OCR handles selection and rules; session viewer, MCP, OpenTelemetry; GitHub Actions, GitLab CI, GitFlic CI and Gerrit.

    Takeaway: OCR is a self-hostable way to add consistent, low-noise LLM review to CI with your own model endpoint and custom security rules. The headline benchmark is the vendor’s own, so validate on your repositories first.

  • Hacking Google with AI for $500,000
    summary

    TL;DR: Arvin Shivram (brutecat) pointed an AI agent at Google’s server-side API surface for three months and reports over $500,000 in bug bounties from tireless testing of obvious flaws.

    • API keys: scraped from more than 60,000 Android APKs, decrypted iOS apps, and a Chrome Debugger API extension capturing traffic on more than 2,800 Google web domains, then filtered to Google-owned GCP projects.
    • Discovery docs: gathered for more than 1,500 APIs, revealing hidden methods by testing visibility labels such as GOOGLE_INTERNAL; domains came from extension logs, keyword-generated names and certificate transparency.
    • Auth bypass: first-party auth headers reverse-engineered from leaked frontend sourcemaps; key restrictions and origin allow-lists were satisfied by sending matching headers, including internal corp origins.
    • Pipeline map: Google’s request path modelled as about 10 checks (key validity, key restrictions, authentication, origin checks, visibility labels and so on), with each probe classified by where it failed.
    • MCP tools: API Explorer code became probe_api, get_endpoint_schema, report_vulnerability and confirm_testing_complete; a “Ralph Wiggum loop” stopped early quitting, and a system prompt refined over a month defined reportable.
    • Accuracy: every report cited operation IDs from real probes for one-click replay; better than 50% accuracy, limited by number of API keys, not the model; models are not named.
    • Findings: Google Voice backend missing authorization ($20,000), AdExchange account takeover ($30,000 total), Translation Hub cross-tenant read, write and GCS exfiltration ($36,500), Vertex AI Search for Commerce config read and write ($30,000), YouTube TV CMS campaign control ($24,000), Widevine console org enumeration and key decoding ($16,004.40); PLX/DataHub staging hosts exposed production data.
    • Cloud Console: 3,448 GraphQL schema pairs scraped, yielding unauthenticated App Engine request-log access ($18,000, CVE-2026-8934), cross-user Vertex Assistant chat access ($30,000) and Maps billing credits leaking customer emails ($12,000).
    • Conclusion: a standardised surface rewards relentless checks for missing IAM, unauthenticated resolvers, debug endpoints and staging tied to production; the AI’s job “wasn’t to be novel, it was to be tireless about the obvious.”

    Takeaway: Treat client-embedded keys, externally reachable schemas and discovery docs, and staging hosts as attack surface. Apply consistent authorization on every read, write and delete path, because AI agents will try all of them.

  • VoiceStudio: Open-Source, Fully Local ElevenLabs Alternative
    summary

    TL;DR: VoiceStudio is an AGPL-3.0, local-first speech app positioned as an open-source ElevenLabs alternative, covering cloning, dubbing, dictation and transcription on your own hardware.

    • Features: voice cloning, voice design from text, video dubbing with timed speech, dictation with a floating widget, transcription, and story and audiobook creation; claims 646 languages.

    • Engines: default built on k2-fsa’s OmniVoice, others selectable; a built-in model manager installs local speech models and shows licensing notices.
    • Automation: local API and MCP support for agents, plus reusable recipes, batch jobs and watch folders.

    • Project: about 82.5k stars and more than 4,500 commits at capture.
    • Licensing: AGPL covers only the app; model weights, tokenizers, voices and generated-output conditions are licensed separately, and you should clone voices only with permission.

Check each model’s licence before commercial use, and assume attackers have easy voice cloning when designing vishing and social-engineering defences.
