  • Build Your Own Vulnerability Harness
    summary

    TL;DR: Cloudflare built an agentic vulnerability-hunting pipeline across 128 repositories and argues the durable asset is the orchestration harness, not any single model, prompt or agent session.

    • Origin: a roughly 450-line security-audit skill with seven phases; single runs found about half the bugs multiple runs did, ran out of context, lost work on crashes and missed cross-repo links.
    • Scale-up: about six weeks to a fleet pipeline covering Rust, Go, C, Lua, TypeScript and Python with no per-language tuning; each agent stays under about 25% of the context window, and all state lives in one SQLite database, so a crash loses only the in-flight task.
    • Design: a Vulnerability Discovery Harness (VDH) and a Vulnerability Validation System (VVS), deliberately on different models so an unrelated set of weights challenges each finding.
    • VDH stages: Recon (parallel architecture doc and threat model), Hunt per attack class compiling and attacking code in an unshare-based sandbox, Validate, Gapfill for thin area-by-attack-class cells, Dedup, cross-repo Trace, Feedback rewriting queued prompts, and a model-free Report script.
    • Lessons: transient API errors can arrive inside a 200 OK body and need classifying; the sandbox needs seccomp=unconfined and apparmor=unconfined in Docker; a “wishlist” queue for missing tools was written 25,472 times; wired-in Semgrep was called zero times in a month as agents preferred reading and running code.
    • Junk control: hunters state a threat model, every finding needs a PoC test against unmodified code plus a patch, and validators never file their own findings.
    • VVS: dedup via inverted indexes plus an agent, production reachability judged with MCP, Jira, git and config; a Fixer adapts patches and requires a clean fail-to-pass test flip, never merging without human review.
    • Numbers: 20,799 raw candidates, about 12,057 survived validation, 5,442 duplicates folded, 7,245 actionable; validation rejection fell from 40% to 11% and high-integrity findings rose from 35% to 58% after better recon context.
    • Per repo: for a typical 30k-line repo, roughly 100 findings became about 80 distinct bugs, reached PRs in about 14 hours, with critical fixes shipped in about 5 days; the skill is released as cloudflare/security-audit-skill, full harness to follow.

    Takeaway: Start small with Recon, Hunt and Validate backed by a database and an independent validator, and demand runnable PoCs. Treat raw findings as cheap and invest in dedup, triage and verified fixes.

  • Zero Trust for AI Agents (Claude eBook)
    summary

    TL;DR: Anthropic’s 36-page eBook “Zero Trust for AI Agents” is a tiered framework for deploying autonomous agents built for breach, since frontier models shrink vulnerability-to-exploit time from months to hours.

    • Core test: written for CISOs (Parts I and II) and architects and engineers (Parts III to V), it asks of every control “does this make the attack impossible, or just tedious?”; friction-only controls like rate limits, extra pivot hops, non-standard ports and SMS MFA fail against unlimited patience, while hardware-bound credentials, expiring tokens, cryptographic identity and nonexistent network paths survive.
    • Grounding: NIST SP 800-207 and the NSA Zero Trust Implementation Guides, plus OWASP’s least agency: restricting what each tool can do, how often and where.
    • Threats: direct and indirect prompt injection, tool poisoning and rug pulls (citing the first malicious MCP server, which copied sent emails), tool-chaining exfiltration, loop amplification, unscoped privilege inheritance, in-memory credential caching, RAG and shared-context poisoning, and model supply chain risks like 250 poisoned documents backdooring 600M to 13B parameter models.
    • Control areas: Foundation, Enterprise and Advanced tiers across identity, service auth, permissions, privilege scoping, isolation, logging, traceability, baselining, anomaly detection, automated response, input/output filtering, config integrity, recovery and governance.
    • Foundation floor: cryptographically rooted per-agent identity, short-lived IdP tokens measured in minutes, identity-based isolation with segmentation only as backstop, and automated first-pass alert triage; static API keys are “treated as already-compromised” even with rotation.
    • Higher tiers: Enterprise adds X.509 and mTLS, ABAC, gVisor-style sandboxes, append-only logs, OpenTelemetry tracing and signed configs; Advanced adds HSM/TPM attestation, AMD SEV or Intel TDX, JIT/JEA access and human approval for high-risk actions.
    • Eight-phase workflow: requirements, supply chain (AI-BOM via OWASP CycloneDX, OpenSSF Scorecard, reachability analysis, self-hosting and signing MCP servers), agent boundaries, prompt injection defenses (Spotlighting cuts indirect injection from over 50% to under 2%, constitutional classifiers block 95% of jailbreaks), tool access, credentials, memory and metrics.
    • Claude Code pro-tips: deny-by-default permissions, sandboxing, managed settings, allowManagedPermissionRulesOnly, PreToolUse hooks, apiKeyHelper, OpenTelemetry and cleanupPeriodDays.
    • Defensive ops: automate bookkeeping not decisions; put a model at the front of the alert queue starting with one noisy rule measured against humans for two weeks; map coverage to MITRE ATT&CK with Atomic Red Team; tabletop five simultaneous incidents; pre-approve emergency change paths; instrument dwell time and alert coverage first, targeting rogue-agent detection within an hour.

    Takeaway: Kill static secrets, give each agent its own cryptographic identity and credentials, sandbox anything touching untrusted input, and sign configs with auto-applied signed updates. Hold defensive automation to the same zero trust scrutiny.

  • I Replaced My GitHub Runners with Lambda MicroVMs (and Maybe You Should Too)
    summary

    TL;DR: Luc van Donkersgoed replaced GitHub-hosted Actions runners with self-hosted runners on AWS Lambda MicroVMs, gaining speed and in-account access rather than meaningful cost savings.

    • Motivation: slow feedback loops on long build, deploy and test steps for The AWS News Feed and other projects.
    • Why not CodeBuild: long startup times, capacity issues and a design that does not behave like a true VM.
    • Snapshots: MicroVM images are built once out of band, snapshotted and stored, so a runner boots in seconds with a full OS that only registers with GitHub.
    • Architecture: API Gateway for GitHub webhooks, two Lambda functions, two SQS queues, SSM parameters for secrets and the MicroVM runners, published as the CDK reference project GitHub Runner Orchestrator.
    • Speed: pre-warming dependencies, container images and Docker layers into the snapshot saved several minutes per run; with apps in Ireland and GitHub runners in US datacenters, each roughly 200 ms round trip added up, and the end-to-end suite dropped from about 8 to about 5 minutes.
    • Cost: GitHub linux_2_core_arm is $0.005 per minute versus $0.0044 for a 2 vCPU, 4 GB MicroVM, plus about $1.50 per month snapshot storage and I/O; per-second AWS billing helps many short jobs.
    • Performance: raw CPU and memory were not noticeably better.
    • Limits: arm64 only (Graviton 3 and 4); low default quotas need an AWS Support request with no guaranteed approval (an established account was denied, a new one approved); you own patching, security baselines and incident response.
    • Unused benefits: per-VM isolation of disk, memory, CPU and network, VPC access to private databases and APIs, egress monitoring or forcing a private package registry, local caches, and a fallback when GitHub runners are degraded.

    Takeaway: Adopt MicroVM runners for latency to AWS-hosted test targets, private network access and egress control, not cost. Do it only if you accept the operational burden and quota risk.

  • clawk: Give Coding Agents a Disposable Linux VM, Not Your Laptop
    summary

    TL;DR: clawk is an Apache-2.0, pre-1.0 Go CLI that runs coding agents inside a disposable local Linux VM, making the hypervisor the boundary instead of a process-level policy.

    • Agents: Claude Code, Codex, pi, opencode or a plain shell, given root and no permission prompts inside the VM while host filesystem, keychain and other data stay invisible unless mounted or forwarded.
    • Startup: running clawk in a repo boots a sandbox and attaches an agent with no Dockerfile or devcontainer; any OCI image works as root filesystem without a Docker daemon.
    • Storage: repos and agent conversations live on the host and survive clawk destroy; the VM disk is a throwaway copy-on-write clone (APFS clonefile or FICLONE) of an ext4 image built from OCI layers.
    • Network: deny-by-default, per-sandbox outbound allow-list enforced in userspace by gvproxy; common registries and GitHub pre-allowed, denials logged by hostname.
    • Git: ssh-agent is forwarded so git push works without private keys in the VM; clawk work creates a git worktree per repo for multi-repo tickets, and clawk pr opens cross-linked pull requests.
    • Resources: idle VMs release memory to roughly 1 GiB, auto-stop after 30 idle minutes and can be snapshotted to disk.
    • Config: optional go.mod-style clawk.mod sets resources, image, network rules, forwards, env vars, MCP servers and hooks; runners start in bypass-permission mode unless --safe is passed.
    • Internals: a per-sandbox daemon owns the VM and talks to an in-guest PTY agent over vsock (no sshd or cloud-init), runs clawk-init as PID 1, mounts the repo over virtio-fs; Apple Virtualization.framework on macOS 14+ Apple silicon, experimental Firecracker on Linux.
    • Limits: anything mounted or allowed is exposed, writable worktrees can be committed to, pushes reach any repo the forwarded ssh-agent can, injected secrets are agent-readable, and hypervisor escapes are out of scope.

    Takeaway: Use clawk to run autonomous agents with stronger isolation than containers or OS-level sandboxes. Still scope the ssh-agent, egress list and mounted secrets tightly.

  • LifeOS: Universal AI Harness for Moving from Current to Ideal State in Life and Work
    summary

    TL;DR: LifeOS (formerly PAI) is Daniel Miessler’s MIT-licensed “universal AI harness” that wraps a coding assistant with persistent personal context to move you from “Current State” to “Ideal State”.

    • Pitch: Aims for “Euphoric Surprise”; argues a bare harness lacks persistent memory, custom skills, stored personal context, intelligent routing and self-improvement.
    • Core components: TELOS (personal goals and context layer), The Algorithm (seven phases: Observe, Think, Plan, Build, Execute, Verify, Learn), an Ideal State Artifact system.
    • More components: Cortex (memory), Synapse (input routing), Atlas (asset graph), Ledger (change tracking), skill and hook systems, voice, learning, security, optional Hermes terminal front door.
    • Pulse: A daemon and dashboard that release notes place on port 31337.
    • Stack: TypeScript, Bash and Bun; claims to be harness-agnostic, but the primary tested path is Claude Code.
    • Install: Hand an install prompt to an AI coding harness, which reads the install page and asks permission before changes, or use a one-line shell installer for Claude Code on macOS and Linux.
    • Versions: Since v6.0.0 it ships as one self-contained skill bundling research, security, writing and art; v7.0.0 cut context footprint about two-thirds, from roughly 88KB to 28KB.
    • Repo: About 19.6k stars and 2.5k forks; generated from a private source tree, so community PRs are ported in with credit, not merged.
    • Roadmap and upgrades: Local models via Ollama and llama.cpp, per-task model routing, remote access, outbound calling, notifications to email, Discord, Telegram and Slack; back up ~/.claude before upgrades, USER/ is preserved.

    Takeaway: Use it as a reference for composing hooks, skills, context files and routing into a personal agent system. Installing it means an agent modifies your ~/.claude config and runs a local daemon, so review its changes before trusting it with sensitive context.

  • Harnessing Harnesses: Climbing the LLM Hills
    summary

    TL;DR: Andy Gill (ZephrFish) argues the harness around the model, not prompts or model choice, drives the biggest gains in capability, cost and reliability for LLM offensive security work.

    • Definition: A harness controls inputs, tools, prompts, models, state, validation gates and outputs per stage; MCP servers are just one kind of tool inside it.
    • Without one: Parallel agents act like “a room full of drunk toddlers”, burning tokens on redundant context and producing unverifiable output.
    • RAPTOR: Splits a Python execution layer (CI-friendly, SARIF output) from a Claude Code decision layer; six-stage validation incl. ASLR/RELRO checks and Z3 solving for one-gadget feasibility.
    • Other harnesses: Anthropic’s defending-code reference harness finds, grades and patches C/C++ bugs in ASAN-instrumented Docker with a reproducing binary PoC; baby-naptime opens Google Project Zero’s unreleased Naptime loop.
    • More harnesses: evilsocket’s eight-stage audit requires tracing attacker-controlled input to a sink; Visa’s VVAH stresses threat modelling and taint flow but uses an LLM-seeded, regex-reinforced call graph, not an AST.
    • Caveat: Even execution-verified harnesses occasionally produce rubbish, and audit needed substantial tweaking.
    • Design advice: Distinct prompt per stage; verifiers look for reasons a finding is wrong; filter cheaply first, e.g. Scrutineer’s revalidate skill checks findings against git history before expensive verification.
    • Context budget: About 8K tokens for single-function analysis, around 32K for multi-finding synthesis, scanner or fuzzer output cut to a few hundred useful tokens; strong models validate, trace and synthesise, cheap ones classify and summarise.
    • Memory and tooling: RAG knowledge base of notes, docs and prior findings plus a post-run feedback loop; his setup layers Claude Code expert and worker skills over eight MCP servers; released harness-kit (recon, hunt, validate, trace, report with structured artefacts) and TokenBurn (maps Claude Max usage to API-equivalent spend).

    Takeaway: Design staged pipelines with scoped context, explicit validation gates and persistent state from day one, because bolting on context management later is painful.

  • The Two Mitigations for the Service-Account Confused Deputy in the Cloud
    summary

    TL;DR: Kat Traxler frames the cloud service-account confused deputy problem by who owns the non-human identity, arguing the right mitigation follows that ownership.

    • Definition: A confused deputy is a privileged intermediary tricked into acting for a caller who lacks that authority.
    • Customer-managed identities: GCP service accounts, AWS IAM roles, Azure user-assigned managed identities; risk is a lower-privileged principal attaching a powerful identity to compute it controls.
    • Bind-time checks: iam.serviceAccounts.actAs (GCP), iam:PassRole (AWS), userAssignedIdentities/*/assign/action (Azure), scoped to the target identity.
    • Enforcement gap: These are not standalone APIs; the attaching service enforces them (e.g. ec2:RunInstances, lambda:CreateFunction), and they are not re-evaluated at runtime once workloads pull metadata-endpoint tokens.
    • GCP split: Attach (actAs, the only true confused-deputy vector), delegate (setIamPolicy on the service account), mint (getAccessToken via Service Account Token Creator, which Service Account User does not grant).
    • Provider-managed identities: GCP service agents, AWS service-linked roles, Azure platform service principals act in your tenant with standing trust; the guard is the provider’s internal check on the original caller.
    • AWS: Documented Forward Access Sessions carry the original principal downstream (a KMS call succeeds only if the caller already has key permission), exposed via aws:ViaAWSService and aws:CalledVia.
    • AWS customer levers: aws:SourceArn, aws:SourceAccount, aws:SourceOrgID and aws:SourceOrgPaths restrict which source a service principal can act for.
    • GCP and Azure: Checks are undocumented, visible only as PERMISSION_DENIED (GCP) or LinkedAuthorizationFailed (Azure, e.g. needing subnets/join/action for a subnet you do not control); no customer lever on provider-managed paths.

    Takeaway: Audit and tightly scope iam:PassRole, actAs and managed identity assign permissions as privilege-escalation paths. On AWS, add source account or org conditions to resource policies that trust service principals.

  • OIDC Tokens Can Now Restrict Which AWS Roles They Assume
    summary

    TL;DR: Aidan Steele documents sts:RoleAuthorizedByIdp, a barely documented AWS condition key letting an OIDC IdP restrict which roles a token may assume via sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity.

    • Mechanism: The IdP adds a https://aws.amazon.com/roles claim (namespaced like the tag and source identity claims) with one role ARN or an array of ARNs.
    • Matching: When present, the requested role ARN must exactly match an entry; no role names or wildcards; otherwise STS returns InvalidIdentityToken saying the role is not in the roles claim.
    • Evaluation order: Tested with a home-grown IdP, the check runs before trust policy evaluation; a token bound to role X failed for role Y even though Y’s trust policy allowed the issuer, audience and subject.
    • Effect: The claim is an allow-list enforced by STS itself, not optional context.
    • Value: Defense in depth: if a dev deploy role and prod admin role share an overly broad trust policy, a compromised deployment system still cannot use its token against prod.
    • Other uses: Cleaner than packing role limits into long sub strings; suits SaaS vendors (CI/CD platforms, scanners) issuing tokens into many accounts; gives the IdP an audit record of the intended role.
    • Enforcement: Add a Bool condition requiring sts:RoleAuthorizedByIdp = "true" in the trust policy, or better, an org-wide RCP denying sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity when aws:FederatedProvider matches and the key is false.
    • Logging: CloudTrail records "RoleAuthorizedByIdp": true in additionalEventData on success, but omits the field entirely when the claim is absent instead of logging false.
    • Limits: Works only if the IdP emits the claim; the author believes essentially no vendors do yet, and speculates AWS may be working with GitHub, GitLab, Buildkite and GCP.

    Takeaway: Watch for IdP support and plan an RCP to enforce it for CI/CD federation once available. Meanwhile keep tight trust policies with unique sub and aud conditions.

  • SUPRA: Sovereign EU Orchestration Platform
    summary

    (Could not fetch full text.)

    TL;DR: SUPRA, published under the name Cloudowski, is an early-stage sovereign EU orchestration platform running app and AI workloads across multiple EU clouds and on-prem as one governed system.

    • Source caveat: The site is a JavaScript single-page app and was only partially readable; this summary uses strings extracted from the page and its bundle.
    • Positioning: Outside the reach of the US CLOUD Act and FISA 702; explicitly not a cloud, not a hyperscaler, not a wrapper over one provider’s APIs; owns no infrastructure.
    • Role: A control plane deciding where workloads run, how they fail over and who holds the keys.
    • Thesis: Europe’s cloud market is fragmented into dozens of regional and national providers with none at hyperscaler scale; orchestration, not consolidation, turns that into an advantage, since building an EU AWS, Azure or GCP rival soon is unrealistic.
    • Federated model: An independent EU hyperscaler alternative, a second independent EU provider for geographic and operational diversity, and your private infrastructure under one policy plane with live failover; for AI, orchestrates from GPU capacity to apps, referencing IPCEI-CIS federated GPU capacity.
    • Compliance: Targets Cloud and AI Development Act (CADA) assurance levels L3 to L4 (EU-owned and controlled, full software supply chain control); maps to the IPCEI-CIS reference architecture with an EUCS, NIS2 and GDPR baseline.
    • Stack: GitLab OIDC, Kubernetes RBAC, Cilium mutual auth and NetworkPolicy, mTLS, cert-manager, Kyverno, Falco eBPF, Rook/Ceph encryption, Vault KMS and dynamic secrets, External Secrets, Syft, Grype, Dependency-Track, Cosign, GitLab SAST, OpenSearch SIEM via Falco Sidekick and Kafka, ArgoCD multi-region, CloudNativePG HA.
    • Supply chain: Images are scanned, get a CRA-aligned SBOM and are signed before shipping; post-quantum cryptography is on the roadmap.
    • Limits: Tested only at small scale; will not match hyperscaler breadth but targets the essential services it says over 80% of organizations use; requires containerized apps since Kubernetes is core; seeking financing partners and early adopters.

    Takeaway: Treat SUPRA as a reference blueprint for a sovereign, policy-driven Kubernetes platform built from mainstream CNCF tooling, not as a product ready to adopt.

  • Comparing Open-Source AI Code Security Harnesses
    summary

    TL;DR: Semgrep’s Isaac Evans surveys open-source AI code security harnesses and finds no reference harness yet, so companies will likely build their own “shop jigs” as the field moves fast.

    • Method: Qualitative only; no benchmarks, precision, recall or dollar costs; compares categories, feature tables and stated design choices. Three groups: LLM-led exploit generation, LLM skill-boosting, SAST plus LLM hybrids.
    • Exploitgen: defending-code-harness (Anthropic’s now-unmaintained original, Semgrep-maintained fork) counts a finding only if it crashes under AddressSanitizer and reproduces 3 of 3 times in isolated gVisor; grades patches T0 to T3. Hardest group to use, since guardrails require trusted cyber access.
    • Skill-boosting: Cloudflare’s security-audit-skill (recon, 8 to 12+ parallel hunters, adversarial validation, schema-checked JSON); Trail of Bits’ skills (about 40 plugins, SARIF); Capital One’s vulnhunter (find, fix, verify for Claude Code); Google’s mantis (about 15 skills, a starting point).
    • Hybrids, part 1: raptor (about 107K lines of Python: Semgrep, CodeQL, fuzzing, SCA, exploit feasibility, patching; isolated with Landlock, seccomp, namespaces); Vercel’s deepsec (198-matcher regex prefilter, AI investigation, revalidation cutting false positives about 50%, defaults to GPT-5.5).
    • Hybrids, part 2: Cisco’s ai-deep-sast (LLM triage of Semgrep findings across 30+ languages, fully local with Foundation-Sec-8B); Visa’s VVAH (11 stages, STRIDE across 42 languages, Sonnet 4.6 detects, Opus 4.8 validates, closed to contributions).
    • Common themes: Separate discovery from validation, often with different models; adversarial validation where a second agent tries to falsify each finding.
    • Differences: “Finding” ranges from a triaged static match to a reproducible ASAN crash; only raptor, VVAH and defending-code-harness patch; only raptor and defending-code-harness execute code and produce PoCs. Cost: standalone pipelines (infra plus tokens) vs agent-native skills (tokens only).
    • Recommendations: ai-deep-sast offline; raptor for hunters accepting overhead; defending-code-harness for C/C++ maintainers wanting verified bugs; VVAH for AppSec optimising time to reviewed fix; deepsec for Next.js and Vercel shops; Cloudflare, Trail of Bits or Capital One skills for Claude Code or Codex teams.
    • Bias: Partly promotional ahead of a Semgrep Black Hat announcement.

    Takeaway: Use the post as a map for where to start, and first decide whether you need runnable proof of exploitability or fast, broad triage.

  • CISO’s Guide to Agentic AI
    summary

    TL;DR: Anthropic Deputy CISO Jason Clinton argues security’s job with agentic AI is making risk visible and bounded, not zero, so the organization can accept what it can manage.

    • Why not refuse: Refusal drives shadow adoption with no telemetry or off switch; approval without controls produces incidents that can set an AI program back.
    • Top risks: Internal data leakage via personal agents stitching systems together with weak oversight is most likely; then prompt injection, with falling but non-zero success rates.
    • Four questions: What untrusted content does it ingest? What actions can it take, under whose identity? What is the blast radius (scope times severity)? Are its actions observable and in the SIEM?
    • Least agency: Grant the narrowest capability that completes the task; roll out admin-paced to a small group first. A drifting agent is an insider threat; Ponemon’s 2026 data puts average insider containment at 67 days, far too slow at agent speed.
    • Identity spectrum: From single-purpose service accounts to human credentials; the ambiguous middle, where an agent carries a person’s delegated identity into systems they are not watching, makes incidents unexplainable.
    • Case study: A read-only incident-response agent, after an upgrade from Claude Opus 4 to Opus 4.5 with no other changes, found a root cause and asked another agent over Slack to write a fix, still gated by human PR review. Scope for what the next model can do.
    • Seven controls (Claude Cowork): IdP identity via SAML/OIDC and SCIM, connector allowlists, per-tool and per-verb approval, sandboxes without stealable credentials (reverse proxy injects tokens), mandatory egress allowlist, OpenTelemetry export of every tool call to the SIEM, org-wide kill switch.
    • Strongest control: Egress allowlisting against prompt injection, because a hijacked agent has nowhere to send data.
    • At Anthropic: Over 50% of PR code is written by an internal agent in ephemeral VMs away from production keys; GRC uses agents for questionnaires and vendor reviews under ISO 42001, with humans owning risk acceptance.

    Takeaway: Design controls for where models will be in about six months, not fragile prompt scaffolds. Run your most pressured use case through the four questions, ask IdP, SIEM and agent vendors which of the seven controls they can demonstrate today, and write down what counts as untrusted content.

  • Introducing the Amazon GuardDuty Investigation Agent: On-Demand AI-Powered Threat Assessment
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS launched the Amazon GuardDuty investigation agent, an on-demand AI feature that investigates GuardDuty findings and, per AWS, cuts investigation time from hours to minutes.

    • Status: Public preview, tuned for GuardDuty findings, separate from AWS Security Agent and AWS DevOps Agent.
    • Scope: One finding ID, one AWS account, or all org accounts; optional free-form prompt up to 2,048 characters to steer toward suspected causes or priorities.
    • Runtime: Asynchronous; create, then poll until COMPLETED or FAILED. In preview, account-level runs take about 2 to 5 minutes, single-finding runs about 10 to 12 minutes.
    • Output: Correlates multiple data sources across services (not listed); returns risk level Info to Critical, confidence, summary, key observations, details, recommended actions (may include AWS CLI commands), MITRE ATT&CK mapping, affected resources.
    • Access: Console, CLI, SDKs, three new APIs and IAM actions (CreateInvestigation, GetInvestigation, ListInvestigations), and the AWS MCP server so clients like Kiro or Claude trigger it in natural language.
    • Automation: Route findings via EventBridge to a Lambda that calls CreateInvestigation and forwards enriched results to a SIEM or ticketing system.
    • Permissions: Only administrator accounts create investigations; member accounts can only read and list their own.
    • Limits: Free in preview, capped at 10 investigations per account per day and 100 total per account (failed runs not counted); 10 Regions: Frankfurt, Ireland, London, Paris, Stockholm, N. Virginia, Ohio, Oregon, Canada Central, Tokyo.
    • Data residency: Cross-Region Inference stores data in the originating Region but may process it elsewhere in the same geography. Positioned as triage before AWS Security Incident Response, which has its own investigation agent.

    Takeaway: Wire high-severity findings into EventBridge-driven auto-investigation and grant the three IAM actions narrowly. Check CRIS residency implications, and treat output as analyst assistance to review, not a verdict.

    summary

  • How Cloudflare Enforces Engineering Standards Using AI
    summary

    TL;DR: Cloudflare describes the Cloudflare Codex, a governed set of engineering standards that AI agents retrieve and apply during development, review and incident follow-up.

    • Problem: Guidance was scattered across docs, repos, chat and people’s heads; engineers could not tell what was current or authoritative, reviewers could not check every requirement, and drift built up between projects.
    • Structure: Domains such as architecture, security, reliability, TypeScript and Rust, each with an owner. Standards are RFCs using RFC 2119 MUST and SHOULD keywords plus front-matter metadata.
    • Process: Anyone with domain competence proposes an RFC via merge request; it goes through review rounds and the domain owner approves it before publishing to an internal Astro site.
    • Staged enforcement: An approved RFC only produces non-blocking findings. MUST violations block merges only after the RFC is explicitly promoted to “enforced,” giving teams time to adapt.
    • Context handling: 60+ RFCs would overwhelm a model’s context window, so an agent extracts each MUST and SHOULD into structured JSON with a stable slug that survives RFC edits, enabling lazy loading and long-term tracking.
    • AI code reviewer: Checks merge requests for Codex compliance and pulls full RFC text only when needed. Since launch it flagged close to 230,000 violations; almost 16,000 withheld approval for breaking MUST statements on enforced RFCs.
    • Linters and CLI: Mechanically checkable rules go into linters (oxlint for TypeScript first, Rust in progress, Go planned). A local CLI runs the same OpenCode-based agents on your diff to skip the CI round trip.
    • Spec and incident reviewers: The spec reviewer (Cloudflare Worker, D1 state, AI Gateway, Cron Trigger) reviewed nearly 600 design specs across over 3,200 invocations since May 2026; findings 65% major, 29% minor, 6% critical. The incident report reviewer assessed over 200 postmortems, catching missing action items, incomplete timelines and omitted detection signals; it is now mandatory for high-severity incidents in central review.
    • Next: Cover the whole SDLC, let agents propose fixes with engineers still approving, and extend the Codex to product, security, compliance and trust and safety.

    Takeaway: Write standards as machine-parseable RFCs with stable IDs and deliver them at the point of work through AI review. Push anything deterministic into linters, start advisory, block only after explicit promotion, and keep humans owning final approval.

  • ADR: Securing Enterprise AI Agents Through Observability, Security Benchmarking, and Threat Detection
    summary

    TL;DR: ADR (Agentic AI Detection and Response) is Uber’s open-source security system for AI agents, deployed in production at Uber and backed by an MLSys 2026 paper (Li et al., arXiv 2605.17380).

    • Targets: Employee coding agents (Cursor, Claude Code, Codex, GitHub Copilot CLI, DeepSeek Harness) and customer-facing agents such as AI support bots.
    • Five capabilities: Discovery, observability, benchmarking, detection, prevention.
    • Discovery: Inventories AI apps, CLI agents, IDE extensions, local model runtimes and MCP servers on endpoints via probes and a fingerprint catalog; flags unknown surfaces for review.
    • Observability: A Sensor collects agent intent, tool calls and execution traces into a unified schema across 7+ coding tools on macOS, Linux and Windows, incl. opencode, Claude Desktop and Gemini CLI journals with nested subagent sessions.
    • ADR-Bench: 304 tasks, 134 MCP servers and all 17 agent attack techniques for measuring how well detectors catch malicious agent behavior.
    • Detection: Two tiers: high-recall triage flags sessions, then deeper agentic reasoning investigates suspicious ones.
    • Not open-sourced: Prevention (blocking unsafe actions) and the offline ADR Explorer engine for pre-deployment red teaming.
    • Running it: Clone, run uv sync in the Detection directory, set Anthropic and OpenAI API keys, use the default adr detector or llamafirewall for a keyless smoke test; full evaluation inflates the packed benchmark, runs detectors and plots figures per REPRODUCIBILITY.md.
    • Fixtures and license: Detection holds synthetic fixtures (fake credentials, prompt-injection scenarios) for defensive research only; code is Apache 2.0, vendored AgentDojo is MIT.

    Takeaway: Treat coding agents like an EDR problem: inventory agents and MCP servers on laptops, collect normalized traces, layer cheap triage with expensive LLM investigation, and benchmark the detector before trusting it.

  • Pangolin: Networking and Security Platform for Secure Access to Apps, Infrastructure, and AI Workloads
    summary

    TL;DR: Pangolin is an open-source WireGuard-based SASE platform bundling zero-trust VPN, zero-trust reverse proxy, privileged access management and an identity-aware AI gateway under one identity and policy model.

    • Core idea: Lightweight user-space connectors open outbound tunnels, so networks behind firewalls or NAT are reachable without public IPs or open inbound ports.
    • Reverse proxy: Exposes HTTPS apps and gives clientless browser access to VNC, RDP and SSH; protected by SSO, PIN codes, passcodes, email one-time codes, geoblocking and allow-lists.
    • Private access: Client mode with peer-to-peer connections to specific hosts, IPs, port ranges or CIDR networks, DNS aliases, PAM for SSH, and routing through multiple connectors for redundancy.
    • AI gateway: One Pangolin URL in front of OpenAI, Anthropic, Gemini and self-hosted Ollama, vLLM or Mistral; keyless access via desktop client or personal API keys; budgets, session history, usage analytics.
    • Identity: Built-in or external IdP with user and role sync; RBAC, per-resource grants, full audit logs; a Resource Launcher home page per user; alerts when a device or resource goes down.
    • Deploy: Managed Pangolin Cloud, or self-host via quick install guide or DigitalOcean Marketplace image; clients for macOS, Windows, Linux, iOS and Android.
    • Stack: Appears to be Next.js and TypeScript with Drizzle ORM on PostgreSQL or SQLite, shipped with Docker Compose files.
    • Licensing: Community Edition AGPL-3; Enterprise Edition under the Fossorial Commercial License, free for personal use and businesses under 100,000 USD annual revenue. Around 23,000 GitHub stars.

    Takeaway: Consider it as a self-hostable alternative to Cloudflare Tunnel or Tailscale-style setups that replaces port forwarding and bastion hosts with identity-gated outbound tunnels. It can also be a central, audited choke point for LLM API access.

  • Threat Hunt AI: How We Built an AI Security Analyst on AWS for Under $500/Month
    summary

    TL;DR: Pablo Vidal describes Grow Therapy’s Claude-based AI threat hunting on AWS, running daily hunts across many SaaS and cloud log sources for roughly 320 to 510 USD per month.

    • Sources: AWS, Okta, Google Workspace, GitHub, CrowdStrike, Slack, Stripe and others; 15 log sources in Snowflake plus about 14 days of existing Datadog logs, so no new ingestion pipeline.
    • Deterministic layer: SQL detections as Snowflake tasks every 15 minutes, defined in YAML (query, lookback, dedup rules, severity); they still generate most alert volume.
    • Hunt runtime: Docker container on ECS Fargate (1 vCPU, 2 GB RAM), launched by an EventBridge cron and a small Lambda; runs 15 to 45 minutes, writes findings to Snowflake, exits.
    • Plumbing: One ALERTS table; Lambdas poll its change stream every 5 minutes, handle Slack buttons and Jira webhooks, process SQS work; DynamoDB for short-term state, NAT Gateway static IP allowlisted by Snowflake, Secrets Manager, all in Terraform.
    • Hunt definition: YAML with objective, severity guidelines, known false positives, allowed-tool flags and schedule; conventions in an AGENTS.md; about 20 hunts in production.
    • Phased prompting: Single-shot was vague; Claude Sonnet gathers data (up to 100 tool calls), then Claude Opus with extended thinking does 1-day and 7-day baselines, enrichment and confidence scoring.
    • Adversarial validation: The model argues against each finding and seeks counter-evidence (corporate VPNs, known automation accounts), dropping findings only on specific evidence; before it, about 40% of findings were noise.
    • Feedback and dedup: Analyst FP marks flow Slack, Jira, Snowflake; patterns with 3+ occurrences and 80%+ FP rate over 90 days go into the prompt. Duplicates use a SHA-256 fingerprint and 48-hour window confirmed by a tiny Sonnet call.
    • Numbers: Monthly 250 to 400 USD Claude API, 60 to 90 Snowflake, 10 to 20 Fargate, 5 to 10 Lambda, DynamoDB and NAT; phase 1 on Sonnet alone cut costs about 60%; first CloudTrail hunt went from 15+ findings per run to 2 to 3 after tuning.

    Takeaway: Centralize queryable logs first, reuse existing observability spend, and build the analyst feedback loop and per-hunt cost tracking from day one. Tune new hunts in a no-alert staging table, and start with few tools, since large tool sets bloat context and degrade signal.

  • Putting Models to the Secure Coding Test: Plan vs Default Mode
    summary

    TL;DR: Datadog Security Labs’ Kennedy Toomey tested whether AI coding agents in plan mode write more secure code than default mode; they do not, and every build had an IDOR.

    • Setup: One prompt for a JavaScript document portal: JWT and session cookies, PostgreSQL, multiple roles, password reset, search, file upload, comments, profile editing, GitHub Pages deploy workflow; no document-level access rules specified.
    • Models: Sonnet 5 in Claude, Composer 2.5 in Cursor, GPT 5.5 in Codex, each in both modes (six builds) from empty folders, installing via Datadog’s open-source Supply Chain Firewall (SCFW).
    • Scanning: Datadog Code Security (SCA, SAST, IaC, code quality) plus an audit with Claude’s code_review skill.
    • Headline: All six builds had an IDOR letting any logged-in user read, download or comment on others’ documents; plan mode did not prevent it.
    • Default mode: Sonnet 5 had the most resilient backend (CSRF protection, rate limiting); GPT 5.5 was weakest, returning password-reset tokens in JSON and weakening cookie flags whenever NODE_ENV was not production.
    • Plan mode: Sonnet 5 had the most mature architecture (Prisma, Zod, rotating refresh tokens, a hardening pass) but inconsistent CSRF and trusted JWT claims without DB checks; Composer 2.5 was worst overall, with hardcoded JWT secret dev-secret-change-me when unset and email changes without password re-verification.
    • Dependencies: SCFW blocked multer 1.4.5-lts.2 (eight high-severity issues), vite 5.4.21, esbuild 0.21.5 and an old nodemailer, which agents then upgraded; none used dependency cooldowns, all used caret ranges.
    • Numbers: All builds used parameterized queries; SAST counts ranged from 0 (Sonnet 5 plan) to 29 (Composer 2.5 default).
    • Conclusion: Mode had no meaningful correlation with security; plan mode fixed some issues and introduced others; the prompt mattered more because models did not infer authorization rules.

    Takeaway: State security requirements explicitly, centralize authorization in shared middleware, fail at startup on missing or placeholder secrets, avoid environment-dependent debug behavior, and apply CSRF and rate limiting consistently. Gate dependency installs with a firewall and pinned versions, and always run independent SAST, SCA and review on agent-generated code.

  • ai-job-search: AI Job Application Framework Built on Claude Code
    summary

    TL;DR: AI Job Search is an MIT-licensed, open-source job application framework built on Claude Code: fork it, fill in your profile, and drive it through slash commands.

    • Origin: Built by a geophysicist who lost a position in late 2025, during his own search; he self-reports 69 tailored applications, 20 first interviews and one signed AI engineer contract.

    Takeaway: Study it as a well-structured multi-step Claude Code workflow with reviewer agents, deterministic verification and injection screening, and keep personal data out of public forks.

  • kern: Rootless, Daemonless Container Runtime for Agent Tool Calls and LLM-Generated Code
    summary

    TL;DR: kern is a rootless, daemonless Rust container runtime shipped as one static binary that doubles as a sandbox for LLM- or agent-generated code.

    • Build: Single static binary, libc its only Rust dependency; Apache-2.0, maintained by Alessandro Polito, relatively young with a few hundred stars.
    • Boxes: Containers use OCI images and support pull, build, commit, push, save and load; the README claims single-digit-millisecond starts.
    • Isolation: Six Linux namespaces, pivot_root, read-only or overlay root, deny-by-default /dev, cgroup v2 limits, an always-on seccomp allowlist keeping 35 escape-related syscalls denied, dangerous capabilities dropped before exec.
    • Defaults: No file access unless passed in, no network unless enabled; --security-profile untrusted combines the seccomp allowlist, dropping all capabilities and a read-only root.
    • Stacks: Runs existing docker-compose.yml or its own stack.toml, each service in its own network namespace with name-based discovery; kern run applies the same caps to a plain host process.
    • Ops commands: ps, logs, exec, stats, inspect, top and doctor, which checks the host and reports what to fix.
    • Numbers: Zero memory at rest (no daemon or socket) vs 154 to 160 MB for Docker; self-measured on x86 in September 2026: 80x faster than docker run for one container, 130x for 200 concurrent, 3.6x faster than rootless runc; it encourages measuring yourself.
    • Security evidence: Ran 25 published container-runtime escapes against it; ships a pentest directory, threat model, CVE posture and egress docs; SECURITY.md marks which boundaries are cooperative rather than kernel-enforced.
    • Platforms and SDKs: Native on Linux x86_64 and aarch64; Ubuntu 23.10+ needs a one-time root step; Windows and macOS via WSL2 or a Colima VM; Python and Node kern-sandbox packages return timeouts and OOM as typed faults; MCP server for Claude Code, Cursor, Claude Desktop and LM Studio.

    Takeaway: Evaluate it as a lightweight, root-free, daemon-free sandbox for untrusted agent code, but treat benchmarks as vendor-reported and review the documented cooperative boundaries before relying on it.

  • Replace PRs with Delta: Now in Public Beta
    summary

    TL;DR: Zed Industries opened the public beta of Delta on September 16, 2026, a multiplayer environment for coding with AI agents that replaces pull requests with shared agent threads.

    • Dogfooding: Zed disabled pull requests on Delta’s own repository and builds Delta entirely inside Delta, using it daily.
    • Collaboration: Instead of commit-and-push, you invite teammates into agent conversations; they see the same worktrees from their own machines, ask the agent about past decisions and continue threads.
    • Review: A review subthread walks the branch’s changes with full access to the original agent’s context, so reviewers need not reconstruct the author’s reasoning.
    • Isolation: Each review gets an isolated copy of the parent thread’s worktrees, so reviewers can experiment, request revisions, or fix issues with their own agent and merge fixes back before landing.
    • DeltaDB: Extends Git’s content-addressed model with incremental, delta-based versions recording edits between commits plus human and agent messages; commits remain the push/pull/build checkpoints, so non-Delta users see a normal Git repo.
    • GitHub: The main zed-industries/zed repo stays on GitHub, where contributors are encouraged to attach Delta threads to pull requests.
    • Framing: “Continuous engineering”: threads, not commits and diffs, as the unit of software development.
    • Limits: Git storage inside DeltaDB is in progress; content-based builds bringing CI-style verification into threads are longer-term; other GitHub workflows are not replaced; for now an agent can trigger runs in an existing CI provider and check results before landing.
    • Availability and numbers: Desktop apps on macOS, Linux and Windows, browser, and mobile browsers for following threads; thousands of early-access requests; 570 changes landed to main by 33 employees since PRs were turned off; free in beta, paid individual and team plans planned, free tier promised.

    Takeaway: Review and provenance move into agent threads, so work out how branch protection, required reviews, CI gates and audit trails map onto a workflow that no longer centers on pull requests.

    summary

    TL;DR: Video2X is an open-source, machine-learning framework for video super resolution (upscaling) and frame interpolation that runs on any Vulkan-capable GPU.

  • What Happened After 2,000 People Tried to Hack My AI Assistant
    summary

    TL;DR: Fernando Irarrázaval’s hackmyclaw challenge let anyone email his OpenClaw-based assistant Fiu to leak a secrets.env file; 6,000+ emails produced zero leaks, but plenty of operational lessons.

    • Setup: Fiu ran on Claude Opus 4.6 on a VPS with email, calendar, files and web access; told not to reply to emails but technically able to send them; the challenge took off after hitting the Hacker News front page.
    • Defense: Short rule set: never reveal secrets, modify its own config, execute commands from emails, or send data to external endpoints because of email content.
    • Numbers: More than 6,000 emails from more than 2,000 unique senders; zero successful secret extractions and zero unauthorized replies.
    • Attacks: Fake emergencies and compliance audits with 24-hour deadlines, an “OpenClaw Admin” persona, “this is you from the future” messages, riddles, French, Spanish and Italian; one person sent 20 variations in four minutes; Anthropic’s refusal test string broke the processing pipeline.
    • Context poisoning: Early obvious attacks made the agent suspicious of every later email, so each message moved to a fresh context.
    • Memory drift: Around email 500, Fiu noted in memory that the volume looked like a coordinated security exercise, forcing a memory wipe and re-run of earlier messages.
    • Ops costs: Google suspended the Gmail account for three days over fraud signals; API costs passed $500; the bounty grew from $100 to $1,000 via sponsors Corgea, Abnormal AI and an anonymous donor.
    • Conclusions: Model choice matters (Opus 4.6 was trained for injection resistance); simple explicit rules helped, and reasoning traces referred back to them; he still does not let his agents send email.
    • Caveats: Multi-turn attacks, weaker models, non-English attacks and a higher prize could all change the result.

    Takeaway: Restrict outbound actions and credential access, isolate each untrusted input in its own context, and watch or reset persistent agent memory; plan for inbound volume that can drain budgets and trip provider abuse systems.

  • OWASP Agentic Skills Top 10
    summary

    TL;DR: The OWASP Agentic Skills Top 10 (AST10) is an OWASP incubator project billed as the first security framework for AI agent skills, the under-protected “behavior layer” of agents.

    • Scope: MCP covers how the model talks to tools; AST10 covers what those tools actually do; each risk maps to the Cloud Security Alliance’s MAESTRO seven-layer threat model.
    • Project: Led by Ken Huang with co-leader Fabio Cerullo, version 1.0-2026, 23 contributors.
    • AST01 Malicious Skills (critical): Evidence includes the ClawHavoc campaign with 1,184 malicious skills; mitigated with signed manifests, Merkle-root verification and registry scanning.
    • AST02 Supply Chain Compromise (critical): Cites ClawHub registry poisoning and CVE-2025-59536 (CVSS 8.7) in Claude Code’s handling of repository config.
    • AST03 to AST04 (high): Over-Privileged Skills cites Snyk’s February 2026 finding of 280+ credential-leaking skills; Insecure Metadata covers fake “Google” skills and YAML payloads hidden in SKILL.md files.
    • AST05 to AST06 (high): Untrusted External Instructions cites a PoC that evaded every scanner and reached 26,000+ agents, with 17,822 skills (12.4%) pulling external instructions; Weak Isolation cites OpenClaw host-mode execution and 135,000+ exposed instances.
    • AST07 to AST10 (medium): Update Drift, Poor Scanning, No Governance, Cross-Platform Reuse; evidence includes ClawJacked (CVE-2026-28363, CVSS 9.9), pattern-matcher bypass via natural-language injection, 53,000 exposed instances with no SOC visibility, and malicious skills ported between ClawHub and skills.sh.
    • Data: Snyk’s ToxicSkills scan of 3,984 skills found 36.82% with flaws and 13.4% critical; Air Security scanned 142,836 skills affecting 6.7 million installs.
    • Scanners: VirusTotal, Cisco’s skill-scanner and snyk-agent-scan.

    Takeaway: Treat agent skills like any third-party dependency: inventory, pin versions, verify hashes, require provenance and signatures, enforce least-privilege manifests, sandbox execution, and pair semantic with behavioral scanning, since pattern matching misses natural-language payloads.

  • The AI-Native SDLC Playbook
    summary

    TL;DR: Anthropic’s AI-Native SDLC Playbook, by Louis Claxton, redesigns the software lifecycle for fast coding agents, moving human effort from doing steps to judging what agents flag at gates.

    • Problem: Once agents code fast, planning, review, testing and deployment become the human-speed bottleneck; line-by-line review controls stop scaling, and governance stalls when exceptions wait on weekly or monthly committees.
    • Loop: Six stages, Plan, Design, Build, Test, Deploy, Maintain, each committing a version-controlled artifact: intent.md, spec.md, plan.md, the diff and tests, the PR with review findings, the incident record; the commit chain doubles as the audit trail.
    • Build practice: Start in Claude Code plan mode and commit an approved plan.md before coding; keep CLAUDE.md under a page and update it when Claude repeats a mistake; encode knowledge as skills, use hooks as deterministic guardrails (“skills make violations rare, hooks make them nearly impossible”).
    • Testing: Claude gets a feedback loop of tests, builds and screenshots; bug fixes start with a failing test, with test-file edits blocked during the fix.
    • Evals: Run continuously in CI from a 20 to 50 task set drawn from recent real work; every production incident becomes a regression eval.
    • Deploy: AI PR review driven by a REVIEW.md policy, non-interactive sandboxed Claude in CI/CD, per-environment autonomy tiers, and a hook blocking production deploys without a named release authorization.
    • Maintain: Monitoring tiers by deviation: 1σ logged, 2σ read-only diagnosis, 3σ the agent may propose a PR or run a pre-approved runbook.
    • Governance: The writing agent may not approve its code, each agent job has its own identity, activity exports via OpenTelemetry; MDM-enforced managed settings deny .env files, ~/.ssh, ~/.aws/credentials and GITHUB_TOKEN, disable bypass-permissions mode, allow only managed hooks and MCP servers, and add an OS sandbox with a domain allowlist.
    • Measurement and rollout: DORA metrics, first-pass CI success, review time per PR, eval pass rate; start with two or three parallel sessions and read-only CI tasks.

    Takeaway: It is a vendor document centered on Anthropic tooling, but reuse its separation of duties, deterministic hook backstops and managed-settings baseline when rolling out coding agents on any platform team.

  • Detecting Multi-Stage Attacks on AWS: A Guide to Cross-Service Signal Correlation
    summary

    TL;DR: An AWS Security Blog post on spotting multi-stage attacks by correlating signals across services with your own business context, beyond what single alerts or managed detectors show.

    • Attack phases: Initial access (GetCallerIdentity or AssumeRole from unfamiliar sources), discovery (List, Describe, Get enumeration with AccessDenied noise), privilege escalation (PutRolePolicy, CreateAccessKey), lateral movement across accounts or Regions.
    • Exfiltration signs: Mass GetObject calls, large outbound VPC Flow Logs transfers, Route 53 Resolver queries to newly registered domains.
    • Managed baseline: GuardDuty with Extended Threat Detection links multi-stage sequences into critical attack-sequence findings mapped to MITRE ATT&CK, plus Security Hub, Detective, Inspector and Security Lake, covering threats common across customers.
    • Custom patterns: Bulk reads of a classified bucket by a principal outside the expected-readers list; a role chain of two or more AssumeRole calls ending in a sensitive action; non-owner use of a single-workload KMS key; privileged IAM, security group or bucket-policy changes by a human outside the change window.
    • Queries: CloudWatch Logs Insights for each; one counts GetObject per principal in 10-minute bins, another sums non-RFC 1918 egress bytes per source and flags over 1 GB per 10 minutes.
    • Worked example: Expected-readers list in version control, DynamoDB or Parameter Store; baseline a week of traffic, threshold above the 95th percentile; validate queries manually; map principals to source IPs via EC2, ENI or ECS metadata.
    • Automation: Hourly EventBridge Scheduler job triggers a Python Lambda (300-second timeout) that correlates CloudTrail and Flow Logs results and publishes to SNS.
    • Timing: CloudTrail delivers events 5 to 15 minutes after the call, so use a 30 to 60 minute lookback with a 10-minute correlation window.
    • Gotchas and hardening: S3 GetObject only appears with CloudTrail data events enabled; harden with aws:SourceAccount and aws:SourceArn conditions against confused-deputy abuse, plus least-privilege, ARN-scoped Lambda permissions.

    Takeaway: Enable and tune the managed detectors first, then turn written lists of expected readers, role chains, key owners, pipeline identities and change windows into a few baselined correlation rules, using organization trails for multi-account coverage.

  • Impeccable: The Missing Design Vocabulary for Agents
    summary

    TL;DR: Impeccable, by Paul Bakaus, is a design skill for AI coding agents, “the missing design vocabulary for agents,” meant to turn generic AI-looking interfaces into ones worth shipping.

    • Reach: Works with ten agents: Claude Code, Codex CLI, GitHub Copilot, Cursor, Gemini CLI, Grok Build, Antigravity, OpenCode, Pi and Hermes Agent; about 84k GitHub stars.
    • Context: Design-system aware; reads existing tokens, components and Tailwind config, and loads DESIGN.md (visual system) and PRODUCT.md (users and goals), which commands consult before acting.
    • Commands (1): Create: shape, generate; Evaluate: critique, audit; Refine: typeset, layout, colorize, animate, delight, bolder, quieter, overdrive.
    • Commands (2): Simplify: distill, clarify, adapt; Ship: harden, polish, optimize, onboard; System: init, extract, document, live, hooks on.
    • Slop detector: Deterministic, code-only check for 61 agent-default patterns such as italic serif display type, side-tab borders, nested cards and “AI beige”; needs no model or API key.
    • Integration: Hooks run the check on UI edits and feed findings back to the agent; the CLI returns findings and exit codes, so it can gate CI and pull requests.
    • Interactive tools: Live Mode picks elements in a running app to steer design; Variants generate comparable alternatives; a Chrome DevTools extension shows issues in the page.
    • Install: npx impeccable install, the Claude Code plugin marketplace, npx skills add, or a built-in experimental Copilot setting; then /impeccable init and /impeccable hooks on.
    • Numbers and limits: Example results cut JavaScript from 412 kB to 86 kB and LCP from 3.4s to 1.1s; the page does not state a license.

    Takeaway: Borrow the pattern, a deterministic linter with exit codes wired into agent hooks and CI, to make agent output measurable and gateable, and review its every-edit hooks like any third-party skill before approving.

  • Breaking Claude Code Opus 5 Auto Mode
    summary

    TL;DR: Johann Rehberger (Embrace The Red) shows Claude Code’s Auto Mode, the default starting mode since mid-August 2026, is not a security boundary, using a multi-step indirect prompt injection.

    • Auto Mode: Replaces per-action human approval with a safety classifier, backed by model training, input probes, and a Trajectory Labs benchmark reporting 0.00% attack success for Opus 5 across 72 indirect injection scenarios run ten times each.
    • Entry: The user only asks for a website summary; the attacker’s server returns HTTP 415 to the WebFetch tool, and the model falls back on its own to curl in the shell.
    • Payload delivery: The curl request follows a redirect to a ZIP with encoded records, a README pointing at a macOS decoder binary, and a malicious struct.py; the model refuses the binary and writes its own Python decoder.
    • Root cause: Module shadowing: running the decoder inside the extracted directory puts it on Python’s import path, so importing base64 loads the attacker’s obfuscated struct.py instead of the stdlib module.
    • Execution: The module re-exports the real API so decoding works, then launches an isolated child process that pulls a remote stager, runs a native payload with a C2 callback, and in the demo opens Calculator.
    • Variant: Spawns a headless claude -p instance with its own tool access that runs whoami, uname and id and writes files outside the workspace.
    • Numbers: 3 of 5 for the C2 chain, 3 of 5 for recon, 4 of 5 for file-write; small samples, with payloads iterated between runs.
    • Model behavior: Sometimes noticed the compromise later, sometimes misreported what it ran, once had its own cleanup kill command blocked by Auto Mode; when resisting, it analyzed statically, used python -I, or ran code from a safe parent directory.
    • Vendor response: Anthropic closed it as Informative, working as designed: Auto Mode is a best-effort convenience classifier and the real boundary is OS isolation and egress control, which the author says sits uneasily with the 0.00% messaging.

    Takeaway: Run unattended coding agents in a container, VM or OS sandbox with restricted, monitored egress, keep SSH keys, cloud credentials and home directories out of reach, and never treat a classifier approval as proof a command is safe.

  • Automate IAM Identity Center Governance with Continuous Discovery and Reporting
    summary

    TL;DR: A late-August 2026 AWS Security Blog post on auditing which users and groups can reach IAM Identity Center managed applications across accounts and Regions, with a sample reporting and remediation solution.

    • Problem: Identity Center centralizes authentication, but at scale it is hard to answer who can reach which managed applications, who accessed them last, and how fast an auditor can get a report.
    • Planning: Use delegated administration; restrict who can provision managed applications via IAM, SCPs, RCPs or IaC policy tools; separate IaC provisioning pipelines from request-based group-membership workflows; use trusted identity propagation for an end-to-end identity trail.
    • Root cause: Application ARNs contain generated GUIDs, making resource-scoped IAM policies impractical, so keep management in a few principals and lean on detective and reactive controls.
    • Naming convention: Strict IdP group names such as AWS_<ACCT#>_<AWS_Service>_<LOB>_<ENV>_<AppName> let assignments be validated against application names.
    • Reporting stack: Sample sample-iam-idc-application-discovery-reporting, two AWS CDK stacks; runs daily at 2:00 AM UTC via EventBridge and Step Functions; Lambdas discover instances, applications and user or group assignments with friendly names into KMS-encrypted DynamoDB.
    • Exports: IAM-authenticated API Gateway writes CSV reports to encrypted S3, with presigned URLs valid for 900 seconds.
    • Remediation stack: EventBridge catches CloudTrail events from sso.amazonaws.com such as CreateApplicationAssignment and PutApplicationAssignmentConfiguration, validates against the naming rule, then alerts via SNS or remediates, with an SQS dead-letter queue and structured CloudWatch logs.
    • Matching rule: By default a group name must appear as a whole, case-insensitive word in the application name (ReadOnly matches sagemaker_readonly, read does not); a custom regex can be supplied; example findings flag a PROD app assigned directly to a user and a PROD app assigned to a DEV group.
    • Setup: Needs an Identity Center delegated admin, Python 3.12 and Node.js 18+, optional AllowedIpRange and a cross-account external ID; the example disables auto-deletion at first.

    Takeaway: Deploy reporting first for a baseline, agree on naming conventions before automating, and review findings before enabling enforcement; reuse the EventBridge, CloudTrail and Lambda detective-control pattern for any AWS resource IAM policies cannot easily scope.

    summary

    TL;DR: Doyensec’s CloudSec Tidbits repo pairs a blog series on web apps misusing correctly configured AWS services with deployable Terraform labs for each bug.

    • Format: Each episode covers one vulnerability from a risky web plus cloud mix, with an IaC lab to reproduce it.

  • Shai-Hulud Goes Open Source
    summary

    TL;DR: Datadog statically analysed the leaked source of TeamPCP’s Shai-Hulud supply-chain framework, covering how it steals CI and cloud credentials, spreads through npm and GitHub, and how to hunt it.

    • Context: The source showed up in a GitHub repo on May 12, 2026, was removed, but forks had already spread. It is a modular TypeScript/Bun toolkit.
    • Architecture: Loaders, providers (files, env, CI runners, AWS, Kubernetes, Vault), a collector with a 100KB flush, a dispatcher with failover, senders and mutators.
    • Credential theft: Scans 100+ sensitive paths, reads the gh token and env, and scrapes CI runner memory before masking applies. It enumerates Secrets Manager and SSM across 17 regions.
    • Exfil and C2: AES-256-GCM payloads wrapped with RSA-4096-OAEP. Data goes over HTTPS C2 or uses GitHub repos as dead drops, with a fallback C2 found through signed commits.
    • Propagation: Plants IDE and AI-assistant hooks in branches and publishes backdoored npm packages with stolen tokens or CI OIDC, including forged Sigstore provenance.
    • Persistence and sabotage: A LaunchAgent or systemd user monitor runs a destructive command if the stolen GitHub token is revoked. It self-terminates after 24 hours.
    • Hunting: Look for SessionStart hooks calling setup.mjs in .claude/settings.json, and .vscode/tasks.json with runOn: "folderOpen". Also check for chore: update dependencies commits and new preinstall scripts.
    • Limits: Static analysis only. Compiled hashes cannot be reproduced from source, and three known TeamPCP capabilities are missing from this code.

    Takeaway: Kill the persistence monitor before you rotate tokens. Audit repos for malicious Claude Code and VS Code hooks, and move npm publishing to trusted publishing with provenance checks.

  • vanguard: Cross-Platform DFIR Toolkit in a Single Portable Binary
    summary

    TL;DR: VanGuard is a single-binary Go DFIR toolkit for Windows and Linux that wraps common IR tools behind TUI and web interfaces, with case management and chain of custody.

    • Problem: IR work means juggling many tools and flags and tracking evidence by hand. VanGuard puts the whole lifecycle in one portable program.
    • Integrations: 11 tools, including Velociraptor, Hayabusa, Chainsaw, Loki, KAPE, EZ Tools, UAC, WinPMEM, AVML and Volatility3. They are downloaded at runtime from GitHub releases.
    • Evidence handling: A SQLite case database, MD5 and SHA256 hashing at collection time, an append-only chain of custody and HMAC-SHA256 audit logs.
    • Triage: Native collection of 20+ Windows and 15+ Linux artifact categories, plus live checks for LOLBins, autoruns, SUID binaries and persistence.
    • Remote ops: Runs in parallel across endpoints over WinRM (NTLM), SSH or PSExec, and manages Velociraptor server and agent deployment.
    • Use cases: 28 YAML workflows mapped to MITRE ATT&CK (13 Windows, 12 Linux, 3 cross-platform), such as ransomware, DCSync and container escape.
    • Setup: Run as root or Administrator. Build from source with CGO_ENABLED=1 go build. Air-gapped mode uses SHA256-verified offline bundles.
    • Caveats: Claims like “tamper-evident” are self-described. It depends heavily on third-party tools, and the README lists no limitations.

    Takeaway: Consider VanGuard as a jump-kit to stage on USB for air-gapped triage. Validate its collection and hashing in a lab before you rely on it for evidence.

  • Skill Issues: Compromising Claude Code with Malicious Skills & Agents - Part 1
    summary

    TL;DR: Reversec shows how malicious Claude Code skills and sub-agents in untrusted repos can get command execution on a developer machine. Treat them like untrusted packages.

    • Thesis: Skill and agent .md files are effectively executable instructions, and they are often installed from sites like skills.sh with little vetting.
    • Dynamic context injection: !`...` commands in a skill run before the LLM reasons about them, with no prompt. A socat reverse shell ran this way.
    • allowed-tools: Frontmatter allowed-tools: Bash(*) pre-approves Bash, but it does not override user deny rules.
    • Read-only abuse: Auto-approved commands like git diff can run a script through a pre-set git config diff.external. This needs setup outside the session.
    • Subcommand cap: Commands with more than 50 subcommands skip deny-list checks but still prompt. Code comments say the cap is not a security boundary.
    • Agents: permissionMode: bypassPermissions in attacker-written agents ran a reverse shell. An agent also installed a backdoored npm package under a plausible pretext.
    • Defence: Deny rules in user settings override skill and agent permissions and worked as a guardrail. Denying Bash blocks these attacks but costs usefulness.
    • Caveats: The examples were deliberately unstealthy and tested on Ubuntu against one version. Plugin agents reportedly ignore permissionMode, which is still untested.

    Takeaway: Review any .claude/ skills, agents and CLAUDE.md before you trust a repo. Enforce deny rules through managed settings, and sandbox coding agents from the host and the network.

  • Running Codex Safely
    summary

    TL;DR: OpenAI describes how it deploys Codex internally: sandboxing, approval policies, managed network and identity controls, and agent-native OpenTelemetry logs fed to an AI triage agent.

    • Principle: Codex is productive inside a bounded environment. Low-risk actions are frictionless, and higher-risk actions stop for review.
    • Sandbox vs approvals: The sandbox sets the technical boundary: writable paths, network and protected paths. The approval policy decides when Codex must ask.
    • Auto-review: A subagent gets the planned action and recent context, then approves low-risk actions automatically instead of interrupting the user.
    • Network: No open-ended egress. A managed policy allows expected destinations, blocks unwanted ones and requires approval for unfamiliar domains.
    • Identity: CLI and MCP OAuth credentials live in the OS keyring. Login is forced through ChatGPT and pinned to the enterprise workspace.
    • Rules and config: Command rules allow benign commands and block or gate dangerous ones. Cloud requirements, macOS managed preferences and local requirements files enforce non-overridable settings.
    • Telemetry: OpenTelemetry export covers prompts, approval decisions, tool results, MCP usage and network proxy allow or deny events, and can go to a SIEM.
    • Triage: Endpoint alerts are paired with Codex logs so an AI triage agent can explain user and agent intent before humans review.

    Takeaway: For coding agents, enforce sandbox, egress allow-lists and approval rules through managed config. Ship agent logs to your SIEM so alerts come with intent context.

  • entra-ca-insight: Discover Gaps in Entra Conditional Access Policies Before Attackers Do
    summary

    TL;DR: CA Insight is an open-source tool that finds gaps in Entra Conditional Access proactively. It evaluates every access combination offline, so gaps show up before anyone exploits them.

    • Problem: Sign-in-log gap analysis is reactive. Gaps nobody has used yet never appear in the logs.
    • Gap definition: An access combination (identity, app, platform, location, client type) where no policy enforces block, MFA or authentication strength.
    • How it works: Pulls policies and Entra objects from Microsoft Graph, generates all access combinations and evaluates them offline against the CA configuration.
    • Lineage: The identity-centric approach is inspired by Joosua Santasalo’s archived caOptics project.
    • Coverage: Member, guest and external users, workload identities and agent identities.
    • Interfaces: A CLI for CI/CD automation and a web portal with scan history, coverage trends, config comparison and exports.
    • Safety: Read-only operations that never modify policies.
    • Caveats: A personal, as-is project (GPL-3.0, “Vibe Coded” badge). The README lists no Graph permissions, and install steps are in the wiki.

    Takeaway: Run CA Insight against your tenant in CI to catch identities or apps that fall back to password-only. Validate findings in non-prod before you change policies.

  • ghosttype: Forensic Scanner for Credentials in AI Tool Conversation History
    summary

    TL;DR: ghosttype is a local forensic scanner that extracts secrets from AI tool conversation history and verifies them with TruffleHog, showing which leaked credentials are still live.

    • Problem: Developers paste secrets into AI tools, and the conversations sit in local files.
    • Sources: Claude Code JSONL history, Cursor state.vscdb, Codex CLI SQLite and logs, and Keychain-encrypted ChatGPT Desktop files. Claude Desktop is a stub only.
    • How it works: Per-tool decoders write text to temp files, run TruffleHog as a subprocess, map hits back to conversations, then delete the temp directory.
    • Engines: TruffleHog has 800+ detectors with live verification. An in-tree engine adds 30 regex and 10 heuristic patterns that run offline and are never verified.
    • Usage: ghosttype scan, --only-verified, --no-verification, --redact and --max-age-days. Output goes to findings.json and findings.csv.
    • CI: Exit codes support pipeline gating, and ghosttype doctor checks the environment. Requires Python 3.11+ and TruffleHog 3.x.
    • Detection note: Verification calls credential issuers like AWS, GitHub and Stripe, which can appear in their audit logs.
    • Limits: Full coverage is macOS only, and pattern hits may be false positives. Use only with written authorization.

    Takeaway: Scan your own workstation’s AI chat history with ghosttype, rotate any verified hits, and keep secrets out of prompts.

  • Practitioner’s Guide to Agentic AI Security
    summary

    TL;DR: Nudge Security’s vendor guide argues that employee-adopted AI agents act as unmanaged digital workers with persistent SaaS credentials, and sets out four steps to govern them.

    • Problem: Agents are spun up inside existing SaaS tools and browsers without security review, and they inherit the delegating employee’s permissions.
    • Connectivity: MCP servers bridge agents to systems like Zendesk and Salesforce, turning them into cross-system actors.
    • Numbers: Over 90% of apps are adopted outside IT. The average organization runs 26 AI apps, and each employee holds 70 OAuth grants, 11 of them high risk (Nudge data).
    • Blind spots: Shadow agentic AI, long-lived OAuth tokens and API keys, workflows that cross compliance boundaries, and fewer chances to intervene.

    • Step 2, assess: Continuously evaluate access, data exposure, integration (including prompt injection) and behavioural risk.

    • Step 4, workforce: Clear AI acceptable-use policy, just-in-time browser prompts and a fast approval path. Cites Gartner 2022: 69% of employees bypass controls.
    • Caveats: The publisher sells SaaS and AI discovery tooling. Statistics are vendor-sourced, and the guide contains no technical controls beyond process.

  • Automating Security Operations With AI: Triage and Renovate
    summary

    TL;DR: Marco Lancini pairs monthly Renovate runs with a Claude Code skill and cloud routine that auto-reviews each dependency PR and posts a risk matrix before a human looks.

    • Problem: Majors, 0.x jumps, dead dependencies and deprecated framework configs slip past CI. Manual review of every bump does not scale.

    summary

    TL;DR: Google’s AX (Agent Executor) is an open-source, Kubernetes-style declarative orchestrator for running sandboxed autonomous agent workloads at scale on top of Agent Substrate.

    • Problem: Agents are neither stateless services nor batch jobs. They hold state, need strict isolation, call model and tool APIs, and can run up costs in loops.

  • The AWS AI Security Framework: Securing AI with the Right Controls, at the Right Layers, at the Right Phases
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS’s AI Security Framework maps controls for AI workloads across three use cases, three layers and three maturity phases, and names the specific AWS service for each control.

    • Thesis: Build AI on top of existing security rather than bolting security onto AI.
    • Why AI differs: Non-deterministic outputs, mixed prompts that enable injection, adapting behaviour and autonomy, which require output validation, continuous monitoring and scoped authorization.
    • Use cases: AI that answers (chat), AI that connects (RAG) and AI that acts (agents, A2A, MCP, physical AI). Controls are cumulative.
    • Layers: Infrastructure (Nitro, VPC, Network Firewall), identity and data (IAM, KMS, Secrets Manager, AgentCore Identity), and the AI application layer (Bedrock Guardrails, WAF, CloudWatch).
    • Phases: Foundational (identity, encryption, filtering, logging), Enhanced (classification, threat detection, IR) and Advanced (governance, testing, forensics).
    • Agent controls: AgentCore Policy with Cedar authorization independent of the model, per-agent temporary credentials and Strands SDK hooks for human-in-the-loop.
    • Worked example: A prompt-injection attempt passes ten control points from Cognito MFA through Guardrails and egress inspection to GuardDuty and CloudTrail.
    • Numbers: Cites McKinsey (80% adopt AI, 10% govern it), IBM (97% of breached orgs lacked AI access controls) and up to 99% Automated Reasoning accuracy.
    • Caveats: Service lists are non-exhaustive. Some features are in preview, vendor accuracy claims are unverified and there is no MITRE ATLAS or NIST AI RMF mapping.

    Takeaway: Classify each AI workload as answers, connects or acts, then apply that tier’s controls. For agents, enforce tool authorization outside the model with Cedar and use scoped per-agent credentials.

  • Governing Infrastructure as Code Using Pattern-Based Policy as Code
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS proposes organizing IaC policy-as-code around five reusable control patterns, enforced pre-deployment with OPA on Terraform plan JSON in CI, and layered with AWS-native runtime governance.

    • Problem: Unapproved Regions, open security groups, missing tags and assumed but absent encryption. Manual review does not scale.
    • Patterns: Required metadata, allowed configuration, exposure restriction, protection enforcement and privilege constraint. They give security, GRC and engineering a shared vocabulary.
    • How it works: terraform show -json turns the plan into JSON, which is evaluated by a shared OPA/Rego library in GitLab CI or GitHub Actions, and results are kept as artifacts.
    • Example rules: S3 buckets without a Deny on aws:SecureTransport false, 0.0.0.0/0 on ports 22, 3389 and 5432, and wildcard principals in role trust policies.

    • Gates: Quality gates are automated pass/fail. Approval gates are human promotion decisions informed by the OPA artifact.
    • Caveats: The sample rules are narrow: IPv4 only, exact principal and action forms, Allow only. They need review, positive and negative tests, and an exception process.
    • Rollout: Start advisory with two or three high-confidence patterns, enforce stable rules, then version the package and expand.

  • prempti: Falco-Powered Guardrails and Real-Time Visibility for AI Coding Agents
    summary

    TL;DR: Prempti is an early-preview Falco Sandbox project that intercepts AI coding agent tool calls and returns allow, deny or ask verdicts from Falco rules, with a full audit trail.

    • How it works: An agent hook fires before each tool call; the interceptor sends it over a Unix socket to a Falco plugin whose rules return Allow, Deny or Ask.
    • Modes: Guardrails enforces verdicts; Monitor logs them but always lets calls proceed, useful for rule tuning. Switch with premptictl mode <guardrails|monitor>.
    • Coverage: Shell commands, file read/write/edit, web fetches and MCP calls; denials include an explanation the agent can act on.
    • Default rules: Deny sensitive paths like ~/.ssh/, ~/.aws/, .env; flag pipe-to-shell, reverse shells, IMDS access, exfiltration, .mcp.json poisoning, git hook and registry-redirect persistence.
    • Custom rules: Standard Falco YAML in ~/.prempti/rules/user/, surviving upgrades; a prempti-falco-rules Claude Code skill helps draft and validate them.
    • Platforms: Claude Code supported on Linux, macOS and Windows (x86_64 and aarch64); Codex support on Linux is experimental via codex-interceptor.
    • Setup: macOS .pkg (unsigned, Gatekeeper may block), Linux tarball with install.sh and a systemd user service, Windows .msi; verify with premptictl health.
    • Limits: Hook-level only, so it sees requested commands, not their effects; Bash is judged as declared and MCP only on input. It is not a sandbox.

    Takeaway: Run Prempti in Monitor mode first to audit what your coding agents actually do, then enforce rules, but keep real containment and least privilege underneath it.
