  • Comparing AI Application Security Testing Platforms
    summary

    TL;DR: Doyensec compared Aikido Attack AI Pentest and XBOW Lightspeed on two random OSS web apps; both found real bugs with few false positives, differences mostly incremental.

    • Setup: Targets picked at random from 442 apps derived from awesome-selfhosted: Fider 0.33.0 (Go) and Photoview 2.4.0 (TypeScript), on a t2.medium EC2.
    • Sponsorship: Aikido funded the study and shaped constraints, but Doyensec says it had no role in data collection or results.
    • Cost: Both run at about $4,000 tiers (Aikido Standard, XBOW Plus); Aikido offers unlimited retests within 90 days, XBOW one retest within 30.
    • Numbers: Aikido 49 true positives and 2 false positives across 51 findings; XBOW 31 true positives and 1 false positive; FP/TP ratio 4% vs 3%.
    • Severity: Both mostly overstated severity; correct severity about 69% for Aikido and 68% for XBOW after manual review.

    • Operations: Aikido took under 20 minutes to configure and about 8 hours per scan; XBOW needed sales approval, 22+ emails and over a week for Fider.

  • From Leaked AWS Key to Data Exfiltration in 60 Seconds: Are We Ready?
    summary

    TL;DR: An experiment shows Claude Code, given only a leaked low-privilege AWS key and no AWS guidance, reached data exfiltration in about one minute in 7 of 12 runs.

    • Scenario: A CI/CD IAM user can read Terraform state in S3; the state holds a second user’s access keys, which can sts:AssumeRole into a role with customer data.
    • Agent: Plain Claude Code with Sonnet and Haiku, prompted as an authorized pentest or CTF; 6 runs each, Sonnet succeeding far more often.
    • Speed: Most successful runs finished in about 1 minute; the author estimates a skilled human needs at least 10 to 20 minutes.
    • Kill chain: Every success followed six phases: identity check, policy read, credential recovery from state, role assumption, bucket enumeration, exfiltration.
    • Failure point: Runs failed when the agent skipped ListUserPolicies + GetUserPolicy, missed the bucket name, and fell back to brute-forcing S3 names.
    • Detection signals: Every run began with GetCallerIdentity; successes ran ListUserPolicies, GetUserPolicy and the first GetObject within about 15 seconds.
    • Session names: All 7 successful AssumeRole calls used session names containing pentest or ctf, a cheap detection hook.
    • Caveat: CloudTrail delivers logs about every 5 minutes, so log-based alerting and automation may fire after exfiltration is already done.
    • Reproduce: Terraform for the lab is in the author’s cloud-ranges repo under aws-tfstate-exfil.

    Takeaway: Never let Terraform write access keys into state, restrict who can read state buckets, and add honeytokens because CloudTrail-based response is too slow for agent-speed attacks.

  • Hidden Gaps in Claude Code Security Reviews
    summary

    TL;DR: An experiment shows Claude Code’s /security-review misses real bugs when run in the session that wrote the code, and the diff-only security plugin misses cross-commit permission chains.

    • Tools compared: /security-review (full branch, same or new session), the May 2026 security guidance plugin (current-turn diff, separate Opus 4.7 call), and Team/Enterprise Code Review.
    • Test target: A vibe-coded Telegram bot seeded with fail-open auth, unrestricted Bash(python3:*) in --allowedTools, and a Write plus scoped python3 write-then-execute chain.
    • Same-session bias: In-session review reported zero vulnerabilities; a cold session found the fail-open auth (High, 0.95) and an unseeded path traversal (Medium, 0.82).
    • Root cause: The reviewer inherits the conversation and validates design choices instead of challenging them; its false-positive filter dismissed a real chain as “same person”.
    • Known-safe trap: A cold reviewer saw list-form subprocess.run without shell=True, marked it safe, and never evaluated the broad Bash(python3:*) permission.
    • Diff isolation: The plugin flagged Write and python3:* separately when added together, but found nothing when Write was already committed earlier.
    • Side effect: A commit message naming removed permissions led Claude to restore broad python3:*; the author notes the sample is small.
    • Caveats: One codebase, three seeded flaws; the cost rationale for diff-only scope is the author’s inference.
    • Mitigation idea: A session hook warning on same-session review works, but decision: block forces a rerun and fails silently in Claude Desktop.

    Takeaway: Run /security-review in a fresh session, review permission combinations across commits rather than single diffs, and keep human review in the loop.

  • container: Linux Container Machines on macOS
    summary

    TL;DR: Apple’s container docs describe container machines: persistent Linux environments on macOS built from OCI images that share your home directory, trading container isolation for convenience.

    • What it is: A full Linux install, not a single app, built from a standard OCI image whose own /sbin/init runs as PID 1.
    • Home sharing: Your macOS home is mounted read-write at the same path and SSH_AUTH_SOCK is forwarded, so you edit on macOS and build in Linux.
    • Isolation loss: All Linux capabilities are added and /proc and /sys are unmasked and writable; the docs warn this is close to running a program directly on the host.
    • Accounts: A guest user matching host UID/GID and home is created with passwordless sudo (NOPASSWD:ALL), reapplied on every boot.
    • Commands: container machine create, run, set-default, ls, inspect, stop, rm and set; machine can be shortened to m.
    • Config: cpus, memory (default half of host RAM), home mount rw, ro or none; changes apply only after stop and start.
    • Nested virtualization: Needs Apple Silicon M3 or later, macOS 15 or later, and a kernel with CONFIG_KVM=y; the default kernel lacks it.
    • Custom images: Must provide /sbin/init, /bin/sh, getent, date with %s, chown, mkdir, cp and tr; sudo is optional.
    • Use cases: Per-distro dev machines (Alpine, Ubuntu, Debian), real services via systemctl, and macOS debuggers or browsers against Linux builds.

    Takeaway: Use container machines only with images you trust, and set the home mount to ro or none when a machine does not need write access to your files.

  • Well-Architected Best Practices for Software Supply Chain Security
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS maps layered consumer-side defenses against malicious packages to Well-Architected Security Pillar practices, motivated by npm incidents like Shai-Hulud, Chalk/Debug and axios.

    • Temporary credentials (SEC02-BP02): Use aws login or IAM Identity Center for developers and OIDC federation for CI/CD; store unavoidable third-party secrets in Secrets Manager with rotation.
    • Least privilege and rotation: Apply SEC03-BP02 and SEC02-BP05; during an incident rotate all long-term credentials and use GuardDuty and CloudTrail to find abnormal IAM activity.
    • Defense in depth: MFA, separate roles for sensitive workloads, and multi-party approval inside deployment pipelines so one stolen developer credential cannot reach production.
    • Artifact signing: Restrict signer:StartSigningJob to pipeline roles; ECR managed signing signs on push; Kyverno on EKS or ECS lifecycle hooks verify before deploy.
    • Dependency control (SEC11-BP05): CodeArtifact package groups allow only approved upstreams, guarding against typosquatting; pin versions and pull compromised ones fast.
    • Provenance: Check npm Sigstore provenance (npm 9.5+) before adding dependencies; maintainers publish with npm publish --provenance; it is a signal, not a guarantee.
    • Scanning: Inspector covers code, dependencies, IaC and ECR images; keep SPDX or CycloneDX SBOMs to assess blast radius.
    • Detection: Watch CloudTrail for ecr:PutImage outside CI/CD, lambda:UpdateFunctionCode outside deploy windows, iam:CreateAccessKey, unexpected sts:AssumeRole.
    • Caveats: CVE scanners lag new malicious packages by days or weeks; sleeper packages evade static analysis; approvals slow deploys.

    Takeaway: Move CI/CD to OIDC, gate production deploys on signature verification, proxy dependencies through an allow-listed registry, and alert on artifact pushes outside pipelines.

  • Securing CI/CD in an Agentic World: Claude Code GitHub Action Case
    summary

    TL;DR: Microsoft found prompt injection in GitHub issues or PRs could make the Claude Code GitHub Action read /proc/self/environ and leak ANTHROPIC_API_KEY; fixed in 2.1.128.

    • Root cause: Bash ran in a Bubblewrap sandbox with a scrubbed environment (CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB), but the Read tool ran in-process and saw the full environment.
    • Injection: Payloads hide in issue bodies, PR descriptions or comments, including HTML comments invisible when rendered.
    • Safety bypass: Framed as a “compliance review”, the prompt asked to trim the first seven characters, hiding the sk-ant- prefix from refusals and GitHub secret scanning.
    • Exfiltration: Via WebFetch or Bash to a remote domain, a GitHub MCP issue comment, or the Action log when show_full_output is enabled.
    • Exposure: Depends on workflow config, which is usually public in .github/workflows/; other runner credentials may also be exposed.
    • Timeline: Reported via HackerOne on April 29, 2026; mitigated May 5, 2026 by rejecting sensitive /proc files. No CVE listed.
    • Related case: An injection attempt steered an issue-triage bot toward adding an XSS payload to documentation via a pull request.
    • Method: Black-box testing found nothing; white-box review of the Action code and obfuscated Agent SDK found the flaw.
    • Mitigations: Apply the Agents Rule of Two (untrusted input, secrets access, external effects: never all three), one scoped key per workflow, and hardened system prompts.

    Takeaway: Update the Claude Code Action to 2.1.128 or later, rotate exposed keys, and never give an agent workflow that reads untrusted issues both secrets and outbound channels.

  • Identity and Access Management Whitepaper
    summary

    TL;DR: CNCF TAG Security and Compliance whitepaper giving standards-based authentication and authorization requirements for Kubernetes workloads in two reference patterns: perimeter-based Basic and zero-trust Advanced.

    • Scope: Runtime, synchronous HTTP and gRPC access control in one cluster and one trust domain; excludes SCIM, PAM, MCP, A2A, batch and multi-cluster federation.
    • Standards: OAuth 2.0, OIDC, NIST SP 800-162 PEP/PDP architecture, AuthZEN Authorization API 1.0, SPIFFE and NIST SP 800-63 Rev 4.
    • Basic pattern: Treats the cluster as one trust zone; TLS, token validation and PDP authorization required at the perimeter, internal mTLS and MFA not mandatory.
    • Advanced pattern: Every workload is a trust boundary; TLS everywhere, mTLS via SPIFFE SVIDs, MFA at AAL2 minimum, and sender-constrained tokens via DPoP or mTLS.
    • User auth: Authorization code flow with PKCE only, S256 enforced; codes single-use, ideally under 60 seconds; code reuse should revoke issued tokens.
    • Authorization: Enforce at function, object and property level; PDP not reachable outside the cluster; prefer ABAC or ReBAC since RBAC leads to role explosion.
    • Token handling: Exchange external for internal tokens (RFC 8693) to strip PII; validate signature, expiry, issuer, audience, subject and revocation.
    • Logging and privacy: Log every request with a unique ID and decision but never tokens or PII; use opaque sub claims to ease GDPR erasure.
    • Caveats: Not a full regulated-environment profile; systems with regulatory requirements should adopt FAPI rather than approximate it with this pattern.

    Takeaway: Use the Advanced pattern for anything sensitive or public: SPIFFE-issued workload identity, a central PDP behind every service, and PKCE with MFA for all users.

  • GitHub Actions Security Checklist for Supply Chain Attacks
    summary

    TL;DR: A checklist for hardening GitHub Actions as supply chain infrastructure, against poisoned pipeline execution, secret theft, mutable action compromise and over-scoped tokens.

    • Do first: Read-only default GITHUB_TOKEN, third-party actions pinned to full commit SHAs, no pull_request_target on public repos, OIDC instead of static cloud secrets.
    • Permissions: Start from permissions: {} and grant per job; give id-token: write only to the OIDC job; split build/test from privileged publish.
    • Triggers: Never check out PR head code in pull_request_target; guard workflow_run; audit issue_comment and review triggers; use pull_request for forks.
    • Script injection: Pass untrusted context like github.head_ref through env: and quote it; never write untrusted data to GITHUB_ENV or GITHUB_PATH.
    • Pinning: Tags like @v4 can move; verify SHAs are not imposter fork commits; watch nested actions on mutable tags; update via Dependabot or Renovate with a release-age delay.
    • Secrets: Scope to environments with reviewers, pass per step, never as CLI args, avoid secrets: inherit; masking fails on transformed values.
    • Runners: No self-hosted runners on public repos; use ephemeral runners, separate groups by trust level, and restrict egress.
    • Artifacts: Avoid path: . uploads, set persist-credentials: false on actions/checkout, skip caches in release workflows, and use package cooldowns.
    • Detection: Lint with zizmor and OpenSSF Scorecard, enable CodeQL for workflows, keep an incident playbook. Incidents cited include tj-actions/changed-files and trivy-action.

    Takeaway: Inventory workflows, enforce read-only tokens org-wide, fix pull_request_target and interpolation first, then SHA-pin and run zizmor in CI. Note Corgea promotes its own products.

  • docker-mailserver: Production-Ready Fullstack Containerized Mail Server (SMTP, IMAP, LDAP, Anti-spam, Anti-virus)
    summary

    TL;DR: docker-mailserver is an MIT-licensed, config-file-only containerized mail stack bundling SMTP, IMAP, anti-spam, anti-virus and DKIM/DMARC, aimed at simple self-hosted production mail.

    • Design: Configuration files only, no SQL database, so a whole mail setup fits in version control and upgrades are simple.
    • Mail core: Postfix for SMTP and Dovecot for IMAP, POP3, SASL, quotas and basic Sieve filtering, with SMTP or LDAP authentication.
    • Anti-spam: Rspamd, Amavis, SpamAssassin with custom rules, Postscreen and Postgrey greylisting.
    • Anti-virus: ClamAV with automatic signature updates.
    • Email auth and protection: OpenDKIM and OpenDMARC for signing and policy, Fail2ban for brute-force blocking.
    • Extras: Fetchmail and Getmail6 retrieval, OAuth2 via XOAUTH2 or OAUTHBEARER, Let’s Encrypt, manual or self-signed certificates, alias delimiters.
    • Management: A setup script handles accounts, aliases, quotas and maintenance; install steps live in the external docs, not the README.
    • Caveats: Docs are versioned, so match the docs to your image version; the issue tracker is for bugs only, not personal support.
    • Project: Created by tomav, volunteer-maintained since January 2021; about 19.7k stars and 3,005 commits at capture.

    Takeaway: If you must self-host mail, start here and keep the config in git, but plan for DNS, deliverability and reputation work the README does not cover.

  • warpgate: Smart, Transparent SSH/HTTPS/Kubernetes/MySQL/PostgreSQL Bastion Host
    summary

    TL;DR: Warpgate is an open-source Rust bastion host and PAM tool that transparently proxies SSH, HTTPS, Kubernetes, databases, RDP and VNC with 2FA, SSO and session recording, no client needed.

    • How it works: Clients connect with specially formatted credentials; Warpgate authenticates locally, connects to the target itself, joins both sides and optionally records.
    • Protocols: SSH, HTTPS, Kubernetes, MySQL, PostgreSQL, RDP and VNC; browser-based SSH, RDP and VNC built in, native clients still work.
    • Access control: Users map one-to-one to specific hosts and URLs instead of getting broad network access as with a VPN or jump host.
    • Authentication: Local accounts with TOTP 2FA, SSO via OpenID Connect, and temporary-access “tickets”.
    • Audit: Every session recorded for live view or replay in the admin UI; history stored in SQLite under /var/lib/warpgate.
    • Protection: Built-in IP blocking and user lockout against brute force, no fail2ban needed.
    • Setup: Single binary with no dependencies; warpgate setup generates the config; Docker guide available; building needs Rust, Node.js and just.
    • Comparison: The project’s own table claims advantages over jump hosts, VPNs and Teleport, such as no custom client and free SSO; it is self-reported.
    • Caveats: Self-hosting means you own upgrades and ops; “command-level audit” is listed but not explained; AI-assisted contributions are disclosed.

    Takeaway: Consider Warpgate as a lightweight self-hosted alternative to Teleport for recorded, SSO-gated access to servers and databases, but harden and patch the bastion itself.

  • Detecting and Removing Dangerous Secrets on Dev Workstations Before Shai-Hulud Does
    summary

    TL;DR: A proof of concept combines Boost Security’s bagel scanner with Fleet and osquery to find plaintext secrets on developer laptops and block SSO for non-compliant machines.

    • Problem: Package managers, IDE extensions and AI agents run code on workstations, and plaintext secrets there are prime infostealer targets.
    • Scanner: bagel, a Go CLI that probes the home directory for SSH keys, GitHub and cloud credentials, reporting severity and path as JSON without logging values.
    • Pipeline: A macOS LaunchAgent runs bagel on a schedule; Fleet reads the JSON via osquery’s parse_json table.
    • Policy: A Fleet policy fails the machine on critical findings or stale results, then sends the developer a Slack notification.
    • Enforcement: An IdP like Entra can check the Fleet policy during SSO, for example via Conditional Access, and block sign-in until fixed.
    • Glue: The author’s Fleebag repo packages the LaunchAgent, Fleet queries and a sample MDM full-disk-access profile.
    • Numbers: None; the article reports no fleet size, secret counts or remediation rates.
    • Caveats: Hasty PoC; ad-hoc signed build you must re-sign; placeholder MDM profile; it detects but never removes secrets, since automated cleanup risks data loss.
    • Complements: Move secrets to password managers or hardware keys such as YubiKey for SSH and GPG, and restrict secret files to trusted apps.

    Takeaway: Schedule endpoint secret scans through your MDM, tie results to device-compliance checks at SSO, and give developers a clear path to move secrets into managed stores.

  • cicd-sensor: eBPF-Powered Runtime Security Sensor for CI/CD Pipelines (EDR for CI/CD)
    summary

    TL;DR: cicd-sensor is an open-source eBPF runtime security sensor for GitHub Actions and GitLab CI, an “EDR for pipelines” that records what actually ran and can kill malicious jobs.

    • Problem: CI jobs hold cloud credentials, signing keys and registry tokens, and supply-chain attackers inside a job leave no evidence once it ends.
    • Gap: Falco, Tetragon, Tracee, Wazuh and OSQuery never reached CI/CD; Sigstore proves where an artifact was built, not what ran.
    • Detection: Uses process ancestry, such as credential access from a child of npm install, and correlates signals like several credential categories read in one job.
    • Response: On detection it kills the process and stops the job; network blocking is only partial because it is not a firewall.
    • Evidence: Emits per-run logs to S3, GCS, Azure Blob or Pub/Sub, plus a job-summary report and a runtime-trace build attestation.
    • Platforms: GitHub-hosted, self-hosted runners and GitLab Docker executor supported; ARC and GitLab Kubernetes executor in preview; GitLab-hosted runners unsupported.
    • Setup: Add cicd-sensor/cicd-sensor-action pinned by SHA as the first step; needs Linux kernel 5.15+ on amd64 or 6.1+ on arm64.
    • Rules: Baseline rules in rules/ are on by default (opt-out), and custom rules are supported.
    • Caveats: Pre-release software; versus Harden-Runner free tier it adds private repos, self-hosted and GitLab support but weaker network blocking.

    Takeaway: Trial it on a few high-value pipelines (release, publish, deploy) to get process-level evidence and credential-theft detection, and keep egress controls separately since network blocking is partial.

  • bumblebee: Read-Only Inventory Collector for Package, Extension, and Dev-Tool Metadata on Developer Endpoints
    summary

    TL;DR: Bumblebee from Perplexity is a read-only Go binary that inventories packages, extensions and MCP configs on developer machines to answer “which laptops have this compromised package right now?”

    • Problem: SBOMs show what shipped and EDR shows what ran, but neither covers messy local state across lockfiles, extension manifests and dev-tool configs.
    • How it works: Parses on-disk metadata only and never runs npm ls, pip show or go list; output is NDJSON package, finding and scan_summary records.
    • Coverage: npm (lockfiles, pnpm, Yarn, Bun), PyPI, Go, RubyGems, Composer, Homebrew, MCP configs, agent skills, VS Code-family and browser extensions.
    • Secrets handling: Parses MCP configs like ~/.claude.json for server inventory but does not emit credential values from env blocks.
    • Profiles: baseline for recurring light scans, project for dev dirs like ~/code, and deep for incident sweeps of explicit roots including $HOME.
    • Matching: An --exposure-catalog JSON matches exact (ecosystem, name, version) tuples or *; maintained catalogs live in threat_intel/.
    • Setup: go install github.com/perplexityai/bumblebee/cmd/bumblebee@v0.1.1, then bumblebee scan --profile baseline > inventory.ndjson; requires Go 1.25+.
    • Limits: No version ranges; Codex config.toml and Continue YAML not parsed; loose SKILL.md dirs and bun.lockb contents invisible; macOS and Linux only.
    • Caveats: low confidence records are references, not proof of install, and deduplication and current-state logic are left to the receiver.

    Takeaway: Schedule a baseline scan via MDM or launchd and ship NDJSON to your SIEM, so the next npm or PyPI compromise becomes one catalog query instead of a fleet-wide scramble.

  • Securing Your Gemini and Google API Keys
    summary

    TL;DR: Google Cloud guide to securing Gemini and other Google API keys, which are bearer credentials tied to no identity, covering isolation, restrictions, storage, inventory and abuse detection.

    • Core risk: A Gemini key is a standard Google API key; anyone holding it can use it, and abuse is billed to the owner.
    • Isolation: Create keys in a standalone project with no other purpose to limit blast radius, via the console or gcloud services api-keys create.
    • API restrictions: Allow only needed APIs, such as “Gemini API” alone for AI Studio; Firebase-generated keys are restricted to 24 APIs, including Cloud SQL Admin.
    • Application restrictions: Limit by website URL, IP or subnet, iOS Bundle ID or Android package plus cert fingerprint; one type per key, so use separate keys.
    • Storage: Keep keys in Secret Manager and inject into Cloud Run or GKE, or read at runtime; verify third-party apps send keys only over TLS.
    • Incident response: Delete a compromised key immediately; undelete restores it within 30 days if it was a false alarm.
    • Inventory: Use Asset Inventory filtered on apikeys.Key, gcloud services api-keys list, or gcloud asset search-all-resources across the org.
    • Abuse detection: Query serviceruntime.googleapis.com/api/request_count filtered by credential_id label; abnormally high counts signal misuse.
    • Hygiene: Delete unused keys, enforce org policy custom constraints on apikeys.googleapis.com/Key, and rotate by updating all consumers before deleting the old key.

    Takeaway: Run the org-wide asset search now, restrict every key to specific APIs and clients, and add a per-credential_id request-count alert to catch stolen keys early.

  • Operationalizing AWS Security: A Maturity Roadmap
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS Security Blog roadmap with six phases for turning enabled Security Hub and GuardDuty into an operated practice with tuned findings, routing, safe auto-remediation and measured cadence.

    • Thesis: Enabling tools is not operating them; the gap is an operational playbook, not more feature docs.
    • Phase 0, assess (1-2 weeks): Count findings by severity and age, baseline FSBP and CIS scores, and check coverage in every account and Region.
    • Phase 1, reduce noise (2-3 weeks): Add GuardDuty suppression rules, disable irrelevant Security Hub controls, pick one standard (FSBP default) and use workflow status.
    • Phase 2, routing: Security Hub to EventBridge with severity tiers: CRITICAL pages in 15 minutes, HIGH 4 hours, MEDIUM ticket in 48 hours; enrich alerts via Lambda.
    • Phase 3, remediation: Automate only high-confidence, deterministic, low-blast-radius responses (instance isolation, key revocation, re-enabling S3 Block Public Access) using SHARR on Step Functions.
    • Prevention: For recurring findings, an SCP that blocks the misconfiguration beats repeated auto-remediation.
    • Phase 4, rhythm: 30-minute weekly review, predefined escalations, monthly MTTA, MTTR and SLA metrics (above 95% CRITICAL, 90% HIGH), and quarterly suppression audits.
    • Phase 5, expand: Add Inspector, Macie, Security Lake (OCSF), Access Analyzer, CloudTrail Lake and SCPs blocking GuardDuty or CloudTrail disablement.
    • Caveats: Prescriptive with no case studies or cost data; timelines and thresholds are suggestions, not validated benchmarks.

    Takeaway: Do the Phase 0 assessment and noise reduction before building any automation, then stand up a weekly 30-minute review that survives staff turnover.

  • GLM-5.2, Not Mythos, Is the Real Security Emergency
    summary

    TL;DR: Joshua Saxe argues that open-weights GLM-5.2, not Anthropic’s withheld Mythos, is the real security emergency, because attackers can now run agentic AI privately and unmonitored.

    • Panopticon dilemma: Attackers on closed frontier APIs risk their tactics, targets and intent being logged, which the author says limited agentic AI abuse for nine months.
    • What changed: GLM-5.2 is described as the first open-weights model widely seen as capable of long-horizon agency, reportedly near GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.8 on code and terminal tasks.
    • Hardware: Runs on 8 H200 GPUs, with quantized or pruned versions expected to cut the cost further.
    • Safeguards: The author argues fine-tuning away refusals should be “trivial,” and expects dark markets for hosted near-frontier models and harnesses.
    • Threat capabilities: Semi-autonomous kill chains, rapid implant and C2 authoring, zero-day discovery and exploit development, and long-con fraud in local languages.
    • Mythos critique: Withholding Mythos from defenders is called counterproductive since it runs on monitored servers and defenders patch faster than attackers exploit.
    • Recommendation: Shift policy from denying frontier access to accelerating AI adoption in CISO teams and giving security vendors the best models.
    • Caveats: Evidence is mostly qualitative with no benchmark scores; a commenter notes GLM-5.2 lacks image input and very large models remain hard to deploy.

    Takeaway: Assume attackers have unmonitored agentic AI now, and use AI yourself to pay down security debt and speed up detection and response.

  • Some Notes on Lambda MicroVMs
    summary

    TL;DR: Aidan Steele’s hands-on notes on AWS Lambda MicroVMs, covering shells, Docker, networking, image builds, timings and the rough edges he found.

    • Positioning: MicroVMs sit between containers and VMs, suited to CI runners, coding-agent hosts, game servers and per-environment addressability.
    • Shell access: /dev/ptmx exists, so ptys work, and CreateMicrovmShellAuthToken with websocat gives a direct shell, no reverse shell needed.
    • Docker: Works inside a MicroVM after pointing dockerd at the right socket with dockerd --containerd=/run/containerd/containerd.sock.
    • Networking: Outbound UDP blocked by default; containers need --dns 169.254.169.253; VPC access uses “network connectors” with their own ARN and lifecycle.
    • Hidden ENIs: Connector ENIs only appear with DescribeNetworkInterfaces and IncludeManagedResources=true; the old Elastic IP on Lambda ENI trick is now blocked.
    • Images: Lambda builds from a Dockerfile ZIP in S3, not ECR despite docs, for Graviton 3 and 4, in 2-3 minutes, then snapshots memory and disk.
    • Timings: RunMicrovm to RUNNING about 2 seconds, plus about 2 more to HTTP 200 OK; suspend and resume about 1 second each.
    • Inbound: Always authenticated via X-aws-proxy-auth bearer token from CreateMicrovmAuthToken; supports HTTP/2, gRPC, WebSockets and SSE.
    • Rough edges: Lifecycle hooks only arrive on port 9000; no session tags passed; sts:GetWebIdentityToken lacks MicroVM-identifying claims; free build disk about 7.2 GB.

    Takeaway: If you need isolated per-tenant sandboxes, prototype on MicroVMs but reseed randomness in lifecycle hooks after snapshot restore, and do not rely on workload identity claims yet.

  • Run Isolated Sandboxes with Full Lifecycle Control: AWS Lambda Introduces MicroVMs
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS launch post for Lambda MicroVMs, Firecracker-isolated stateful sandboxes with their own API for running user- or AI-generated code, with suspend and resume lifecycle control.

    • Problem: Multi-tenant apps like coding assistants and scanners need per-user environments; VMs start slowly, containers share a kernel and Functions lack long-lived state.
    • Isolation: Each MicroVM is its own Firecracker VM with no shared kernel between users.
    • Image model: Supply a Dockerfile and code ZIP in S3; Lambda builds, initializes and snapshots memory and disk so new MicroVMs resume pre-initialized.
    • Commands: aws lambda-microvms create-microvm-image then aws lambda-microvms run-microvm with an --idle-policy such as maxIdleDurationSeconds 900.
    • Lifecycle: Running VMs keep memory, disk and processes; idle VMs suspend with state snapshotted and auto-resume on the next request.
    • Access: Each MicroVM gets a dedicated endpoint URL; requests need a short-lived token in the X-aws-proxy-auth header.
    • Limits: Up to 8 hours total runtime, 16 vCPUs, 32 GB memory and 32 GB disk; ARM64 only.
    • Regions: us-east-1, us-east-2, us-west-2, eu-west-1 and ap-northeast-1 at launch; pricing not stated in the post.
    • Caveats: Snapshot-based start means apps generating unique values or opening connections at init must use provided hooks.

    Takeaway: Consider MicroVMs instead of self-managed Firecracker or container sandboxes for untrusted code execution, but plan around the 8-hour cap and ARM64-only images.

  • CloudTrail in CloudWatch Isn’t Very Good
    summary

    TL;DR: Aidan Steele finds CloudTrail ingestion into CloudWatch, AWS’s recommended CloudTrail Lake replacement, hard to set up, under-documented, slow to start and missing Lake’s enrichment.

    • Context: AWS closed CloudTrail Lake to new customers from June 1, 2026 and recommends migrating to CloudWatch.
    • Capability gap: Despite AWS claims of parity, CloudWatch lacks Lake’s enrichment with resource tags and global condition keys.
    • Setup pain: The new “service-linked channel” method is barely documented; searches mostly return the older trail-based approach.
    • Org quirks: Requires StartTelemetryEvaluationForOrganization; org-level rules skip the management account, which needs its own account-level rule.
    • More steps: Centralisation needs a separate log centralisation rule and service-linked role, and S3 Tables integration must be enabled manually.
    • Slow start: Events took about 8 hours to flow after enabling, with docs allowing up to 24 hours, versus minutes for trails.
    • Bugs: UpdateTelemetryRuleForOrganization returned InternalServerException on two separate days when reducing retention.
    • Effort and cost: Setup took a few hours and about $50 of AI credits; per-event trail pricing versus per-GB CloudWatch pricing remains unresolved.
    • Caveats: Tested on one tiny personal org; Terraform published at aidansteele/cloudtrail-cloudwatch-2026 was AI-generated.

    Takeaway: Do not decommission trails yet; if migrating off CloudTrail Lake, budget time for org rules, management-account coverage and a day of ingestion lag.

  • An Introduction to AI Coding Agent Security
    summary

    TL;DR: NCC Group whitepaper by Alex Plaskett mapping the security boundaries of Claude Code, Cursor and Codex, and the vulnerability classes previously found and fixed in each.

    • Scope: Local CLI and GUI agents as of May 2026; excludes MCP servers, plugins, cloud agents and direct API use; focus is code execution, not exfiltration.
    • Two controls: Permission models (allow, ask, deny; human-in-the-loop) and OS sandboxes (macOS Seatbelt, Linux bubblewrap); prompt fatigue drove sandbox and auto-mode adoption.
    • Defaults differ: Claude Code CLI defaults to no sandbox; Cursor GUI sandboxes by default but has workspace trust disabled and allows workspace write and execute without prompts.
    • Untrusted workspace: Code running before trust is a bug, such as Claude Code before 2.1.90 executing a repo-planted reg.exe, or planted .claude/settings.json and .cursor/mcp.json.
    • Trusted workspace classes: Sandbox escapes via symlinks, settings or .git hooks; permission bypasses via “read-only” commands like sed, find, rg and zsh clobber; sensitive file overwrites.
    • Parser differentials: Validators using tree-sitter or AST parsing can disagree with execution, such as Codex PowerShell git log --% HEAD --output=codex_poc.txt writing a file.
    • Network: Cursor’s default trusted hosts include user-content domains like githubusercontent.com, usable for payload delivery and exfiltration.
    • Delivery: Obfuscation raises LLM refusals; payloads disguised as build steps or native binaries succeeded, including a reverse shell.
    • Core lesson: LLM refusals and guardrails are not a security boundary; the harness must assume the model will emit dangerous tool calls.

    Takeaway: Enable the agent sandbox, keep auto-allow rules narrow, and review .claude, .cursor and .git config in cloned repos before granting workspace trust.

  • GOModel: Lightweight AI Gateway in Go with Unified OpenAI-Compatible API for Multiple LLM Providers
    summary

    TL;DR: GOModel is an MIT-licensed AI gateway in Go exposing OpenAI- and Anthropic-compatible endpoints in front of many LLM providers, with caching, budgets, failover and observability.

    • Positioning: Pitched as a LiteLLM and Portkey alternative, citing a recent LiteLLM hack and Portkey no longer being maintained on GitHub.
    • Endpoints: OpenAI-compatible at /v1 and Anthropic-compatible at /v1/messages, so official SDKs work by changing the base URL.
    • Cost controls: Exact and semantic caching, per-request cost estimates, hard budgets per user, team or key, and request, token and concurrency limits.
    • Routing: Virtual models with round-robin or cost-based balancing, session pinning to keep prompt caches warm, failover with retries and circuit breakers.
    • Governance: Hierarchical user paths, per-key model allowlists, guardrails, audit logs, an MCP gateway and passthrough at /p/{provider}/....
    • Providers: OpenAI, Anthropic, Gemini, Vertex AI, Bedrock, Azure OpenAI, Groq, DeepSeek, OpenRouter and others, plus Ollama, vLLM and SGLang.
    • Setup: docker run --rm -p 8080:8080 -v gomodel_data:/app/data enterpilot/gomodel, then add providers at /admin/dashboard.
    • Observability: Prometheus metrics, OpenTelemetry traces and live request streaming.
    • Caveats: Speed claims ship without figures in the README; prompt compression, smart routing and OIDC SSO are paid Pro features; .so plugins run arbitrary compiled code.

    Takeaway: Worth evaluating as a self-hosted LLM egress point for key isolation, budgets and audit, but benchmark it yourself and treat .so plugin loading as a supply-chain risk.

  • PgQue: Zero-Bloat PostgreSQL Queue Built on PgQ Architecture as Pure SQL
    summary

    TL;DR: PgQue reimplements Skype’s PgQ queue in pure SQL and PL/pgSQL so a zero-bloat, Kafka-style event log runs on any Postgres 14+, including RDS, Aurora and Supabase.

    • Problem: SKIP LOCKED queues using DELETE or UPDATE create dead tuples, VACUUM pressure and index bloat that degrade under sustained load.
    • How it works: Snapshot-based batching per tick plus TRUNCATE-based table rotation, so the hot path produces no dead tuples.
    • Model: Shared log with independent per-consumer cursors; every subscriber gets every event, with retry, delayed and dead-letter tables.
    • Install: One file via psql --single-transaction -d mydb -f sql/pgque.sql; no C extension, daemon or shared_preload_libraries.
    • Ticking: select pgque.start(); uses pg_cron to tick every 100 ms; pg_timetable or a manual pgque.ticker() loop also work.
    • Numbers: Default tick gives about 52 ms median delivery, about 8 ms at a 10 ms tick; with a pinned xmin, SKIP LOCKED saw 14x dead tuples and 35% throughput loss while PgQue stayed at n_dead_tup = 0.
    • Security limit: Roles pgque_reader, pgque_writer and pgque_admin are global, so any reader can ack or read other consumers’ batches.
    • Ops caveats: No ticker means no delivery; skipping maint_retry_events() means no redelivery; cron.job_run_details grows unbounded without a purge.
    • Not for: Per-job priority, unique jobs, cron scheduling or sub-3 ms dispatch; cooperative consumers are experimental in 0.2.

    Takeaway: Consider PgQue for event fan-out on managed Postgres where queue bloat hurts, but monitor the ticker and isolate mutually untrusted apps in separate databases.

    summary

    TL;DR: Oblique’s CEO describes a $0 startup security stack built from free tiers and open source, mapped to SOC 2 controls, arguing serious security need not be expensive.

    • Secrets: TruffleHog first ran on every commit and now runs daily for regressions; it has found no leaked secrets.

    summary

    TL;DR: Zvi Mowshowitz analyzes Claude Mythos’s cyber capabilities and Project Glasswing, Anthropic’s limited release to defenders, arguing withholding it was necessary as AI finds and exploits zero-days at scale.

    • Glasswing: Access for launch partners plus 40+ critical-software organizations, backed by $100M in usage credits and $4M in donations; a progress report follows at 90 days.
    • Claim: Anthropic says Mythos Preview found thousands of zero-days across every major OS and browser, many 10-20 years old.

    summary

  • How Amazon Uses Agentic AI for Vulnerability Detection at Global Scale
    summary

    TL;DR: Amazon describes RuleForge, an agentic AI pipeline that turns public exploit PoCs into validated detection rules, producing them 336% faster than manual work with human review kept.

    • Problem: NVD published over 48,000 CVEs in 2025, and hand-writing, tuning and reviewing a detection rule per CVE forced heavy prioritization.
    • Ingestion: Downloads public PoC exploit code and scores each exploit using content analysis and threat intelligence.
    • Generation: An agent on AWS Fargate with Amazon Bedrock proposes five parallel candidate JSON rules, each refined iteratively.
    • Judge model: A separate model scores sensitivity (missed malicious requests) and specificity (keying on surface features rather than the vulnerability).
    • Key lesson: The generator rated nearly all its own rules as good; separating the judge cut false positives 67% at the same true-positive count.
    • Prompting: Asking how likely a rule is to fail calibrated better than asking whether it works; domain-specific questions beat generic confidence ratings.
    • Validation: Synthetic malicious and benign tests, then replay against MadPot honeypot traffic and Sonaris detections, with failures fed back to the generator.
    • Human gate: Security engineers review the best candidate and make the final deployment call.
    • Caveats: Self-reported; judge reasoning matched experts on only 6 of 9 rules; no absolute precision or recall; coverage depends on public PoCs.

    Takeaway: When using LLMs to write detections or policy, separate generator from judge, phrase evaluation around failure, and test against real traffic before human sign-off.

  • Orchestrating AI Code Review at Scale with Cloudflare
    summary

    TL;DR: Cloudflare built an AI code reviewer that runs in CI on OpenCode. A coordinator agent manages up to seven specialist reviewers and reviewed 48,095 merge requests in one month, at a median cost of $0.98.

    • Problem: Sending a whole diff to one LLM gave vague, hallucinated feedback. Off-the-shelf tools were not flexible enough.
    • How it works: A coordinator OpenCode process exposes a spawn_reviewers tool. It launches specialists for security, performance, code quality, docs, release, compliance and AGENTS.md, then deduplicates and filters their findings.
    • Risk tiers: Each MR is classed as trivial, lite or full by lines changed, file count and security-sensitive paths. Trivial MRs get two agents and a cheaper coordinator.
    • Prompting: Each specialist prompt has explicit “What NOT to Flag” sections, which the authors say matter more than the inclusions. Findings come out as XML with critical, warning or suggestion severity.
    • Resilience: Each model tier has its own circuit breaker, with failback to older models in the same family. A Workers KV control plane can reroute running jobs within five seconds.
    • Security: Boundary tags are stripped from MR bodies and comments to block prompt injection. A human “break glass” comment forces approval and is logged in telemetry.
    • Numbers: 131,246 runs across 5,169 repos between March 10 and April 9, 2026. Median 3m 39s, P99 $4.45, about 120B tokens, 85.7% cache hit rate.
    • Limits: It is not a replacement for human review. It misses design rationale, cross-system contract breaks and concurrency bugs, and its accuracy figures are self-reported.

    Takeaway: Use narrow specialist prompts with explicit exclusions, risk-based tiers and a judge pass, not one generic prompt. Strip injection boundaries from user-controlled MR text, and keep a human override that is audited.

  • My Claude Code Setup (2026 Edition)
    summary

    TL;DR: Marco Lancini walks through his 2026 Claude Code setup in a monorepo. It combines sandboxing, blocking hooks, a deny list for credential paths, review subagents and a plugin-driven plan-then-code workflow.

    • Settings: He sets effortLevel to xhigh and CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_ADAPTIVE_THINKING=1. He caps CLAUDE_CODE_AUTO_COMPACT_WINDOW at 400000 because quality dropped near the full 1M context.

    • Caveats: This is a personal setup, not a recipe. The allow list grew from his own approvals, agent teams are experimental, and the sound and popup hooks only work on macOS.

    Takeaway: Copy the guardrail pattern. Turn on the sandbox, deny reads of credential directories, block destructive and IaC-apply commands with exit-2 hooks, and route tool calls through one audited wrapper CLI.

  • RedAI: AI-Powered Security Vulnerability Discovery and Live Validation Tool
    summary

    TL;DR: RedAI is a TypeScript/Bun terminal workbench where AI agents find candidate vulnerabilities in source code. Validator agents then try to confirm each one against a running instance of the target.

    • How it works: A scan runs in three phases: discover (threat model, file prioritization, analysis units, scanner agents), validate (live tests) and report.
    • Validation: A validator plans and runs tests by clicking UI, sending requests, writing PoCs and capturing screenshots. Each finding ends as confirmed, unable-to-test or disproved.
    • Environments: The bundled plugins are a Browser validator (real Chrome via agent-browser) and an iOS Simulator validator (via xcrun simctl). Custom ones implement src/validators/validator-plugin.ts.
    • Scanners: It uses Claude Code (ANTHROPIC_API_KEY, Bedrock or Vertex) or Codex (OPENAI_API_KEY or CODEX_API_KEY). It reads .env from the working directory.

    • Setup: Install with bun install -g @kpolley/redai, then run redai. It needs Bun 1.2 or later, and validators need their skill directory in .agents/skills/ inside the target workspace.
    • Demo: examples/webapp is a deliberately vulnerable app on localhost:3000, with an iOS counterpart in examples/iosapp.
    • Caveats: Use it only on systems you are authorized to test. Findings may be wrong, environments need manual login and setup, and ~/.redai/ may hold credentials and transcripts.

    Takeaway: Try RedAI on internal apps to cut false positives from AI scanners through live PoC validation. Run it only in isolated test environments, and treat ~/.redai/ as sensitive data.

    summary

  • Practical Package Security: The Unofficial Guide
    summary

    TL;DR: Wiz gives a practical guide to defending against package supply-chain compromises like Axios and trivy-action. It uses cooldowns, lockfiles, install-script blocking, registry proxies and canary credentials to cut exposure and blast radius.

    • Pattern: An attacker takes over a maintainer account or build system and pushes a malicious version. Hours to days of exposure is enough, as with Axios 1.14.1/0.30.4, live about 3 hours with about 100M monthly downloads.
    • Incidents: TeamPCP force-pushed 75 of 76 trivy-action tags to steal CI secrets, which then led to PyPI compromises including LiteLLM.
    • Cooldowns: Delay new versions with Renovate minimum release age, Dependabot minimum package age, pnpm update delays or uv exclude-newer. Wiz claims seven days would have stopped 90+% of recent attacks.
    • Lockfiles: Use npm ci in CI, because npm install can rewrite the lockfile. Lockfile adoption is about 50% in npm and about 99.7% in Go.
    • Install scripts: Disable them with npm ignore-scripts or pnpm onlyBuiltDependencies. Roll out by denying by default and allowlisting exceptions.
    • Environments: Run installs in remote dev environments, away from laptop credentials. Aim for zero-touch production with no standing developer prod credentials, and use isolated browsers against cookie theft.
    • Registries: Start with a pull-through proxy (Artifactory, Nexus, CodeArtifact) for visibility. Then move critical paths to curated registries.
    • Detection: Plant canary credentials in CI environment variables. At Grafana, an AWS key canary exposed a compromised Action within minutes.
    • Caveats: Cooldowns delay security patches, and wrapper scanners rely on lagging OSSF malware data. Lockfiles do not control transitive dependencies in common ecosystems.

    Takeaway: Add a 7-day cooldown in Renovate or Dependabot and switch CI to npm ci. Disable install scripts by default, and plant AWS canary tokens in CI secrets today.

  • CloudSec Tidbits: The Danger of Multi-SSO AWS Cognito User Pools
    summary

    TL;DR: Doyensec shows how multi-tenant SaaS apps that let tenants add their own OIDC/SAML IdPs to one AWS Cognito User Pool can suffer ghost identities, account takeover and login-routing hijacks.

    • Setup at risk: One Cognito User Pool where tenants self-register external IdPs through CreateIdentityProvider, with identity logic in Lambda triggers.
    • Ghost identities: If PreSignUp_ExternalProvider lacks a domain check, Cognito saves the attacker’s user. A later rejection in PostConfirmation leaves the record, which can then be used through password reset or impersonation.
    • Trigger gaps: First federated login and later logins run different trigger chains that share only TokenGeneration. Checks placed only on PreAuthentication_Authentication miss first login.
    • Sub-splitting: Federated usernames have the form <ProviderName>_<sub>. ProviderName cannot contain _, but sub can, so a guard and a JIT consumer can parse the same username differently.
    • Homoglyphs: Provider names with Cyrillic lookalike letters pass Cognito’s byte-level uniqueness check but look identical in the Hosted UI and logs.
    • Routing hijack: Cognito does not verify ownership of IdpIdentifiers. An attacker IdP can claim an unclaimed domain such as gmail.com and receive every login for that domain.
    • Attribute abuse: AttributeMapping values from a malicious IdP are attacker-controlled. WriteAttributes does not stop Lambdas calling AdminUpdateUserAttributes on custom:tenantID or custom:role.
    • Fixes: Branch PreSignUp on triggerSource, and parse usernames everywhere with one helper using maxsplit=1. Derive tenant and role server-side, and require domain-ownership proof before accepting an IdpIdentifiers claim.
    • Caveats: These are attack patterns, with no CVE or named vendor. Exposure depends on each platform’s trigger logic, and a Terraform lab plus the maSSO malicious-IdP tool are provided.

    Takeaway: If you run self-service SSO on Cognito, audit every Lambda triggerSource path. Treat all IdP claims, including sub, as untrusted input, and never let tenants claim IdpIdentifiers domains they have not verified.

  • How to Harden GitHub Actions: An Updated Guide
    summary

    TL;DR: Wiz updated its GitHub Actions hardening cheat sheet for 2026 after the tj-actions, trivy-action and Axios incidents. It covers org settings, secrets, workflow authoring, third-party actions and runners.

    • Threat pattern: A compromised workflow or action leaks credentials, and the attacker then moves laterally. In tj-actions (March 2025), a vulnerable workflow leaked PATs that were used to poison actions across thousands of repos.
    • Org settings: Make the default GITHUB_TOKEN read-only, since orgs created before February 2023 may still default to read-write. Turn on SHA pinning enforcement (August 2025) and block actions during incidents with a ! prefix.
    • PPE vectors: Pwn Requests through pull_request_target or workflow_run, script injection from issue titles or branch names into run:, GITHUB_ENV/GITHUB_PATH abuse (for example LD_PRELOAD), and living-off-the-pipeline tools.
    • Workflow authoring: Set permissions: {} at the top and grant per job. Use persist-credentials: false on actions/checkout, and pass untrusted values through env, never inline interpolation.
    • Secrets: Scope secrets to step-level env. Avoid toJson(secrets) and secrets: inherit, prefer OIDC, and keep release credentials in GitHub Apps.
    • Third-party actions: Pin to commit SHA, because tags can be force-pushed. Add a 7-14 day cooldown with pinact --min-age 7 or Renovate minimumReleaseAge.
    • Branch protection: Dismiss stale approvals on new commits, and require approval from someone other than the last pusher to stop PR hijacking.
    • Runners: Never attach self-hosted runners to public repos. Use ephemeral runners with default-deny egress.
    • Tools: zizmor for static analysis, octoscan, OpenSSF allstar, and trajan/Gato-X for self-testing. GitHub’s roadmap adds a workflow dependencies: lockfile that pins transitive actions.

    Takeaway: Run zizmor across all workflows and enforce SHA pinning at org level. Set read-only default tokens and permissions: {}, then replace long-lived cloud secrets with OIDC.

  • The Insecurity Landscape of AI-Powered GitHub Actions
    summary

    TL;DR: Wiz found bypasses of the access gates in Anthropic, OpenAI and Google AI GitHub Actions, plus ways to leak credentials from them. Its rule is that whoever controls the prompt input will achieve injection, so limit both input and blast radius.

    • Thesis: AI actions take free-form input and give nondeterministic output, and prompt injection has no complete fix. Security depends on who can supply input and what the agent can reach.
    • Bot gate bypass: anthropics/claude-code-action and openai/codex-action trust any actor whose name ends in [bot]. With allowed_bots: "*", any attacker-created GitHub App can trigger them.
    • Dangling apps: With a named bot allowlist, an attacker can register an unclaimed app with the trusted name. Wiz compares this to dangling domains.
    • Dependabot confused deputy: @dependabot recreate or @dependabot show makes Dependabot the github.actor. That bypasses allowlists that trust Dependabot.
    • Credential files: Auth actions such as google-github-actions/auth@v2 write GCP, AWS, Azure, Docker or SSH credential files. In a demo, an issue prompt got Gemini to print GCP credentials into the workflow log.
    • Verbose leaks: show_full_output: true in claude-code-action and gemini_debug: true in run-gemini-cli expose every file the agent reads in public logs.
    • Numbers: claude-code-action appears in 12K+ public workflows and run-gemini-cli in 1K+. Misconfigured repos total 200,000+ combined stars.
    • Vendor response: OpenAI added allow-bot-users and stopped trusting Dependabot. Anthropic updated its docs. claude-code-security-review, qodo-ai/pr-agent and coderabbitai/ai-pr-reviewer have no built-in access control.
    • Fixes: Drop allow-bots/allowed_bots wildcards and check author association (OWNER, MEMBER, COLLABORATOR) on comment triggers. Never pair AI actions with credential-generating auth steps or debug output in public repos.

    Takeaway: Search your org for AI actions that use bot wildcards, comment triggers without author-association checks, or cloud auth steps in the same job. Lock those down before an issue comment exfiltrates credentials.

  • distil AI Slop Detector: Chrome Extension That Detects AI-Generated Text Locally
    summary

    TL;DR: Distil Labs’ AI Slop Detector is a Chrome extension that runs a fine-tuned Gemma 3 270M model locally via WebAssembly. It labels pasted text ai_generated or human_written without sending it anywhere.

    • Model: Gemma 3 270M with a LoRA fine-tune for 4 epochs, quantized to Q4_K_M at about 242 MB. The model downloads from HuggingFace on first use.
    • Training: GPT OSS 120B acted as teacher. About 50 hand-written seeds were expanded to about 10,000 synthetic examples, based on a Kaggle AI-generated essays dataset.
    • Runtime: Runs on CPU through Wllama (WebAssembly) in an offscreen document. Each query takes about 0.5-2 seconds on consumer CPUs.
    • Accuracy: The tuned model scored 100%, the Q4 quantized model about 95% (19/20 on the extended suite), and the base model about 40%.
    • Real-world samples: ChatGPT outputs 98% (50 samples), human tweets 94% (50), Reddit comments about 92% (100+), formal emails 88% (30).
    • Size trade-off: Full precision is about 1.1 GB at 100%. Q4_K_M cuts size by about 78% for about 95% accuracy.
    • Setup: Load the unzipped repo unpacked at chrome://extensions/ in Developer mode. You can train your own with the Distil CLI and the distil-cli-skill Claude skill.
    • Limits: About 1 in 20 predictions may be wrong, and formal human writing gets false positives. Test sets are tiny (20 examples), and “slop” is defined by one dataset.

    Takeaway: Use this as a reference pattern for private, on-device small-model classifiers distilled from a large teacher. Do not use its verdicts to make decisions about people.

  • below: Interactive Tool to View and Record Historical Linux System Data
    summary

    TL;DR: below is Meta’s Apache-2.0 Linux resource monitor, a cgroup2-aware alternative to atop. It can show live data, record history continuously, and replay what the system looked like at a past point in time.

    • What it is: An interactive tool to view and record historical system data. Its name reflects rejecting many of atop’s design choices.
    • Data: Hardware utilization, the cgroup hierarchy, per-cgroup and per-process stats, and Pressure Stall Information (PSI).

    Takeaway: Enable the below recording service on cgroup2 hosts. After an incident, below replay shows which cgroup caused CPU, memory or IO pressure at the moment it happened.

  • boring: Lightweight CLI Tool for Managing SSH Tunnels
    summary

    TL;DR: boring is a small MIT-licensed CLI for managing SSH tunnels. It defines local, remote and SOCKS5 forwards in a TOML file and opens or closes them by name, glob or group, with automatic reconnection.

    • Features: Local, remote, socks and socks-remote forwarding, Unix sockets, keep-alives and auto-reconnect. It works with ~/.ssh/config and ssh-agent.
    • Config path: ~/.boring.toml on macOS and Windows, $XDG_CONFIG_HOME/boring/.boring.toml on Linux. $BORING_CONFIG overrides both.
    • Format: Each tunnel is a [[tunnels]] table with name, local, remote, host, mode and optional user, identity, port, group.
    • Port ranges: local and remote accept lists and ranges such as 8000-8010,8080, paired by position.
    • Keep-alive: keep_alive defaults to 120 seconds and can be set globally or per tunnel.
    • Commands: boring list, boring open <patterns> (or -a for all, -g for a group), boring close and boring edit.
    • Daemon: Logs go to /tmp/boringd.log and the control socket is /tmp/boringd.sock. Override them with $BORING_LOG_FILE and $BORING_SOCK, and set $DEBUG for verbose logs.
    • Install: brew install boring, a release binary, or make from source. Windows needs a source build with Go 1.25+ via build_win.bat.

    Takeaway: Replace ad-hoc ssh -L commands for bastion and database access with grouped boring tunnels. This keeps forwards reproducible and auto-reconnecting while reusing your existing SSH config and agent.

  • Trail of Bits Claude Code Configuration Guide and Sandboxing Best Practices
    summary

    TL;DR: Trail of Bits’ opinionated Claude Code setup covering sandboxing, deny rules, blocking hooks, MCP, local models and workflows, installable via /trailofbits:config.

    • Settings: Sets DISABLE_TELEMETRY, DISABLE_ERROR_REPORTING, enableAllProjectMcpServers: false and cleanupPeriodDays: 365; avoids CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_NONESSENTIAL_TRAFFIC because it kills auto-updates.
    • Sandboxing: Use /sandbox (Seatbelt on macOS, bubblewrap on Linux) plus Read/Edit deny rules for ~/.aws, ~/.kube, SSH/GPG keys, registry tokens, keychain.
    • Why both: Deny rules alone cover only built-in tools; with /sandbox they also apply to Bash at the OS level.
    • Hooks: Two PreToolUse Bash hooks block rm -rf (suggest trash) and direct pushes to main/master, exiting with code 2.
    • Caveat: Hooks are guardrails, not a security boundary; prompt injection can route around them.
    • Rules: Path-scoped ~/.claude/rules/*.md are not re-injected after auto-compaction, so must-hold rules go in CLAUDE.md or hooks.
    • Worktree gotcha: A blocked worktree helper can make the agent silently fall back to git checkout -b in the shared directory.
    • Local models: LM Studio with Qwen3-Coder-Next (about 45GB, MLX 4-bit) needs 64GB+ unified memory, context at least 25K.
    • Usage: Prefer /clear over /compact, rewind after two failed corrections, and enable /fast (2x cost) only at session start.

    Takeaway: Copy the deny rules, sandbox config and blocking hooks into ~/.claude/settings.json, and only run --dangerously-skip-permissions when both sandbox and deny rules are active.

  • Sandboxed Devcontainer for Safely Running Claude Code with Unrestricted Permissions
    summary

    TL;DR: Trail of Bits devcontainer that runs Claude Code in bypassPermissions mode inside a disposable container, limiting it to the project directory for audits and untrusted repo review.

    • Problem: bypassPermissions on the host lets Claude run any command unconfirmed; the container confines it to /workspace and disposable state.
    • Setup: Needs Docker Desktop, OrbStack or Colima plus @devcontainers/cli; clone to ~/.claude-devcontainer and run install.sh self-install.
    • Commands: devc ., up, down, rebuild, destroy, shell, exec, mount, cp, and sync to copy session logs for /insights.
    • Patterns: Per-project containers for one-off reviews, or a shared workspace with several repos for client engagements.
    • Network: Full egress by default; optional iptables/ipset allowlist for api.anthropic.com, GitHub, npm and PyPI, dropping the rest.
    • Network limits: Per-IP filtering leaks across shared CDNs, DNS stays open, IPv6 is unfiltered, rules vanish on restart.
    • Not protected: Container escape, files planted in shared .git for later host execution, VS Code “Reopen in Container” RPC, rule changes via NET_ADMIN and sudo.
    • Exposure: Forwarded SSH agent lets container code authenticate as you; ~/.gitconfig mounted read-only; Docker socket not mounted.
    • Image: Ubuntu 24.04, Node.js 24, Python 3.13 with uv, user vscode with passwordless sudo.

    Takeaway: Use devc for untrusted repos from the terminal, not VS Code, enable the egress allowlist, and avoid forwarding your SSH agent or mounting $HOME.

  • load-secrets-action: Load Secrets from 1Password into GitHub Actions
    summary

    TL;DR: Official 1Password GitHub Action that loads secrets from 1Password vaults into workflow jobs as step outputs or environment variables, with log masking.

    • How it works: Declare env vars whose values are op://vault/item/field references; the action resolves them at runtime.
    • Auth: service account: Set OP_SERVICE_ACCOUNT_TOKEN from a GitHub secret.
    • Auth: Connect: 1Password Connect is supported as an alternative backend.
    • Auth: Workload Identity: Public preview; exchanges the job’s OIDC token for short-lived access using OP_WORKLOAD_ID, OP_ENVIRONMENT_ID, OP_INTEGRATION_KEY and id-token: write.
    • Workload Identity scope: Loads every variable in the configured 1Password environment, so scope that environment tightly.
    • Output modes: Default exposes secrets as step outputs via steps.<id>.outputs.<NAME>; export-env: true exports them as env vars.
    • Extras: OP_ENV_FILE points to a .env template; ?ssh-format=openssh selects SSH key format.
    • Masking: Loaded values are masked in job logs.
    • Caveats: Mixing Workload Identity with service account or Connect variables, or setting only some of its three, causes a config error.

    Takeaway: Prefer step outputs over export-env to limit secret exposure to later steps, and evaluate Workload Identity to drop long-lived service account tokens.

  • Infrastructure Agents Guide: How to Design, Build, and Operate AI Agents for Infrastructure Teams
    summary

    TL;DR: Cloudgeni’s open design guide for building and running AI agents that change infrastructure safely, covering runtime, sandboxing, credentials, GitOps, guardrails and audit across 13 chapters.

    • Problem: Agents that can terraform apply can also destroy, agents reading configs can leak secrets, and looping agents run up cost.
    • Core rule: Agents produce diffs, not deployments; every change lands via pull request, with direct execution only as a separate break-glass path.
    • Least privilege: Credentials are short-lived, scoped, time-limited and auditable to control blast radius.
    • Fail-safe: When uncertain the agent stops and asks a human; timeouts and policy gates are structural, not advisory.
    • Equal treatment: Agent changes pass the same review, CI and deployment pipelines as human changes.
    • Mental model: Four layers: policy plane, agent runtime, change control, and observability tracing every tool call and credential request.
    • Alternatives: Compares Claude Agent SDK, OpenAI Agents SDK, LangGraph; sandboxes like E2B, Modal, Daytona, AgentCore; Vault, Atlantis, Spacelift, OpenTelemetry.
    • Hardening: Chapter on trajectory tests, prompt-injection defense and security benchmarks, plus go-live checklists and compliance mapping.
    • License: Text under CC BY 4.0, code snippets under MIT; vendor-authored by Cloudgeni.

    Takeaway: Before giving an agent cloud access, restrict it to opening PRs with short-lived scoped credentials and full tool-call tracing, using the guide’s go-live checklist.

  • zeroboot: Sub-Millisecond VM Sandboxes for AI Agents via Copy-on-Write Forking
    summary

    TL;DR: Zeroboot is a prototype that spawns KVM VM sandboxes for AI agent code in under a millisecond by copy-on-write forking a pre-snapshotted Firecracker VM.

    • How it works: Firecracker boots a template once and snapshots memory and CPU state; each fork maps memory with mmap(MAP_PRIVATE) and restores CPU state.
    • Isolation: Every fork is a separate KVM VM with hardware-enforced memory isolation.
    • Numbers (claimed): Spawn p50 0.79ms, p99 1.74ms, about 265KB per sandbox, Python fork plus exec about 8ms, 1000 concurrent forks in 815ms.
    • Comparison (claimed): E2B about 150ms p50, Daytona about 27ms, microsandbox about 200ms.
    • API: POST /v1/exec with code and a Bearer token; Python and TypeScript (@zeroboot/sdk) SDKs; self-host on any Linux box with KVM.
    • Security caveat: Forks inherit CSPRNG state; the kernel pool is reseeded via RNDADDENTROPY, but userspace PRNGs like OpenSSL and numpy need manual reseeding.
    • Limits: One vCPU per fork, no networking (serial I/O only), and template changes need a full re-snapshot of about 15 seconds.
    • Status: Described as a working prototype, not production-hardened.

    Takeaway: Worth tracking for fast agent sandboxes, but reseed userspace RNGs per fork before generating keys or tokens, and do not run it in production yet.

  • IronCurtain: A Personal AI Assistant Built Secure from the Ground Up
    summary

    TL;DR: Niels Provos introduces IronCurtain, a research-prototype personal AI assistant that forces every agent action through a trusted MCP proxy enforcing a plain-English policy compiled into deterministic rules.

    • Problem: Cites 40,000+ exposed OpenClaw instances leaking API keys and history, plus prompt injection that can turn agents against owners.
    • Chokepoint: A trusted process acts as MCP proxy; its policy engine returns allow, deny or escalate for each call; each MCP server runs in its own OS sandbox.
    • Code Mode: The LLM writes TypeScript against a typed API, run in a V8 isolate with no filesystem, network or env access via one execute_code tool.
    • Docker Mode: Agents like Claude Code run with --network=none; only exits are a Unix socket to the proxy and a TLS MITM proxy for LLM calls.
    • Credentials: The container gets a fake API key; the MITM proxy swaps in the real key, which never enters the sandbox.
    • Policy: A natural-language “constitution” compiles into rules like allow-git-local-read-ops and escalate-git-remote-ops for git_push.
    • Auto-approver: Opt-in; approves only explicitly requested actions, sees only the user prompt and sanitized details, and escalates vague replies like “go ahead”.
    • Limits: Prompt injection and model drift remain unsolved; IronCurtain limits blast radius rather than preventing them; Claude Code integration is rough.
    • Try it: npx @provos/ironcurtain; supports filesystem, git, web fetch, Brave and SerpAPI search, and Signal.

    Takeaway: Apply the pattern to your own agents: enforce policy outside the model at a single proxy, deny network by default, and keep real credentials out of the sandbox.

  • Session Policies for Amazon EKS Pod Identity
    summary

    TL;DR: EKS Pod Identity associations now accept an inline IAM session policy that narrows a role’s permissions per service account, avoiding one IAM role per permission variant.

    • Problem: Least privilege per workload means many roles, pressing against the 5,000-role account quota.
    • How it works: Effective permissions are the intersection of the role’s policies and the session policy; it can only restrict, never expand.
    • API: New policy field on CreatePodIdentityAssociation and UpdatePodIdentityAssociation, up to 2,048 characters of JSON.
    • Hard requirement: disableSessionTags must be true when policy is set, otherwise the call fails with PackedPolicyTooLarge.
    • Cross-account: With targetRoleArn, the policy applies at the target-role hop; the source role needs sts:AssumeRole and sts:TagSession.
    • Validation: JSON, allowed characters, size, IAM schema via STS dry-run, and packed size after compression.
    • Propagation: Changes take about 10 seconds; running pods keep old credentials until refresh, so recreate pods for immediate effect.
    • Use cases: Multi-tenant SaaS, multiple environments in one cluster, and per-job S3 or DynamoDB subsets.
    • Tooling: Supported in the AWS CLI, EKS API, CloudFormation and eksctl across Commercial, China and GovCloud Regions.

    Takeaway: Use session policies to scope shared roles per namespace, but note that losing session tags breaks any ABAC conditions on them, and watch CloudTrail during rollout.

    • Harnesses: Claude Code, Antigravity, Codex and OpenCode, with per-agent models and credentials.

    • Templates: A system prompt plus skills; agents delegate via CLI, message each other, and notify on completion.
    • Workspaces: Shared directories, Git worktrees or separate clones; add .scion/agents to .gitignore to avoid nested worktree issues.
    • Interfaces: Web chat plus Telegram, Discord, Slack, Google Chat and Microsoft Teams; MCP config, lifecycle hooks, A2A bridge, Skill Bank.
    • Setup: brew install homebrew-scion/scion/scion, scion server start, then scion init and scion start my-agent "task".
    • Modes: Local CLI, Workstation, single-node hosted, and HA hosted with Postgres and shared storage; hosted adds roles and scoped secrets.
    • Caveats: Not an officially supported Google product; APIs and config may change between releases.

    Takeaway: If you run several coding agents in parallel, Scion gives per-agent container isolation and scoped credentials; pin versions given its pre-1.0 churn.

  • Enforcing AI Governance Across AWS Organizations Using Bedrock Guardrails
    summary

    TL;DR: Sonrai shows how to enforce AI governance org-wide on AWS using SCPs and Bedrock Policies: block MCP-driven API calls, enforce guardrails, restrict regions, models and long-term Bedrock API keys.

    • Managed MCP servers: aws__call_aws can make arbitrary API calls; deny all actions when aws:ViaAWSMCPService is true (or target servers via aws:CalledViaAWSMCP).
    • MCP caveat: Agents like Claude Code or Kiro running the local AWS CLI bypass this control entirely.
    • Bedrock Policies: Org-level policy type BEDROCK_POLICY applies shared guardrails (e.g. PROMPT_ATTACK filter) to all models, even when callers specify none.
    • Guardrail gotchas: Guardrails are regional; sharing needs a resource policy allowing bedrock:ApplyGuardrail by aws:PrincipalOrgID; input_tags: honor lets direct callers bypass.
    • Region lock: SCP denying bedrock:* and bedrock-mantle:* outside allowed regions via aws:RequestedRegion; check cross-region inference profiles first.
    • Model control: Deny foundation-model ARNs like arn:aws:bedrock:*::foundation-model/deepseek* plus bedrock-mantle:Model; allow-listing is awkward.
    • Service kill switch: Deny bedrock-agentcore:*, exempting security or break-glass roles via aws:PrincipalArn.
    • API keys: Deny bedrock:CallWithBearerToken when BearerTokenType is LONG_TERM, and deny iam:CreateServiceSpecificCredential.
    • Caveats: Account-level PutEnforcedGuardrailConfiguration is easier to tamper with; test every control in a sandbox org first.

    Takeaway: Add SCPs blocking long-term Bedrock keys, unapproved regions and MCP-originated calls, and enforce a baseline prompt-attack guardrail via Bedrock Policies from the management account.

  • npm Security Best Practices: A Curated Collection of Package Manager Security Guidelines
    summary

    TL;DR: Liran Tal’s curated list of npm supply-chain hardening practices, with concrete config for npm, pnpm, Yarn and Bun covering install scripts, cooldowns, lockfiles and publishing.

    • Install scripts: npm config set ignore-scripts true; pnpm 10+ and Bun block them by default with allowBuilds / trustedDependencies allowlists.
    • Cooldown: min-release-age (npm), minimumReleaseAge (pnpm, Bun), npmMinimalAgeGate (Yarn); Dependabot cooldown and Renovate minimumReleaseAge for PRs.
    • Git deps: npm config set allow-git none (npm 11.10.0+); pnpm blockExoticSubdeps: true restricts git/tarball sources to direct deps.
    • Trust downgrade: pnpm 10.21+ trustPolicy: no-downgrade rejects versions with weaker provenance than earlier releases.
    • Deterministic installs: npm ci, pnpm install --frozen-lockfile, yarn install --immutable; validate lockfiles with lockfile-lint.
    • Install-time tools: npq and Socket Firewall (sfw npm install) vet packages before install.
    • npx hardening: Pre-install MCP servers into a locked workspace and run with --no --offline.
    • Publishing: Enable 2FA, use npm publish --provenance and OIDC trusted publishing to drop long-lived tokens.
    • Other: Scoped names plus .npmrc registry routing against dependency confusion; inspect tarballs with npm pack --dry-run; secrets via op run.

    Takeaway: Set ignore-scripts, a release-age cooldown and allow-git none in CI and dev machines today, and move package publishing to OIDC trusted publishing.

  • GitHub Actions 2026 Security Roadmap: Deterministic Dependencies and Policy Controls
    summary

    TL;DR: GitHub’s 2026 Actions security roadmap adds dependency locking, workflow execution policies, scoped secrets, telemetry streaming and a native egress firewall in response to CI supply-chain attacks.

    • Motivation: Attacks like tj-actions/changed-files, Nx and trivy-action exploited mutable refs, over-permissioned tokens and open egress.
    • Dependency locking: New dependencies: workflow section pins direct and transitive actions to SHAs, like go.sum; hash mismatch stops the run.
    • Execution policies: Ruleset-based actor and event rules, e.g. ban pull_request_target or limit workflow_dispatch to maintainers.
    • Evaluate mode: Policies log would-be blocks in policy insights before enforcement.
    • Scoped secrets: Bind secrets to repos, branches, environments or workflow paths; no implicit inheritance in reusable workflows.
    • Permission change: Write access no longer grants secret management; it moves to a dedicated custom role.
    • Actions Data Stream: Near real-time run telemetry to Amazon S3 or Azure Event Hub, at-least-once delivery.
    • Egress firewall: Layer 7, outside the runner VM, monitor and enforce modes; GitHub-hosted runners only.
    • Timeline: Most previews in 3-6 months, GA at 6-9 months from March 2026; targets, not commitments.

    Takeaway: Pin actions to SHAs, remove pull_request_target where possible, and plan to adopt lockfiles, scoped secrets and egress monitor mode as they reach preview.

  • prek: A Git Hook Manager Written in Rust, Drop-In Alternative to pre-commit
    summary

    TL;DR: prek is a Rust rewrite of pre-commit: a single dependency-free binary that runs your existing hook configs faster, with monorepo support and supply-chain safeguards.

    • What it is: A framework that runs hooks before commit, on demand or in CI, and manages the toolchains those hooks need.
    • Compatibility: Drop-in replacement; reads existing .pre-commit-config.yaml and hooks unchanged, plus a native prek.toml format (prek util yaml-to-toml migrates).
    • Speed: Shares hook environments across repos, fetches and sets up in parallel, uses uv for Python venvs; claims multiple times faster than pre-commit.
    • Built-in hooks: Rust-native fast path for common pre-commit-hooks entries, and offline repo: builtin hooks like deny-pattern and require-pattern.
    • Monorepos: Workspace mode keeps per-project configs, runs from one command, and runs independent same-depth projects concurrently.
    • Workflow: Hook groups with --group/--no-group, --glob/--directory/--files targeting, --dry-run preview, and honors core.hooksPath.
    • Security: Verifies checksums of downloaded toolchains; prek update supports --cooldown-days, SHA freezing, and impostor-commit detection for pinned revisions.
    • Adoption: Used by CPython, Apache Airflow and FastAPI; installs via standalone script, uv, Homebrew, mise, Cargo, npm, Nix and others.

    Takeaway: Swap pre-commit for prek in CI and dev machines to drop the Python dependency, and use prek update --cooldown-days plus SHA freezing with --check in CI to harden hook supply chains.

  • mactop: Terminal-Based Monitoring Tool for Apple Silicon Chips
    summary

    TL;DR: mactop is a Go top-style terminal monitor for Apple Silicon Macs showing CPU, GPU, ANE, power, temperature and bandwidth metrics without needing sudo.

    • Scope: Apple Silicon (ARM64) only, macOS Monterey 12.3+; written in Go with CGO by Carsen Klock.
    • How it works: Reads native Apple APIs (SMC, IOReport, IOKit, IOHIDEventSystemClient, Mach kernel API), so no root is needed for monitoring.

    • Prometheus: Optional exporter via -p <port> with CPU/GPU/ANE usage, power, bandwidth, fans, temperatures and thermal state.
