  • memlab: Framework for Finding JavaScript Memory Leaks and Analyzing Heap Snapshots
    summary

    TL;DR: MemLab is Meta’s open-source framework for finding JavaScript memory leaks by driving browsers with Puppeteer and diffing heap snapshots, plus CLI, APIs and an MCP server for analysis.

    • How it works: A scenario file defines url, action and back; memlab run --scenario replays it, compares heap snapshots and filters leaked objects.
    • Output: Clusters leaks and prints one retainer trace per cluster, the reference chain from GC root that keeps the object alive.
    • Custom detectors: Add a leakFilter(node, heap) callback to flag objects using your own logic over the heap graph.
    • Heap analysis: memlab analyze unbound-object finds growing objects; memlab view-heap and memlab trace --node-id inspect snapshots interactively.
    • Runtimes: Works with snapshots from Chromium browsers, Node.js, Electron and Hermes; Node programs can assert on their own memory in tests.
    • MCP server: @memlab/mcp-server lets AI assistants load snapshots, find largest retainers, detached DOM nodes, closures and duplicated strings.
    • Caveats: Readable traces need non-minified code or preserved names; the example MCP config sets --max-old-space-size=8192 for large snapshots.

    Takeaway: Add memlab run scenarios for key user flows to CI to catch frontend or Electron leaks before release, and use memlab analyze on Node service heap dumps during memory incidents.

  • zerobrew: A 5-20x Faster Experimental Homebrew Alternative
    summary

    TL;DR: zerobrew is an experimental, uv-style Homebrew client for macOS and Linux that installs the same Homebrew bottles several times faster via a content-addressed store and in-process relocation.

    • What it is: A zb CLI that uses homebrew-core formulae, bottles and metadata; it compiles nothing and maintains no formulae.
    • How it works: Relocates binaries in-process and links from a content-addressed store with APFS clonefiles, instead of Ruby evaluation plus install_name_tool and re-signing per binary.
    • Numbers: 100-package benchmark: 782s vs 216s cold (3.6x) and 650s vs 27.7s warm (23x); median package 4.8x cold, 52x warm.
    • Bottlenecks: Cold installs are bandwidth bound; ca-certificates post-install (~5s) sets the cold floor; fontconfig’s fc-cache is not cached yet.
    • Commands: zb install, zb bundle (Brewfile), zb outdated, zb upgrade, zb gc, zb reset, and zbx to run without linking.
    • Brew compat: zb install-alias brew adds a brew shim for common subcommands; search, services, tap and --cask are not supported.
    • CI guardrails: Parity workflow compares installed prefixes byte for byte with Homebrew; nightly timing fails below 1.5x cold or 3x warm speedup.
    • Caveats: Warm speed costs extra disk (bottle plus unpacked keg kept); maintainers advise running alongside Homebrew, not replacing it.

    Takeaway: Try zb bundle against your Brewfile to speed up laptop or macOS CI runner provisioning, but keep real Homebrew installed for casks, taps and services.

  • aws-config-d: Manage Multiple AWS SSO Organizations with Separate Config Files
    summary

    TL;DR: aws-config-d is a dependency-free shell tool that splits ~/.aws/config into per-organization files under ~/.aws/config.d/ and rebuilds the real config on shell start.

    • Problem: Multi-org consultants end up with one huge ~/.aws/config; the AWS CLI has no native config.d/ support (feature request open since March 2022).
    • How it works: A shell hook runs aws-config-d auto; if any part is newer than ~/.aws/config, it concatenates all parts in lexicographic order.
    • Disabling: Suffix a file with .off or move it to disabled/; aws-config-d enable, disable, list and rm manage this.
    • Drift detection: A SHA-256 of the last generated file catches outside edits (e.g. aws configure sso) and warns instead of silently overwriting.
    • Setup: install.sh creates config.d/, migrates the existing config to 01-migrated-config, installs to ~/.local/bin, and hooks bash, zsh or fish.
    • Testing: 42 tests across bash, zsh and fish in isolated Docker containers.
    • Limits: Pure concatenation workaround; aws configure sso still edits the generated file; ~/.aws/credentials is not managed.

    Takeaway: If you juggle several AWS organizations, keep one file per client in ~/.aws/config.d/ and disable finished engagements, so stale SSO profiles stop leaking into daily work.

  • Top 10 Web Hacking Techniques of 2025
    summary

    TL;DR: PortSwigger’s annual community-nominated, panel-ranked list of the ten most novel web attack techniques published in 2025, with side-channels as the dominant theme.

    • Process: 63 community nominations (down from 121), community vote to 15 finalists, then a panel including James Kettle, Soroush Dalili and LiveOverflow ranked the top 10.
    • #1: “Successful Errors” by Vladislav Korchagin: error-based techniques for blind SSTI and code injection, polyglot detection, open-source toolkit.
    • #2: “ORM Leaking More Than You Joined For” by Alex Brown: a generic method to exploit search and filter features via ORM leaks.
    • #3: Novel SSRF via HTTP redirect loops by @shubs, turning blind SSRF into visible response data.
    • #4-5: Unicode normalization exploitation (Ryan and Isabella Barnett, ActiveScan++ updates) and SOAPwn: .NET HttpWebClientProtocol proxy and WSDL abuse to RCE (Piotr Bazydło).
    • #6-8: Cross-Site ETag Length Leak (Takeshi Kaneko), Next.js internal cache poisoning (Rachid Allam), XSS-Leak via Chrome connection-pool prioritisation (Salvatore Abello).
    • #9-10: HTTP/2 CONNECT abuse for internal port scanning (flomb) and parser differentials across languages and frameworks (joernchen).
    • Themes: Side-channels became a core exploitation primitive; Kettle speculates vibe-coding may revive older weak coding practices.
    • Also noted: Kettle’s “HTTP/1.1 Must Die” was shortlisted but excluded by his own rules; malformed-chunk desync and SAML auth bypass nominations missed the cut.

    Takeaway: Use the list as a 2026 testing checklist: probe for parser differentials, blind SSTI via errors, ORM filter leaks, redirect-loop SSRF and cache poisoning in your own apps and Next.js deployments.

    summary

    TL;DR: Cisco AI Defense’s Skill Scanner is a best-effort open-source scanner that flags prompt injection, data exfiltration and malicious code in AI agent skills before you install them.

    • How it works: Combines YAML and YARA-X signatures, AST and dataflow analysis, an optional LLM-as-a-judge, and a bounded CEL decision layer (cel-go v0.32.0).
    • Formats: Agent Skills spec (OpenAI Codex, Cursor); --lenient also scans Claude Code .claude/commands/*.md and flat markdown skill repos.
    • Numbers: On MaliciousSkillBench held-out split (839 malicious, 545 harmless), rules alone catch 7.7% at HIGH with 4.0% FPR.
    • With LLM judge: Gemma 4 26B on balanced sends 66.7% to review at MEDIUM+ (15.4% FPR) and blocks 33.7% at HIGH (6.4% FPR).
    • Presets: balanced, low-noise and quiet policies trade recall for false positives; a local model keeps everything on the machine.
    • CI/CD: SARIF output for GitHub Code Scanning, reusable GitHub Actions workflow, pre-commit hook, REST API and --fail-on-severity exit codes.
    • Setup: uv tool install cisco-ai-skill-scanner (CPython 3.11-3.14); extras for Bedrock, Gemini, Vertex and Azure OpenAI judges.
    • Limits: No findings does not mean safe; false positives and negatives occur, and the authors say human review remains essential.

    Takeaway: Gate third-party agent skills with skill-scanner scan --use-llm --policy balanced --fail-on-severity high in CI, but treat it as triage, since rules alone miss over 90% of malicious skills.

  • AI-Assisted Cloud Intrusion Achieves Admin Access in 8 Minutes
    summary

    TL;DR: Sysdig documents an apparently LLM-assisted AWS intrusion that went from credentials leaked in a public S3 bucket to admin via Lambda code injection in about 8 minutes, then abused Bedrock and GPUs.

    • Initial access: IAM user keys found in public S3 buckets holding RAG data, named with common AI tool conventions; the user had Lambda read/write plus ReadOnlyAccess.
    • Privilege escalation: Used UpdateFunctionCode and UpdateFunctionConfiguration on EC2-init, which had an admin execution role, to mint access keys for admin user frick.
    • Technique detail: Injected code returned new keys in the function response, with no reverse shell; timeout raised from 3 to 30 seconds; path matches pathfinding.cloud lambda-004.
    • Persistence and spread: Created backdoor-admin with AdministratorAccess; 19 principals, 6 roles over 14 sessions, 5 users taken over; tried OrganizationAccountAccessRole across accounts.
    • AI indicators: Fast, heavily exception-handled code with Serbian comments, hallucinated account IDs, and a nonexistent anthropic/training-scripts GitHub repo.
    • LLMjacking: Confirmed Bedrock invocation logging was off, then invoked Claude, DeepSeek R1, Llama 4 Scout, Nova and Titan models, accepting Marketplace agreements as needed.
    • GPU abuse: p5.48xlarge launches failed on capacity; a p4d.24xlarge ($32.77/hour) ran 5 minutes with public JupyterLab on port 8888 and all TCP ports open.
    • Evasion: IP rotator per request, role chaining across many principals; Sysdig publishes IP and naming IOCs such as stevan-gpu-key.
    • Defenses: Short-lived role credentials, private RAG buckets, restrict Lambda update and PassRole, Lambda versioning, Bedrock invocation logging, SCPs limiting models and instance types.

    Takeaway: Audit who can call lambda:UpdateFunctionCode on functions with privileged roles, enable Bedrock invocation logging, and use SCPs to block unapproved models and GPU instance types.

  • OpenClaw Security Engineer’s Cheat Sheet
    summary

    TL;DR: Semgrep’s cheat sheet for security teams on OpenClaw (Clawdbot/Moltbot), the popular local LLM agent: its risks, how to find it on your fleet, and how to harden or contain it.

    • What it is: An LLM orchestrator (about 160k GitHub stars) whose model output becomes tool calls on the host, likely already on employee laptops.
    • Core risk: Data becomes code; any processed web page, email or document can carry prompt injection, and blast radius grows with connected capabilities.
    • Credentials: Secrets are stored unencrypted in a known location; prompt-injected exfiltration has been publicly demonstrated.
    • Skills supply chain: About 12% of audited ClawHub skills were malicious (386 or 341 of 2,857); 26% of 31,132 ecosystem skills had a vulnerability.
    • Malware tactics: ClickFix lures, typosquatting, reputation washing, gamed popularity, curl | bash, base64 commands and xattr -d com.apple.quarantine.
    • Detection: Look for openclaw/moltbot/clawdbot binaries and npm packages, ~/.openclaw config dirs, gateway port 18789, and domains like clawhub.com.
    • Hardening: Dedicated VM, container sandbox, allowInsecureAuth false, dmPolicy pairing, groupPolicy allowlist, OPENCLAW_DISABLE_BONJOUR=1, run openclaw audit.
    • Extra tooling: Cisco skill-scanner and Clawdex for skills, ClawShield audits, nono kernel sandbox and Trail of Bits devcontainer for isolation.
    • Incident response: Assume every secret the agent could reach is compromised and rotate it; check lateral movement including via Moltbook.

    Takeaway: Sweep endpoints for port 18789 and ~/.openclaw, and block corporate connections until agents run isolated with vetted skills and no crown-jewel credentials.

  • ClawShield: Security Preflight and Guardrails for OpenClaw
    summary

    TL;DR: ClawShield is a small Node CLI that audits OpenClaw/Moltbot configs for risky settings, detects exposed gateways, and fingerprints installed skills to catch tampering.

    • Problem: OpenClaw runs powerful tools, so one config mistake can expose its control panel or let the wrong agent use dangerous tools.
    • Risks checked: Gateway bound to the network without auth, group chats triggering actions without a mention, non-main agents running unsandboxed, skills modified after install.
    • Audit: clawshield audit is read-only and prints PASS/WARN/FAIL items with suggested fixes.
    • Exposure: clawshield exposure flags control-port listeners reachable beyond localhost; on macOS it relies on lsof.
    • Safe profile: clawshield profile safe prints a JSON snippet; apply safe shows a dry-run diff, and --write applies it with a .bak-* backup.
    • Tamper detection: clawshield lock fingerprints installed skills, clawshield verify reports changes or missing files.
    • Monitoring: clawshield watch --interval 30 or a generated LaunchAgent re-checks exposure in the background on macOS.
    • Setup: Install via curl script, Homebrew tap or npm install -g clawshield; reads ~/.openclaw/openclaw.json or OPENCLAW_CONFIG_PATH.
    • Limits: Skill provenance verification, notifications and guided remediation are roadmap items, not shipped features.

    Takeaway: On any machine running OpenClaw, run clawshield audit and clawshield exposure, then clawshield lock after vetting skills, so later supply-chain tampering shows up in verify.

  • clawdstrike: Runtime Security Enforcement and Threat Hunting for Autonomous AI Fleets
    summary

    TL;DR: Clawdstrike is a pre-1.0 fail-closed policy engine and EDR that treats AI agent tool calls and kernel events as one event stream, with Ed25519-signed audit receipts.

    • What it is: One engine shipped as Rust crate, TypeScript, Python and Go SDKs, CLI, desktop EDR agent and enterprise control plane.
    • How it works: SDK adapters and OS sensors (macOS Endpoint Security and Network Extension, Linux Tetragon and Hubble) emit canonical events evaluated by a guard stack.
    • Guards: ForbiddenPathGuard, EgressAllowlistGuard, SecretLeakGuard, ShellCommandGuard, McpToolGuard, PromptInjectionGuard, JailbreakGuard and computer-use guards.
    • Enforcement: clawdstrike run --policy clawdstrike:strict -- python my_agent.py checks every tool call; denials raise typed errors and emit signed receipts.
    • Policies: Versioned policy-as-code with built-in rulesets like strict, ai-agent and cicd; remote extends pinned via #sha256=.
    • Policy loop: policy synth builds least-privilege candidates from observed events, policy simulate replays them, policy diff compares against baselines.
    • Verification: Core decision logic specified in Lean 4 (5 properties across 44 of 45 core functions) plus Z3 analysis via clawdstrike verify.
    • Fleet deploy: Helm chart brings up hushd, Spine checkpointer and witness co-signing, and NATS JetStream; keys must be pre-created as Secrets.
    • Caveats: Beta status, defaults may tighten before 1.0; proofs exclude guards beyond core logic, IO, network and crypto primitives.

    Takeaway: Wrap agents in clawdstrike run with the strict ruleset, then use policy synth and simulate on recorded events to build least-privilege policies for CI or developer agents.

  • Sandboxed Devcontainer for Running Claude Code in Bypass Mode Safely
    summary

    TL;DR: Trail of Bits devcontainer that runs Claude Code in bypassPermissions mode inside a disposable container, limiting it to the project directory for audits and untrusted repo review.

    • Problem: bypassPermissions on the host lets Claude run any command unconfirmed; the container confines it to /workspace and disposable state.

  • Testing Access to AWS Resources Without Angering the People That Pay the Bills
    summary

    TL;DR: Plerion’s Daniel Grzelak explains how to empirically and safely prove whether an AWS resource (mainly SNS topics) is reachable, without reading data or changing state, and releases sns-buster.

    • Problem: Policy-only checks miss IAM edge cases across resource policies, SCPs, boundaries and conditions, causing false positives that erode trust.
    • Safe test rules: No reading sensitive data, no state changes, fast and repeatable, minimal CloudTrail footprint; resource names and tags may still be sensitive.
    • Unsigned probes: Work for SQS, where public queues accept anonymous calls; SNS returns 403 MissingAuthenticationToken to all unsigned requests, even with "Principal": "*".
    • Metadata and no-ops: Use reads like GetTopicAttributes or DescribeSecret, or no-op writes like UntagResource with a nonexistent key; 200 proves access.
    • Malformed probes: AWS often checks authorization before parameters, so an invalid Publish returns 400 if authorized and 403 if not.
    • 3-topic method: Run each probe against an allowed, a denied and a nonexistent resource; 400/403/403 confirms the probe tests authorization.
    • SNS details: Resource policies reject SNS:*, console defaults add an AWS:SourceAccount condition, and GetTopicAttributes leaks owner account and KMS key ID.
    • Tool: sns-buster tests 14 SNS actions with 30+ parameter mutations each, signed and unsigned, and outputs reproducible evidence.
    • Caveats: Validation ordering is intuition-based and varies per API; “no-op” idempotent writes are not guaranteed safe; get permission before testing others’ resources.

    Takeaway: Before reporting a public SNS or SQS finding, confirm it with metadata reads or validated malformed probes, and run sns-buster --safe against your own topics.

    summary

    TL;DR: Brutus is Praetorian’s pure-Go, single-binary credential testing tool for 43 network protocols, positioned as a dependency-free, pipeline-friendly alternative to THC Hydra.

    • Operations: SOCKS5 via --proxy, --mode cautious|default|aggressive, rate limiting, connection pooling, and an importable Go library.

    Takeaway: Run brutus creds and brutus badkeys against your own ranges after deployments to catch default credentials and known SSH keys before attackers do, with written authorization.

    summary

  • Locking Down AWS Principal Tags with RCPs and SCPs
    summary

    TL;DR: Aidan Steele shows how to make IAM principal tags trustworthy for SCP exemptions by combining SCPs and RCPs so only a central tagger role can set scp-* tags.

    • Problem: Gating iam:CreateAccessKey, iam:CreateLoginProfile and similar on aws:PrincipalTag/scp-exempt-access-keys fails because account admins can tag their own roles.
    • Second gap: Principal tags can also arrive as session tags from outside the org (SaaS cross-account roles, OIDC, SAML), which SCPs do not cover.
    • SCP step: Deny iam:TagRole, iam:TagUser, untag and iam:CreateRole/iam:CreateUser when aws:TagKeys matches scp-* and aws:PrincipalArn is not the tagger role.
    • RCP step: Deny sts:TagSession for scp-* keys via two statements: caller is not tagger, or aws:ResourceOrgID differs from aws:PrincipalOrgID.
    • Why two statements: Conditions inside one statement are ANDed; separate statements give OR logic, and StringNotEquals on a missing key also blocks org-less accounts.
    • Protect the tagger: Another SCP denies role-mutating IAM actions on role/tagger and role/stacksets-exec-*, except for stacksets-exec-* principals.
    • Deployment: tagger is rolled out everywhere with CloudFormation service-managed StackSets so new accounts get it automatically.
    • Tag design: Prefer a locked-down prefix with one key per exemption over key-value forms like scp-exemption=access-keys.
    • Caveats: Service-linked roles bypass SCPs/RCPs; no test results are given; full ABAC is out of scope; StackSets carry operational pain.

    Takeaway: If your SCP exemptions rely on principal tags, lock down who can set those tag keys with both SCPs and an RCP on sts:TagSession, or the exemption is self-service.

  • AWS Incident Response: IAM Containment That Survives Eventual Consistency
    summary

    TL;DR: OFFENSAI shows attackers with IAM admin rights can detach a containment deny policy during IAM’s roughly 4-second propagation window, and fixes it with an SCP protecting the quarantine policy.

    • Problem: IAM changes take about 4 seconds to propagate, so an active attacker can undo identity-level containment before it applies.
    • Gap in standard playbook: The re:Post credential cleanup procedure attaches deny and session-revocation policies, but an attacker keeping iam:DetachUserPolicy can remove them.
    • Attacker techniques: Poll for new attachments and detach them, delete the policy object, or overwrite it with a permissive version.
    • Fix: SCP IRQuarantinePolicyGuard denies iam:DetachUserPolicy/iam:DetachRolePolicy matched via the iam:PolicyArn condition key, plus iam:DeletePolicy, iam:CreatePolicyVersion and iam:SetDefaultPolicyVersion.
    • Exemption: Only AWSReservedSSO_IR-Engineer_* (via aws:PrincipalArn with ArnNotLike) may modify it; that role is an MFA-enforced Identity Center permission set with SIEM alerting.
    • Result: In testing, the attacker’s monitor got AccessDenied trying to detach IR-QuarantinePolicy and S3 calls failed within seconds; without the SCP the attacker kept access.
    • Alternative: Moving the account to a quarantine OU also works since SCPs apply immediately, but it hits every workload and needs management-account access.
    • Caveats: Needs AWS Organizations; SCPs do not cover the management account; single-environment proof of concept; break-glass role must stay unreachable.

    Takeaway: Pre-deploy a quarantine managed policy and an SCP that makes it undetachable except by a break-glass IR role, so containment is one attach call that attackers cannot race.

    summary

  • The AWS Console and Terraform Security Gap
    summary

    TL;DR: Include Security shows AWS resources created via Terraform often get weaker legacy API defaults than the same resources made in the Console, silently producing less secure infrastructure.

    • Root cause: Terraform’s AWS provider uses the SDK, which keeps legacy defaults for backward compatibility; Terraform cannot change defaults without breaking idempotency.
    • RDS: aws_db_instance without storage_encrypted = true is unencrypted, including snapshots; fixing requires restoring into a new encrypted instance.
    • Lambda: aws_lambda_permission for apigateway.amazonaws.com without source_arn lets any API Gateway in the region, even cross-account, invoke it (confused deputy).
    • IAM password policy: Setting only minimum_password_length replaces the policy and defaults other requirements to false, hidden as “known after apply”.
    • Counterexample: S3 now defaults to public access block and SSE-S3, though versioning, MFA delete and logging stay optional.
    • Detection: Trivy, Checkov, Terrascan and Semgrep catch these but add false positives and overstated severity.
    • Prevention: OPA or Sentinel policy as code, SCPs like denying rds:CreateDBInstance when rds:StorageEncrypted is false, and hardened golden modules.
    • Supply chain: After Trivy’s repo was compromised twice, pin GitHub Actions to commit SHAs.
    • Caveats: Tested on AWS provider v6.28.0; static tools miss context-dependent IAM logic.

    Takeaway: Never assume Console defaults in Terraform; set security arguments explicitly, enforce them with scanners plus SCPs, and ship golden modules for RDS, Lambda permissions and IAM policies.

  • hackerbot-claw: An AI-Powered Bot Actively Exploiting GitHub Actions
    summary

    TL;DR: StepSecurity analyses hackerbot-claw, an AI-driven account that exploited GitHub Actions misconfigurations in major repos over about 10 days, fully compromising Aqua Security’s Trivy repository.

    • Actor: Account created 20 February 2026, self-described as an “autonomous security research agent powered by claude-opus-4-5”, active roughly 21 February to 2 March.
    • Targets: awesome-go, CNCF akri, microsoft/ai-discovery-agent, DataDog/datadog-iac-scanner, ambient-code/platform, aquasecurity/trivy and RustPython; 12+ PRs opened.
    • Pwn Request: pull_request_target workflows checking out fork code with secrets; awesome-go leaked a GITHUB_TOKEN with contents: write.
    • Injection: Shell payloads in branch names and Base64-encoded filenames executed via unescaped GitHub expressions in run: blocks; akri ran a modified version.sh from a /version minor comment.
    • Trivy impact: Stolen PAT used 19 minutes later to vandalise the README, make the repo private, delete releases v0.27.0 to v0.69.1 and push a suspicious Open VSX extension.
    • AI prompt injection: A poisoned CLAUDE.md tried to make Claude Code commit and fake approval; Claude refused both attempts.
    • Common weaknesses: Missing author_association checks, allowed_non_write_users: '*' in Claude workflows, broad token permissions.
    • Fixes: Egress allowlists on runners, no untrusted checkout under pull_request_target, pass untrusted values via env vars, least-privilege GITHUB_TOKEN, CODEOWNERS on AI config files.
    • Caveats: Vendor post promoting Harden-Runner; its own counts of compromised targets vary between 4 and 6.

    Takeaway: Audit your workflows today for pull_request_target with head checkout, expression interpolation in run:, and comment triggers without author checks, and restrict runner egress.

  • RTK: CLI Proxy That Reduces LLM Token Consumption by 60-90% on Dev Commands
    summary

    TL;DR: RTK is a single Rust binary CLI proxy that filters and compresses shell command output before AI coding agents read it, claiming 60 to 90% fewer tokens on common dev commands.

    • How it works: An agent hook rewrites commands, e.g. git status to rtk git status, so the agent gets compact output transparently.
    • Strategies: Smart filtering of boilerplate, grouping similar items, truncation, and deduplicating repeated log lines with counts.
    • Overhead: Under 10ms per command, per the README.
    • Coverage: 100+ commands including git, cargo test, pytest, go test, docker ps, Kubernetes, AWS, Terraform and many JS toolchains.
    • Setup: brew install rtk or winget install rtk-ai.rtk, then rtk init -g for Claude Code; flags exist for Gemini, Codex, Cursor and 18 tools total.
    • Analytics: rtk gain shows savings; rtk discover finds missed opportunities; rtk bypass <cmd> runs unfiltered.
    • Number caveats: Savings measure bash output only and use bytes/4 token estimates, so real bill savings are smaller.
    • Limits: Hook fires only on Bash tool calls, not built-in Read, Grep or Glob; a different crates.io project shares the rtk name.
    • Licensing: rtk is Apache-2.0 plus BUSL-1.1, rtk-oss is Apache-2.0 only; telemetry is off by default.

    Takeaway: Install RTK with rtk init -g to trim noisy test, git and infra CLI output in agent sessions, then verify with rtk gain that the correct rtk binary is active.

  • Caveman: Claude Code Skill That Cuts 65% of Tokens by Talking Like Caveman
    summary

    TL;DR: Caveman is an Apache-2.0 plugin for coding agents that makes replies terse and compresses tool input while keeping code exact, measuring 33.2% fewer session input tokens.

    • Say less: Short direct replies; code, commands, paths and errors stay verbatim; security warnings and irreversible actions get full sentences.
    • Read less: Compresses logs, JSON, CSV, YAML and test output before the model reads them, leaving originals on disk.
    • Modes: /caveman, /ultracave and /megacave (Chinese output), plus /caveman-commit, /caveman-review and /caveman-compress.
    • Numbers: 33.2% fewer input tokens across 54 Claude Code runs with 18 of 18 answers correct; HTML got 9.9% worse with no compressor yet.
    • Output benchmark: Ten dev questions dropped from 6,983 output tokens with no instruction to 4,119 with /caveman and 2,693 with /ultracave.
    • Newer pieces: Auto model/effort routing (requires sign-in), pre-release Python-only Blocks script reuse, and compressed web Browse.
    • Install: npx @caveman-ai/cli@latest (Node.js 22.13+) or claude plugin marketplace add JuliusBrussee/caveman; supports Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, OpenCode, Aider and more.
    • Caveats: Rules cost about 1,160 input tokens, so per-request billing like Copilot saves nothing; several features are unmeasured; results vary.
    • Privacy: Telemetry is on by default (commands, token counts, install ID, IP); disable with caveman telemetry off or DO_NOT_TRACK=1.

    Takeaway: Try Caveman to cut agent token spend on per-token billing, but turn telemetry off first and check whether Auto mode sends prompts to Caveman Cloud.

  • Docker Hardened Images for Everyone
    summary

    TL;DR: Docker made its Hardened Images free and Apache 2.0 licensed for all developers in December 2025, offering minimal Debian/Alpine-based images with SBOMs and SLSA Build Level 3 provenance.

    • Announcement: Over 1,000 hardened images and Helm charts are now free and open source; DHI originally launched in May 2025.

  • Tales of OIDC & OAuth Security: What It Takes to Trust a Token
    summary

    TL;DR: Mercari’s security team frames OAuth/OIDC security as answering who the user is, whether they intended the action, and whether the client should receive the tokens, with common attack patterns.

    • Thesis: Secure flows must establish user legitimacy, user intent and correct token recipient, all on a client free of XSS; threat model first.
    • Redirect URI bypass: Loose startsWith/endsWith checks accept attacker-mercari.com or mercari.com.attacker.com; use exact matching.
    • Open redirect chaining: Allowing /callback path prefixes plus an open redirect like /shop?next= forwards codes to attackers.
    • Hybrid flow leak: With response_type=code id_token, the ID token sits in the URL fragment, which survives redirect chains.
    • State and nonce: Use cryptographically random values; nonces must be single-use or ID tokens can be replayed.
    • PKCE limit: Does not help if the attacker starts the flow with their own code_challenge and intercepts the code.
    • DPoP: Many implementations skip binding the access token to the key at issuance, letting stolen tokens be reused with attacker-signed proofs.
    • Mix-up attacks: Multi-IdP setups sharing one callback can send codes to a malicious server; validate iss and use per-provider redirect URIs.
    • Caveats: Deliberately concise; does not cover aud, alg, signature or expiry checks, and reports no specific Mercari vulnerabilities.

    Takeaway: Review your OAuth clients for exact redirect matching, single-use nonces, iss validation and DPoP key binding, and consider PAR or FAPI 2.0 for sensitive APIs.

  • BadPods Series: Everything Allowed on AWS EKS
    summary

    TL;DR: Part 1 of a BadPods series showing how BishopFox’s “everything allowed” pod on AWS EKS escapes to the node, hijacks neighbour pods and steals the node IAM role.

    • Testbed: EKS on Kubernetes 1.34, two c7i-flex.large Amazon Linux 2023 nodes, default Pod Security Admission, which admitted the pod without warnings.
    • Manifest: privileged: true, hostPID, hostIPC, hostNetwork and a hostPath of / mounted at /host.
    • Node escape: chroot /host lands on the node filesystem with access to /var/lib/kubelet and /run/containerd/containerd.sock.
    • Lateral movement: From the host, nerdctl ps/inspect find a victim nginx pod’s PID, and nsenter enters its namespaces.
    • IMDS bypass: hostNetwork puts the pod in the node’s network namespace, so http_put_response_hop_limit = 1 does not block 169.254.169.254.
    • IMDSv2 no help: The pod fetches its own session token (21600 s TTL), so token enforcement does not stop credential theft.
    • Impact: aws sts get-caller-identity returned the eks-demo-node role, which had AdministratorAccess attached on purpose for maximum impact.
    • Caveats: Lab cluster, victim pinned with nodeSelector, PSA restricted profile not tested; AKS, GKE Autopilot and reduced-flag pods come in later parts.

    Takeaway: Enforce PSA baseline/restricted on EKS namespaces, treat hostNetwork as direct IMDS access, and keep node IAM roles minimal.

    summary

    TL;DR: Tailsnitch is a Go CLI from Adversis that audits a Tailscale tailnet for risky ACLs, auth keys, devices and settings, with optional API-driven fixes.

    • Coverage: 57 checks across 7 categories: access, auth, network, ssh, log, device and dns.

  • AWS Privilege Escalation: IAM, Service & AI Threats
    summary

    TL;DR: Software Secured maps AWS privilege escalation from classic IAM edits to service abuse and new Bedrock AgentCore paths, and shows which ones native controls cannot block.

    • Era 1, IAM: iam:CreatePolicyVersion, iam:AttachUserPolicy, iam:PutUserPolicy, iam:UpdateAssumeRolePolicy, iam:CreateAccessKey, iam:CreateLoginProfile.
    • Era 2, services: lambda:UpdateFunctionCode, EventBridge routing, poisoned ECR images and Step Functions chains, usually enabled by iam:PassRole.
    • Era 3, AI: bedrock-agentcore-control:CreateCodeInterpreter runs Python under the agent’s IAM role with unrestricted SDK calls.
    • Agent tool hijack: Replacing the Lambda behind an agent action group with schema-conforming malicious code lets the agent exfiltrate S3 data.
    • Role pivoting: iam:PassRole over shared AgentCore trust domains lets an interpreter creator run as a more privileged role.
    • Control gaps: IAM and STS have no resource policies, and actions with Resource: "*" can only be allowed or denied globally by SCPs.
    • Framework: Ask whether a permission grants code execution, credentials, policy changes, cross-service routing or lateral movement into compute.
    • Numbers: 16 of 16 scenarios (12 IAM-Vulnerable, 4 modern) were exploitable unrestricted and blocked by SCP-based guardrails.
    • Caveats: Co-written with Sonrai, whose Cloud Permissions Firewall supplied the guardrails; tools cited include Pacu, PMapper, CloudFox and CloudGoat.

    Takeaway: Treat AgentCore Code Interpreters and agent Lambdas as privileged compute, and restrict iam:PassRole and CreateCodeInterpreter with SCPs.

  • 2025 LLM Year in Review
    summary

    TL;DR: Andrej Karpathy’s review of six LLM paradigm shifts in 2025, from RLVR reasoning training to Claude Code, vibe coding and visual LLM interfaces.

    • RLVR: Reinforcement learning on verifiable rewards became a major training stage, from o1 (late 2024) to o3 (early 2025).
    • Test-time compute: RLVR made longer “thinking” a new capability lever with its own scaling law, absorbing compute once meant for pretraining.
    • Jagged intelligence: LLMs are “ghosts”, not animals; capability spikes near verifiable domains, and jailbreaks still trick them.
    • Benchmarks: He has lost trust in them because labs “benchmaxx” with RLVR and synthetic data near benchmark regions.
    • App layer: Cursor showed LLM apps that do context engineering, orchestrate DAGs of calls, give a GUI and an autonomy slider.
    • Local agents: Claude Code proved agents that loop over tools on localhost with your data, secrets and config beat cloud containers.
    • Vibe coding: Building software by describing it in English, e.g. a Rust BPE tokenizer for nanochat without learning Rust.
    • LLM GUI: Gemini “Nano banana” hints at models answering with images, slides and apps instead of console-style text.
    • Bottom line: Models are smarter and dumber than expected, with well under 10% of their potential realised so far.

    Takeaway: Expect local coding agents with access to your secrets and config to spread; scope their credentials and permissions now.

  • Claude Code Safety Net: Catch Destructive Git and Filesystem Commands Before Execution
    summary

    TL;DR: CC Safety Net is a pre-execution guard that blocks AI coding agents from running destructive commands or reading secrets before the tool call runs.

    • Blocks: git reset --hard, git push --force, rm -rf on dangerous targets, and curl … | bash installers.
    • Secrets: Denies access to SSH keys, .env files, ~/.aws and coding-CLI credentials, in shell commands and file tools.

    • Team use: Commit .cc-safety-net/ to share rules; a broken config falls back to safe defaults; checkCommand is a Node API.
    • Limits: Not a sandbox: no process containment, filesystem permissions or network egress monitoring.

    Takeaway: Add it as a cheap guardrail for agent sessions, but pair it with a real sandbox, because it only filters commands.

  • The Cloud-Native Detection Engineering Handbook
    summary

    TL;DR: A practical handbook for cloud-native detection engineering, covering nine phases from threat research to tuning, with detection-as-code and multi-cloud log normalisation.

    • Prioritise: Rank TTPs by exploitability, blast radius and detection return; favour privilege escalation and lateral movement over noisy recon.
    • Telemetry first: Validate log sources and coverage with red team or simulated ground truth; mark a detection INFEASIBLE if data is missing.
    • Normalise: Map GKE, EKS and AKS logs to ECS or OCSF in Bronze, Silver and Gold layers: “Normalize once, deploy everywhere”.
    • Enrich: Add identity, IP reputation, geolocation, asset metadata, baselines and a 1-hour temporal window.
    • Techniques: T1525 implanted images, T1609 kubectl exec, T1619 storage discovery, reverse shells via curl | bash and /dev/tcp/.
    • Testing: Unit, replay, negative and red team tests, with pytest treating detections as functions.
    • Deploy: Detection-as-code with Terraform for BigQuery, Databricks and Lambda jobs, peer review and rollback via git revert.
    • Numbers: Log lag under 5 minutes, target FP rate below 5%, retune above 20%; claimed 90% FP cut from Gold-layer filtering.
    • Caveats: Results come without methodology, schemas are simplified, and SQL and Terraform snippets are illustrative.

    Takeaway: Store detections as tested code with Terraform deployment, and normalise multi-cloud Kubernetes audit logs to one schema first.

    summary

    • Code auditing: Largest group, including differential-review, insecure-defaults, sharp-edges, static-analysis, semgrep-rule-creator, variant-analysis, supply-chain-risk-auditor, agentic-actions-auditor.

    Takeaway: Add the marketplace and install only the audit skills you need, such as insecure-defaults and supply-chain-risk-auditor, for security reviews.

  • Streamlining Security Investigations with Agents
    summary

    TL;DR: Slack Security Engineering explains how it replaced one large prompt with a structured team of AI agents to investigate detection alerts.

    • Problem: Billions of events per day make alert triage the main on-call load; a ~300-word single-prompt prototype (May 2025) gave inconsistent results.
    • Root cause: Prompts are only guidelines and give no fine-grained control over the investigation process.
    • Fix: Split work into single-purpose model calls with JSON-schema outputs, chained by the application.
    • Roles: A Director plans and steers; four experts cover Access, Cloud, Code and Threat; a Critic scores findings against a rubric.
    • Cost tiers: Low-, medium- and high-cost models for experts, Critic and Director respectively; models are not named.
    • Phases: Discovery queries every expert, Trace targets one, a Director Decision meta-phase advances, and Conclude writes the report.
    • Tooling: A stdio MCP server exposes data sources; a hub, scalable workers and a dashboard run production investigations.
    • Result: The Critic caught a credential exposed in an ancestor process command line that an expert missed.
    • Limits: Early phase, edited example report, no accuracy, false-positive, cost or throughput figures.

    Takeaway: For agentic triage, use structured single-purpose steps and a separate critic model instead of relying on one long prompt.

  • Phishing Actors Exploit Complex Routing and Misconfigurations to Spoof Domains
    summary

    TL;DR: Microsoft Threat Intelligence shows phishers spoofing a tenant’s own domain when MX routing goes through third parties and SPF, DKIM or DMARC are not enforced.

    • Problem: Attackers send mail that appears internal, with the same address in “To” and “From”, hitting tenants whose MX records do not point to Office 365.
    • Root cause: Complex routing through on-prem Exchange or third-party filters breaks native spoof detection; Microsoft says it is a configuration gap, not a Direct Send flaw.
    • Misconfigurations: SPF soft fail ~all instead of -all, DMARC p=none, no DKIM signing, and misconfigured third-party connectors or mail flow rules.
    • Tooling: Most activity uses the Tycoon2FA AiTM phishing-as-a-service kit; Microsoft blocked over 13 million Tycoon2FA-linked emails in October 2025.
    • Lures: Password expiry, voicemail, HR and DocuSign/SharePoint themes, QR codes in attachments, Google Maps redirects, Base64-encoded recipient URLs and custom CAPTCHA gates.
    • Financial scam: Fake CEO email threads push ACH payment of invoices around $9,860, backed by a fake invoice, a W-9 with a stolen SSN and a fake bank letter.
    • Header indicators: X-MS-Exchange-Organization-InternalOrgSender: True with incoming directionality, AuthAs: Anonymous, and reason codes 905, 451 or 601 showing delivery despite failures.
    • Mitigations: DMARC reject, SPF hard fail, DKIM on, correct connectors and enhanced filtering, Safe Links, ZAP, and phishing-resistant MFA such as FIDO2 or passkeys.
    • Hunting: Defender XDR EmailEvents queries find inbound mail with no connector, your own domain as sender, and failed SPF, DKIM or DMARC.

    Takeaway: If your MX does not point straight at Office 365, set DMARC to reject, SPF to -all and DKIM on, then hunt EmailEvents for self-domain mail that failed authentication.

  • A Brief Deep-Dive into Attacking and Defending Kubernetes
    summary

    TL;DR: A hands-on walk through eight Kubernetes attack techniques, each paired with hardening steps and custom Falco rules, built and tested on a microk8s lab.

    • Anonymous access: Kubelet ports 10250 and 10255 default to AlwaysAllow, and API server risk depends on what system:anonymous is bound to; tested with kubeletctl and curl.
    • RBAC abuse: cluster-admin bindings, wildcards and the bind/escalate verbs allow escalation; an Aqua honeypot case used a rogue kube-controller ClusterRole to deploy a cryptominer.
    • Token theft: A stolen token from /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token creates a privileged pod that mounts the host and escapes with chroot.
    • Persistence: A rogue MutatingWebhookConfiguration injects a privileged initContainer into every new pod and survives credential rotation; a CoreDNS rewrite rule in the ConfigMap redirects service names.
    • Host and etcd: hostPath mounts of /, /var/log or the Docker socket enable symlink and SUID attacks; Shodan shows about 4.7k exposed etcd endpoints, and kubetcd creates hidden “ghost pods”.
    • Golden Ticket: With ca.key and sa.key, k8s_spoofilizer forges admin certs and tokens; Kubernetes has no certificate revocation, so only a CA rebuild evicts the attacker.
    • Detections: Falco rules use the k8s_audit source (ka.user.name, ka.verb) and syscalls (execve, openat, symlinkat), with alerts routed through Falco Sidekick.
    • Hardening: Set --anonymous-auth=false, kubelet --authorization-mode=Webhook, automountServiceAccountToken: false, etcd --client-cert-auth, Secrets encryption at rest and an immutable CoreDNS ConfigMap.
    • Caveats: Paths and ports are microk8s-specific (API server 16443, etcd 12379), several rules rely on string matching, and none were validated against production traffic.

    Takeaway: Disable anonymous auth on the kubelet and API server, audit cluster-admin and wildcard bindings, and deploy Falco with audit-log rules for webhook, CoreDNS and etcd changes.

    summary

    TL;DR: Companion repo to a Kubernetes attack research post, with custom Falco rules, attack simulation scripts and a lab setup for testing detections of real cluster attacks.

    • Numbers: 81 commits and 58 stars at the time of fetch.

    Takeaway: Run the example scripts in a throwaway cluster to validate the Falco rules, then tune and port the useful ones into your own Falco Helm customRules.

    TL;DR: promptfoo is an MIT-licensed CLI and library for evaluating and red-teaming LLM apps and agents with declarative configs that also run in CI/CD.

  • Best Practices for Coding with Agents
    summary

    TL;DR: Cursor’s guide to working with coding agents argues the developer’s job shifts to planning, giving context, setting verifiable goals and reviewing output.

    • Harness: Instructions, tools and model together shape behavior, and Cursor tunes prompts and tools for each model.
    • Planning: Plan Mode (Shift+Tab) researches the codebase and writes an editable Markdown plan saved to .cursor/plans/; when output is wrong, revert and rerun from a tighter plan.
    • Context: Let the agent find files with grep and semantic search, and start a new chat when the task changes or the agent keeps repeating mistakes.
    • Rules: Keep short Markdown files in .cursor/rules/ with build and test commands, and point to example files rather than copying them; add a rule only after a mistake repeats.
    • Skills and hooks: SKILL.md skills load only when relevant (Nightly channel only); a stop hook in .cursor/hooks.json loops until .cursor/scratchpad.md says DONE, capped at 5 iterations.
    • TDD workflow: Write tests, confirm they fail and commit them, then have the agent implement without changing the tests.
    • Commands: Reusable /pr, /fix-issue, /review and /update-deps live in .cursor/commands/ and use gh.
    • Parallel and cloud: Git worktrees isolate parallel agents, one prompt can run on several models side by side, and cloud agents work in remote sandboxes and open PRs.
    • Caveats: This is vendor content, agent code can look right but be subtly wrong, and loops need iteration caps and checkable success criteria.

    Takeaway: Give agents checkable goals such as tests, linters and type checks, keep rules short and versioned in git, and cap any autonomous loop with an iteration limit.

  • Running Renovate as a GitHub Action (and NO PAT!)
    summary

    TL;DR: Chainguard shows how to run Renovate as a scheduled GitHub Action, using Octo STS for short-lived tokens so no long-lived personal access token is stored.

    • Why an Action: Suits small projects with little configuration and no extra infrastructure, but scheduled runs pick up new versions later than hosted Mend or a self-hosted setup.
    • Auth problem: The official docs suggest a fine-grained PAT, which is a long-lived credential that attackers target.
    • Default token limit: The built-in Actions token cannot update GitHub Actions workflows, which rules it out for many teams.
    • How it works: Octo STS exchanges the workflow’s OIDC token for a scoped GitHub token after it checks issuer and subject against .github/chainguard/renovate.sts.yaml.
    • Setup: Install the Octo STS app on the repo, add .github/workflows/renovate.yaml with a nightly 3 AM cron and manual trigger, add the trust policy and renovate.json.
    • Config hardening: Pin container images and Actions to digests, and set minimumReleaseAge to 1 day so you skip malicious releases that get pulled quickly.
    • Automerge: Enabled for everything except major versions in the example, so it is a deliberate trade-off against human review.
    • Caveats: The Octo STS app asks for broad privileges, though you can limit it to chosen repos; closing a PR stops Renovate reopening that update.

    Takeaway: Replace Renovate PATs with OIDC-based token exchange such as Octo STS, pin dependencies to digests and add a minimumReleaseAge cool-down before enabling automerge.

31.12.2025

  • All Paths Lead to Your Cloud: A Mapping of Initial Access Vectors to Your AWS Environment
    summary

    TL;DR: Palo Alto Networks maps how AWS initial access happens through misconfiguration rather than exploits, grouped into publicly exposed services and misconfigured access-control services like IAM, IoT and Cognito.

    • Thesis: The cloud perimeter breaks through two classes of mistake: service exposure (public or shadow resources) and access-by-design services that are configured wrong.
    • Lambda: Invoking a function needs only network reachability (the default) plus a resource policy for any principal. Read and write go through AWS APIs, which can’t easily be limited by network.
    • ECR: The authors brute-forced repository names from popular Docker Hub names and found public registries. A single public allow, such as ecr:PutImage, can poison the image supply chain.
    • DataSync: If a location bucket is deleted and recreated in another account, syncs keep flowing to it. The resourceaccount condition in the autogenerated service role blocks this.
    • IAM/STS: Main risks are Principal: * trust policies, OIDC trusts without sub/aud conditions, third-party roles without ExternalId, and leaked long-lived keys. 97% of customers have IAM users.
    • IoT: The chain thing → certificate → policy → roleAlias → IAM role lets a device certificate get role credentials via iot:AssumeRoleWithCertificate.
    • Cognito: Used by 84% of organizations. Self-registration is on by default, user pools don’t enforce per-app authorization, and unauthenticated identity pools hand out guest credentials.
    • Scope: Over-privileged identities, CVEs and Cognito group-to-role mappings are out of scope. The post surveys common patterns and is not exhaustive.

    Takeaway: Audit resource policies and trust policies for * principals, OIDC trusts missing sub/aud, open Cognito sign-up and guest access, and DataSync roles missing resourceaccount.

  • Exploiting AWS IAM Eventual Consistency for Persistence
    summary

    TL;DR: OFFENSAI shows that AWS IAM eventual consistency leaves a window of about 4 seconds in which revoked credentials still work, so attackers can persist and IAM-policy containment fails.

    • Finding: Deleted access keys stayed usable for 3 to 4 seconds in tests across us-east-1 and eu-central-1.
    • Persistence: During that window a deleted key could still call CreateAccessKey and mint a fresh key pair.
    • Detection by attacker: Polling ListAccessKeys about every 3 seconds shows an empty list once deletion propagates, which signals when to act.
    • Containment fails: Attaching AWSDenyAll to the compromised user doesn’t help, because the attacker can see the policy and detach it inside the window.
    • Scope: The same lag affects policy attach/detach, role creation and deletion, role-assumption permissions and login profiles.
    • AWS fix: AWS says this is distributed-system behavior, not a vulnerability. A December 2025 retest found deleted keys can no longer create new keys, but other changes inside the window still work, such as creating an externally assumable admin role.
    • Mitigation: Apply an Organization SCP that denies everything for the principal via aws:PrincipalArn, wait out propagation, then rotate keys and clean up policies.
    • Caveats: The 4-second figure comes from the authors’ tests. They found no in-the-wild exploitation, and the vendor sells detection products.

    Takeaway: Update IR playbooks to contain compromised IAM principals with an SCP first, not IAM policy changes. Tune CloudTrail detections for activity by already-deleted keys, and drop long-term access keys.

  • Things You Wish You Didn’t Need to Know About AWS Service-Linked Roles
    summary

    TL;DR: Daniel Grzelak of Plerion digs into AWS service-linked roles, AWS-owned roles you can’t edit, and finds enumeration, list-by-proxy, tag-collision and privilege-escalation risks.

    • What SLRs are: They live under /aws-service-role/ with names starting AWSServiceRoleFor. You create them with create-service-linked-role, and only the description is editable.
    • Template enumeration: The undocumented iamv2 method getServiceLinkedRoleTemplate returns the trust policy, managed policies and name prefix for a given service.
    • Usage probing: SLR names are predictable, so principal-validation errors reveal which services another account uses. The defense is to create every available SLR.
    • List by proxy: With everything denied except iam:DeleteServiceLinkedRole, a failed deletion’s RoleUsageList still exposed five SageMaker domain ARNs.
    • Confused deputy: No SLR trust policy reviewed had a Condition block. iam:PassRole rejects SLRs, which appears to block the attack.
    • Tag and name scoping: About 60 tag keys such as Owner and CreatedBy, and bucket prefixes such as amazon-connect-*, are unreserved, so customer resources can collide with them.
    • Over-broad grants: The RDS SLR gets EC2 network actions on *. The License Manager Linux subscriptions SLR can read any secret. MSK can PutResourcePolicy on AmazonMSK_* secrets.
    • Privesc paths: The Application Migration Service SLR can modify user data and restart instances. The Directory Service SLR can ssm:SendCommand AWS-RunPowerShellScript.
    • Caveats: The author reports no vulnerabilities, and the service list is incomplete.

    Takeaway: List your SLRs, check that tags like Owner and AWS-reserved bucket prefixes don’t collide with SLR conditions, and treat SLR policies as part of your privilege-escalation graph.

  • Simplify IAM Policy Creation with IAM Policy Autopilot, a New Open Source MCP Server for Builders
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS released IAM Policy Autopilot, an open-source local MCP server and CLI that reads application code deterministically to generate and troubleshoot IAM identity-based policies for AI coding assistants.

    • Problem: Developers start with broad permissions, and AI assistants miss actions or invent invalid ones.
    • How it works: Static analysis maps AWS SDK calls in code to IAM actions, using the published AWS service reference data.
    • Workflows: It generates an initial policy, proposes fixes for Access Denied errors (applied after user approval), and regenerates the policy when code changes.
    • Languages: Python, TypeScript and Go.
    • Clients: Kiro, Claude Code, Cursor and Cline. A standalone CLI is also available.
    • Output: Policies can go into CloudFormation, CDK or Terraform, and can be checked with IAM Access Analyzer validation.
    • Setup: Install with curl -sSL https://github.com/awslabs/iam-policy-autopilot/raw/refs/heads/main/install.sh | sudo sh, then register the MCP server in your IDE.
    • Limits: It generates only identity-based policies, not resource policies, boundaries, SCPs or RCPs. It favors working permissions over least privilege.

    Takeaway: Use it for a first-draft policy, then tighten it and run Access Analyzer before deploying. Review the install script rather than piping it straight to sudo sh.

  • Top AWS re:Invent Announcements for Security Teams in 2025
    summary

    TL;DR: Wiz picks the AWS re:Invent 2025 announcements that matter most to security teams, led by browser-based CLI login, outbound JWT federation and direct account transfers between Organizations.

    • aws login: Gets CLI credentials from a browser console session. An SDK change also records in the user-agent how credentials were obtained, so CloudTrail shows the source.
    • Outbound identity federation: AWS principals get JWTs to authenticate to non-AWS services. The JWTs carry the Org ID, OU path and principal tags, replacing pre-signed sts get-caller-identity tricks.
    • Account transfers: Accounts move directly between Organizations without going standalone, so they keep SCP coverage and org CloudTrail logging during the move.
    • Flat-rate plans: Fixed pricing with no overages for static-website services, plus a free tier with 5GB of S3 and WAF.
    • IAM Policy Autopilot: An open-source MCP server that generates IAM policies with deterministic analysis, not an LLM.
    • Temporary delegation: Currently limited to ISV Accelerate partner onboarding. The author speculates it could become groundwork for just-in-time access.
    • Org-level S3 Block Public Access: Moves the account-level setting up to the Organization.
    • Other: A managed TLS inspection proxy, and re:Inforce folded into re:Invent, announced only via an FAQ update.

    Takeaway: Adopt outbound JWT federation to drop static third-party secrets. Enforce S3 Block Public Access at the Org level, and use credential-source user-agent data in CloudTrail detections.

  • re:Invent 2025 Recap
    summary

    TL;DR: Chris Farris gives a skeptical, governance-focused recap of AWS re:Invent 2025, calling it underwhelming and GenAI-heavy while flagging the security and cost features worth adopting.

    • IAM: He welcomes JWT outbound federation, aws login (for hobbyists; enterprises should use Identity Center), STS IPv6 and the SourceVpcArn condition key. He worries Policy Autopilot could hallucinate actions.
    • S3: Org-level Block Public Access is all-or-nothing across the four controls. ABAC on bucket tags fills an RCP gap. SSE-C gets disabled for new buckets from April 2026.
    • Detection: GuardDuty Extended Threat Detection now covers EC2 and ECS, and severity 9+ findings are useful signal. Security Hub CSPM still depends on AWS Config recorders.
    • CloudTrail: Data event aggregation adds roughly 30% to data event cost. Native CloudTrail-to-CloudWatch costs $0.75/GB.
    • Network: Regional NAT Gateway removes the need for public subnets, but can cost 2-3x in non-prod. VPC encryption controls cost $110/month per non-empty VPC and enforce mode blocks rather than encrypts.
    • EC2: He recommends turning on EBS Recycle Bin everywhere and locking it with SCPs. AMI ancestry may help OS end-of-life reporting.
    • Governance: He praises direct Org account transfers, multi-org billing, StackSets drift detection and required-tag validation for Terraform, CloudFormation and Pulumi.
    • Caveats: AWS Interconnect preview 1 Gbps links get deleted at GA. He finds the MCP server churn and DevOps Agent unconvincing.

    Takeaway: Enable EBS Recycle Bin and Org-level S3 BPA, plan the April 2026 SSE-C cutoff, and run the numbers before adopting regional NAT or CloudTrail data event aggregation.

  • Shai Hulud 2.0: Ongoing Supply Chain Attack
    summary

    TL;DR: Wiz tracks Shai-Hulud 2.0, a November 2025 npm worm that trojanized hundreds of packages, stole developer and CI secrets at install time and leaked them to public GitHub repositories.

    • Scope: Wiz cites roughly 700 compromised packages in one place and nearly 800 in another. Affected publishers include Zapier, ENS Domains, PostHog, Postman and AsyncAPI.
    • Leak volume: Over 25,000 repositories were created. A deduplicated sample held 775 GitHub tokens, 373 AWS, 300 GCP and 115 Azure credentials.
    • Execution: It runs in preinstall via setup_bun.js and bun_environment.js. It runs synchronously in CI, detected via GITHUB_ACTIONS and BUILDKITE, and in the background on laptops.
    • Cloud theft: Bundled AWS, Azure and GCP SDKs harvest config files, environment variables and IMDS. It dumps secret managers and tries STS role assumption.
    • GitHub exfiltration: It pushes formatter_123456789.yml on an add-linter-workflow-* branch to dump repo secrets as actionsSecrets.json, then deletes the evidence.
    • Persistence: It registers a self-hosted runner named SHA1HULUD and adds discussion.yaml, so opening a discussion runs a shell command.
    • Privesc: It attempts docker run --rm --privileged -v /:/host ubuntu to drop a sudoers file.
    • Follow-on abuse: Attackers used leaked AKIA keys and PATs, published private repos, and pushed about 3,200 spam repositories.

    Takeaway: Hunt for SHA1HULUD runners, discussion.yaml, and repositories with Shai-Hulud in the description. Rotate npm, GitHub and cloud credentials, and disable install scripts in CI.

  • Inside the GitHub Infrastructure Powering North Korea’s Contagious Interview npm Attacks
    summary

    TL;DR: Socket maps North Korea’s Contagious Interview campaign: fake job tests lure Web3 developers into installing typosquatted npm packages that load the OtterCookie stealer through Vercel and GitHub.

    • Numbers: At least 197 new malicious npm packages since October 10, 2025, with over 31,000 downloads. 15 packages were still live when the post was written.
    • Lure: Fake interviews and test assignments use cloned Web3 and DEX repositories such as dexproject, which wires in a malicious node-tailwind.
    • Loader: tailwind-magic clones tailwind-merge. Its postinstall and import-time code fetch a payload from a Vercel endpoint and run it with eval().
    • Infrastructure: Vercel stages the payload, a GitHub repository hosts the code, and a separate C2 server receives data. The stardev0914 account held 18 repositories.
    • Malware: OtterCookie v4 is an infostealer and RAT that overlaps with BeaverTail. It does VM detection, remote shell, keylogging, screenshots, clipboard theft, and wallet and browser credential theft.
    • Persistence: The Windows build uses Run keys and scheduled tasks. Worker modules run as detached Node.js processes.
    • Evasion: Hex-escaped strings are decoded at runtime, and the malware exits early inside sandboxes.
    • Caveats: Counts are lower bounds. The removed GitHub account’s techniques reappear under new accounts.

    Takeaway: Treat npm install as code execution. Never run interview take-home repositories on machines that hold secrets or wallets. Isolate build agents, restrict egress, and flag packages that eval network responses.

  • PromptPwnd: Prompt Injection Vulnerabilities in GitHub Actions Using AI Agents
    summary

    TL;DR: Aikido names PromptPwnd, a flaw class in which untrusted issue or PR text injected into AI agent prompts in GitHub Actions or GitLab CI makes privileged agents leak secrets or tamper with repositories.

    • Pattern: Untrusted input such as github.event.issue.title goes into the prompt. The agent then runs privileged tools, and secrets leak or workflows change.
    • No RCE needed: An injected instruction can make the agent write GITHUB_TOKEN into an issue body with gh issue edit.
    • Env vars don’t help: Passing input through environment variables stops shell injection, not prompt injection.
    • Gemini CLI: run-gemini-cli issue triage leaked GEMINI_API_KEY and GITHUB_TOKEN in a test fork. Google patched it within four days.
    • Claude Code Actions: Setting allowed_non_write_users: "*" is dangerous. Aikido could almost always leak the token whenever an attacker could trigger the workflow.
    • Codex and AI Inference: Codex is exploitable only with allow-users: "*" plus a non-default safety-strategy. GitHub AI Inference is exposed with enable-github-mcp: true.
    • Scope: Aikido says at least 5 Fortune 500 companies are affected. That figure is vendor-reported, and many details remain unpublished.
    • Tooling: Aikido released Opengrep detection rules in AikidoSec/opengrep-rules.

    Takeaway: Audit workflows that feed issue or PR text to AI agents. Keep write-capable tools and high-privilege tokens away from those agents, never allow untrusted triggers, and treat agent output as untrusted.

  • We Should All Be Using Dependency Cooldowns
    summary

    TL;DR: William Woodruff argues that dependency cooldowns, which delay adoption of new releases by days, would have blocked most recent open-source supply-chain attacks at almost no cost.

    • Thesis: Malicious versions are usually caught and pulled within hours or days, so waiting before upgrading avoids them.
    • Attack windows: Ultralytics 12h and 1h, rspack 1h, Nx 4h, web3.js 5h, chalk under 12h, tj-actions 3 days, Kong Ingress Controller about 10 days, xz-utils about 5 weeks.
    • Numbers: 8 of 10 windows were under a week. A 14-day cooldown would have blocked all of them except xz-utils.
    • Dependabot: Add cooldown: default-days: 7 to dependabot.yml.
    • Other tools: Renovate’s minimum release age, pnpm’s minimumReleaseAge, and uv’s exclude-newer (an absolute date cutoff). zizmor audits for a missing Dependabot cooldown.
    • Limits: Manual installs and bumps bypass bot cooldowns, so the author wants package managers to enforce them natively.
    • Caveats: The sample is small and the 80-90% risk-reduction estimate is informal. Patient attackers can outlast any cooldown, and cooldowns don’t address credential compromise.

    Takeaway: Set a 7 to 14 day cooldown in Dependabot or Renovate and in pnpm. Keep an override path for urgent security fixes.

  • Learnings from Recent npm Supply Chain Compromises
    summary

    TL;DR: Datadog Security Labs reviews three 2025 npm compromises (s1ngularity/Nx, debug/chalk, Shai-Hulud) and draws lessons on CI/CD triggers, phishing and long-lived tokens.

    • s1ngularity (Aug 26): A pull_request_target workflow leaked the Nx npm token. The telemetry.js post-install stealer pushed data to s1ngularity-repository repos and leaked over 2,000 secrets.
    • debug/chalk (Sep 8): A fake 2FA-reset email from npmjs[.]help phished the maintainer “Qix”. A wallet-swapping browser stealer reached 18+ packages, which were downloaded over 2.5 million times, with about $500 reported stolen.
    • Shai-Hulud (Sep 16): Tokens left unrotated after s1ngularity fed a self-replicating worm. It exfiltrated to webhook[.]site and flipped private repos public with a -migration suffix.
    • Total: The three incidents compromised more than 500 packages, with about three weeks between s1ngularity and Shai-Hulud.
    • Controls: Don’t run untrusted PR code with secrets, rotate tokens after incidents, and use fine-grained tokens with MFA.
    • Package age: Use a minimum package age setting; pnpm and yarn have one, but npm didn’t when this was written.
    • npm changes: Publishing will be limited to 2FA, 7-day granular tokens and trusted publishing, and TOTP is being phased out for FIDO.
    • Tools: Datadog rules github-actions/script-injection and github-actions/dangerous-trigger, plus GuardDog and Supply-Chain Firewall.

    Takeaway: Remove pull_request_target workflows that check out PR code, rotate every token after any compromise, and move npm publishing to trusted publishing.

  • Claude Is Competitive with Humans in (Some) Cyber Competitions
    summary

    TL;DR: Anthropic entered Claude in seven human cybersecurity competitions in 2025. It often finished in the top 25% and solved challenges fast, but failed the hardest CTFs and drifted on long tasks.

    • PicoCTF 2025: 297th of 10,460 teams (top 3%), with 32 of 41 challenges solved.
    • HackTheBox AI vs Human: 30th of 161 with 19 of 20 solved, despite a 32-minute late start.
    • Hard CTFs: It solved zero challenges at PlaidCTF and the DEF CON CTF Qualifier. About 70% of PlaidCTF teams also solved none.
    • Defense (CCDC): 10th of 28 in the WRCCDC Qualifier and 6th of 9 in the Regional, though the red team targeted it less aggressively than human teams.
    • Speed: When Claude solved a challenge, it matched or beat elite human teams. It kept pace with the fastest team for about the first 17 minutes.
    • What helped: Autonomous flag submission, Kali Linux tooling, a robust terminal interface, specialized personas and context summaries written to file.
    • Failure modes: An ASCII aquarium login banner flooded the context and the goal was lost. Summaries also drifted into philosophical musings about security.
    • Caveats: The sample is small, setups were tuned mid-event, and Claude didn’t face physical systems in CCDC.

    Takeaway: Assume AI agents can now automate basic exploitation fast, and patch the low-hanging vulnerabilities first. Long-horizon autonomous defense is not reliable yet.

  • Introducing CodeMender: An AI Agent for Code Security
    summary

    TL;DR: Google DeepMind’s CodeMender is a Gemini Deep Think agent that patches vulnerabilities and proactively hardens code. It has upstreamed 72 security fixes so far, all reviewed by humans.

    • Reactive: It finds root causes with a debugger, code browser, static and dynamic analysis, fuzzing, differential testing and SMT solvers.
    • Proactive: It rewrites code to remove whole bug classes, for example adding -fbounds-safety annotations to libwebp.
    • libwebp: The authors say those annotations would have made CVE-2023-4863, used in a zero-click iOS exploit, unexploitable. This is a projection, not a measurement.
    • Multi-agent: An LLM critique agent diffs original and patched code to catch regressions, and the agent self-corrects compile and test failures.
    • Validation: Patches are checked for root-cause fix, functional correctness, regressions and style. Only patches that pass go to human review.
    • Numbers: 72 fixes upstreamed over about six months, in projects as large as 4.5 million lines.
    • Example: A heap overflow crash traced back to wrong XML element stack handling, fixed in a few lines.
    • Limits: These are early results with no benchmarks or success rates. The tool is not publicly available, and upstreaming is ramping up slowly.

    Takeaway: Watch it, but don’t plan around it: it isn’t available yet. Compiler-enforced bounds safety such as -fbounds-safety is worth evaluating now for C codebases you maintain.

  • WTF is AI-Native SAST?
    summary

    TL;DR: Parsia argues “AI-native SAST” is really SAST+AI: LLMs cannot replace static analysis yet, but static analysis pointing them at code beats either alone.

    • Thesis: Current LLMs need traditional static analysis to tell them where to look; combining both gives better results than either one.
    • Why now: Tokens are subsidized and cheap-ish, AI-generated code needs review, and AI may catch authorization and business-logic bugs SAST misses.
    • Hurdles: Cost (a cited test had GPT-5 mini take 10+ minutes to find 32 secrets), context rot, and non-deterministic answers needing repeat runs.
    • Context rot: Cited research suggests 500K and 1M context windows behave roughly like 64K in practice.
    • Blueprint: Feed the model input, a prompt (objective), RAG (vulnerability-class background), and context (surrounding code or data flow).
    • Technique ladder: Prompt+code, then prompt+agent with grep, then tailored prompts on SAST results, then agent+code graph+SAST MCP.
    • SAST-first tricks: Semgrep “hotspot” rules cut tokens; long CodeQL flows (20+ steps) caused hallucinations, so split them per function.
    • Tools named: Semgrep, CodeQL, tree-sitter, ZeroPath, Slice (CodeQL+tree-sitter, triage with gpt-5-mini then GPT-5), hound, Anthropic /security-review, OpenAI Aardvark.
    • Caveats: Personal, anecdotal experiments (“YMMV”); embeddings and code-intent approaches for logic bugs remain unproven.

    Takeaway: Run Semgrep/CodeQL first and use cheap models to triage their output before escalating survivors to stronger models; do not trust one-shot “find bugs” prompts.

  • RAPTOR - Autonomous Offensive/Defensive Security Research Framework
    summary

    TL;DR: RAPTOR is an open-source autonomous security research framework on Claude Code that scans, validates, exploits and patches vulnerabilities, by Gadi Evron, Thomas Dullien and others.

    • Architecture: Python layer (raptor.py) runs scanners, parses SARIF, dedupes and tracks cost; Claude Code layer (CLAUDE.md, .claude/) makes judgment calls.
    • Pipeline: /understand maps entry points and sinks; /agentic scans, then validates findings through stages A-F before generating PoCs and patches.
    • Commands: /scan, /codeql, /validate, /fuzz (AFL++), /ghidra, /frida, /sca (KEV, EPSS, CycloneDX SBOM), /web (ffuf, nuclei), /cve-diff.
    • Rules: 200+ custom rules: about 150 Semgrep, 68 Coccinelle for C/C++, and 8 CodeQL queries.
    • Models and cost: Supports Claude, OpenAI, Gemini, Mistral, Ollama; default $10 cap per run via --max-cost-usd; /agentic on ~50 findings costs about $2-8.
    • Sandboxing: Untrusted-content subprocesses run under Linux namespaces, Landlock and seccomp with no network; injection-prone env vars are stripped.
    • Setup: Needs uv, Python 3.12+, Node 18+, Semgrep; CodeQL and Z3 optional; ~6 GB Docker image available; standalone mode works without Claude Code.
    • Caveats: Self-described unpolished and mostly AI-generated; binary results are candidates, not proof; CodeQL license forbids commercial use.

    Takeaway: Try RAPTOR in its container against your own repos for triaged Semgrep/CodeQL findings, but verify every exploit claim and check licenses before commercial use.

  • What Are Atomic Detection Rules?
    summary

    TL;DR: Detection Engineering field manual explaining atomic detections, single-indicator rules on IPs, domains, hashes or usernames, and why they are cheap but brittle and noisy.

    • Definition: Atomic detections are “narrowly defined rules that detect activity at a point in time with little to no context”.
    • Framework: Uses David Bianco’s Pyramid of Pain; indicators at the bottom are cheap for attackers to change, TTPs at the top are costly.
    • Example 1: A rule on user = admin AWS logins fired three times; only one was a true positive, the rest normal admin work.
    • Example 2: A C2 IP rule went stale after the IP was reassigned to a CDN, turning every later hit into a false positive.
    • Pros: Cheap and fast to write and deploy; good for quick exposure checks against newly published indicators.
    • Cons: One changed bit in a hash or new IP defeats them; high false positives; investigation load grows with alert volume.
    • Trade-off: Adding context such as timestamps, related domains or location cuts noise but costs time, maintenance and threat intel.
    • Limits: No rule is perfect; catching everything means alerting on everything, and defenders lack visibility into attacker choices.

    Takeaway: Use IOC-only rules for short-lived exposure sweeps, give them expiry, and invest sustained alerting effort in behavior- and context-based detections.

  • Enumerating AWS the quiet way: CloudTrail-free discovery with Resource Explorer
    summary

    TL;DR: Datadog Security Labs showed attackers could enumerate an AWS account via Resource Explorer ListResources without default CloudTrail logging, until AWS reclassified it in July 2025.

    • Technique: Query Resource Explorer’s index instead of many List*/Describe* calls; one ListResources call can return most resources.
    • Laundering: Crawling is done by the AWSServiceRoleForResourceExplorer service-linked role, not the compromised identity.
    • Logging gap: ListResources was a data event, logged only if data-event logging was enabled; Search, ListViews, GetIndex were already management events.
    • Fix: Since July 15, 2025, ListResources is a management event, logged by default in Event History and management trails.
    • Activation: Attackers can call CreateIndex to turn Resource Explorer on in a region and start discovery.
    • Timeline: Reported April 24, 2025; AWS Health notice May 31; reclassified July 15; published August 19.
    • Detection: Alert on CreateIndex and on ListResources from long-lived access keys or new principals.
    • Mitigation: SCP denying resource-explorer-2:* where unused, at the cost of losing console search Resources results.

    Takeaway: Deny resource-explorer-2:* via SCP in accounts that do not use it, remove now-redundant data-event selectors, and alert on CreateIndex/ListResources from access keys.

  • IAM SAR-ry: Deep Dive and Nuances of AWS’s Programmatic IAM Action List and Service Authorization References
    summary

    TL;DR: Fog Security compares AWS’s new machine-readable service reference JSON with the Service Authorization Reference pages and finds classification differences that can break IAM tooling.

    • Background: The SAR is AWS’s human-readable list of actions, resources, condition keys and access levels; tools used to scrape it.
    • Programmatic source: JSON released late 2024; resources and condition keys added March 2025; action annotations added June 2025.
    • Annotations: IsList, IsPermissionsManagement, IsTaggingOnly, IsWrite; there is no Read annotation.
    • Multi-classification: 355 actions are both Permissions Management and Write; 670 are both Write and TaggingOnly; 1,023 gained categories vs SAR.
    • Example: sqs:AddPermission and sqs:SetQueueAttributes can both change queue policy; the JSON tags both Permissions Management and Write.
    • Mismatches: 118 duplicate actions in SAR from versioned services; 27 actions only in the JSON, mostly IoT 1-Click.
    • Tooling: Results published as JSON in Fog Security’s aws-iam GitHub repo; permissions.cloud previously relied on SAR scraping.
    • Caveats: Snapshot as of July 10, 2025; Read is inferred from unannotated actions, not confirmed by AWS.

    Takeaway: If your IAM linting or least-privilege tooling reads access levels, validate it against both sources before migrating, and treat Write as overlapping Permissions Management.

  • Stealthy Persistence in AWS - A Practical Simulation for Defenders
    summary

    TL;DR: A defender-oriented lab that simulates AWS persistence via a Lambda behind API Gateway, hidden in a non-$LATEST version, exfiltrating its role credentials to S3.

    • Scenario: Attacker with limited IAM access builds a Lambda that dumps its temporary credentials from environment variables into an attacker S3 bucket.
    • Why stealthy: Avoids heavily monitored actions like creating new IAM users.
    • Backdoor trigger: HTTP API Gateway route such as /backdoor with a GET method integrated with the Lambda.
    • Concealment: Benign code stays on $LATEST; malicious code is published as a numbered version and invoked by its version ARN.
    • Lab steps: Minimal-permission test user, locked-down bucket, Python handler, API Gateway route, publish benign then malicious version.
    • Detection: Alert on CreateFunction, UpdateFunctionCode, PublishVersion, API Gateway route changes, and unexpected PutObject targets.
    • Key signal: Track Lambda invocations per version; old or inactive versions suddenly receiving traffic are suspicious.
    • Prevention: Least-privilege execution roles, API Gateway authorizers and WAF, SCPs, GuardDuty and Security Hub.
    • Caveats: No statistics; text and code disagree on /proc/self/environ vs os.environ; credential lifetime is not discussed.

    Takeaway: Monitor PublishVersion and per-version invocation metrics, and review all Lambda versions and aliases, not only $LATEST, during audits.
