  • Avoiding AWS Control Plane Overload During EKS Upgrades: The 20% Roll Rate Strategy
    summary

    TL;DR: An AWS Builder Center post by Todd Bevins recommending capping concurrent EKS node replacements at 20% to avoid AWS API throttling during upgrades.

    • Problem: Replacing all nodes at once creates API bursts from draining, terminating, provisioning and joining nodes.
    • Symptoms: ThrottlingException, HTTP 429, rising latency, failed node registration, stalled Auto Scaling, pods stuck Terminating, ghost nodes.
    • Compounding: Retries pile onto the backlog; stateful workloads risk issues if volumes do not detach and reattach cleanly.
    • Rule: Limit concurrent replacements to 20% of nodes, e.g. 20 at a time for a 100-node cluster.
    • Waves: Split node groups into waves and let each stabilize before the next.
    • Config: Set managed node group update constraints to max 20% unavailable; apply the same limit for kOps or custom scripts.
    • Monitoring: Watch CloudWatch ThrottledRequests, alarm on API error rates, pause when throttling grows; use exponential backoff in automation.
    • Caveats: The 20% figure is called “proven” without data; the 150-node, ~$2.3M outage anecdote is unsourced; config and code are only images.

    Takeaway: Cap how many nodes upgrade at once, roll in waves, and alarm on API throttling during EKS upgrades; tune the percentage to cluster size.

  • Kubernetes security fundamentals: Networking
    summary

    TL;DR: Part 6 of Datadog’s Kubernetes security fundamentals series by Rory McCune covers cluster networking: flat pod networks, CNI choice, and NetworkPolicy with a Calico demo.

    • Problem: By default “all pods are able to communicate with all other pods”, giving a flat, unrestricted network.
    • Trust zones: Node network (can reach cloud metadata) and pod network; managed clusters (EKS, AKS, GKE) hide the control-plane network.
    • CNI matters: kindnet and flannel do not enforce NetworkPolicy; Cilium and Calico do, plus extensions.
    • Policy model: Pods are open until a policy selects them; then only explicitly allowed ingress or egress passes, including within a namespace.
    • Demo: kind cluster with disableDefaultCNI: true plus Calico; nginx and postgres in netpol-demo tested from a client pod via curl.
    • Policies shown: default-deny-ingress, allow TCP 80 from namespaces labeled purpose: frontend, allow TCP 5432 only from app: web.
    • Enforcement: Calico implements policies as iptables cali-pi-... chains; iptables-differ helps view changes.
    • Egress: Unrestricted egress lets a compromised pod reach the cluster or cloud via the instance metadata service.
    • Host networking: hostNetwork pods bypass standard policies; restrict them or use Cilium’s host firewall.

    Takeaway: Ship default-deny ingress and egress NetworkPolicies with each app, confirm your CNI enforces them, and block pod access to the metadata endpoint.

  • How I Scanned all of GitHub’s “Oops Commits” for Leaked Secrets
    summary

    TL;DR: A Truffle Security guest post on mining GH Archive for force-pushed “Oops commits” since 2020, finding thousands of live secrets and about $25k in bounties.

    • Premise: Force-pushed commits stay reachable on GitHub by hash; the author believes GitHub keeps them indefinitely but could not verify.
    • Signal: Zero-commit PushEvent entries in GH Archive mark force pushes; the before field gives the overwritten commit hash.
    • Method: Minimal clone without blobs or checkout, fetch the before commit, scan with TruffleHog; .patch/.diff URLs also work.
    • Tool: Open-source Force Push Scanner queries GH Archive in BigQuery and scans dangling commits so orgs can check their own exposure.
    • Findings: MongoDB credentials leaked most; GitHub PATs and AWS keys were most valuable; .env was the top leaking file.
    • Case study: A dotfiles-leaked PAT had admin on all Istio repos (~36k stars), enabling pipeline changes, releases or repo deletion; it was revoked quickly.
    • Age effect: Older secrets were much less likely to still be active.
    • Caveats: Public repos only; “thousands” is approximate; GitHub WAF may block heavy Git access; REST limit 5,000 req/hour authenticated.

    Takeaway: Treat any committed secret as compromised and revoke it; deleting or force-pushing does not help. Run Force Push Scanner against your org.

  • My AWS Account Got Hacked - Here is What Happened
    summary

    TL;DR: A first-hand incident write-up of a personal AWS account compromised via an access key hardcoded in Next.js SSR code, leading to IAM persistence, EC2 abuse and attempted SES phishing.

    • Root cause: An S3 access key was hardcoded during development; the author did not realize SSR code was exposed to the browser.
    • Initial access: Attacker created an admin IAM user, then waited about six hours, likely to avoid alerts.
    • Compute abuse: Large Ubuntu 24.04 EC2 instance launched in us-east-2 with SSH open; crypto-mining suspected, unconfirmed; spend rose over 400%.
    • Persistence: Four more IAM users created, one named after the author.
    • Distraction: A spam flood (70+ emails in an hour) aimed to bury AWS notification emails.

  • Fantastic AWS Policies and Where to Find Them
    summary

    TL;DR: A catalog of every place AWS access policies live, from identity policies and SCPs/RCPs to resource, “resource-ish”, RAM and Identity Center policies, plus a collection tool.

    • Principal policies: AWS/customer managed, inline user/role/group policies, permission boundaries and SCPs; boundaries and SCPs only limit, never grant.
    • Required resource policies: KMS key policies and IAM role trust policies; without a key policy grant, even root is denied.
    • Optional resource policies: VPC endpoint (allow-all by default), S3 bucket and access points, Organizations resource policy, SES sending authorization.
    • Coverage: About 27 services with resource policies listed, including Lambda, SQS, SNS, ECR, Secrets Manager, DynamoDB.
    • Resource-ish: Entity Resolution, IoT, CloudWatch account policies for log groups, and per-region Glue policies.
    • RCPs: Organization policies applied to resources, the counterpart of SCPs for principals.
    • Sharing: RAM shares 70+ resource types like subnets; Identity Center permission sets combine across all assignments; delegated admins matter.
    • Tool: @actsecurity/iam-collect (init, download) pulls IAM, org, resource, RAM and SSO policies into iam-data; IAM Lens analyzes effective access.
    • Caveats: SCPs can deny by omitting an Allow; S3 access points add their own policies; collected policies do not show effective access.

    Takeaway: When auditing access, inventory all these policy types across accounts, not just IAM roles; check VPC endpoint, KMS and RAM shares explicitly.

  • IAMhounddog: Identify Privileged Principals and Privilege Escalation in AWS
    summary

    TL;DR: IAMhounddog is a Go tool from Virtue Security that maps AWS IAM into BloodHound to expose privileged principals and second-order privilege escalation paths in unfamiliar accounts.

    • Problem: Tools like Cloudsplaining catch first-order issues such as iam:PutRolePolicy, but miss indirect paths through resources a principal can modify.
    • Example: A user in a group with Lambda full access can modify a function whose role has AdministratorAccess, inheriting admin indirectly.
    • How it works: Enumerates the account and writes output.json in BloodHound OpenGraph format for ingestion into BloodHound Community Edition.
    • Coverage: IAM users, roles, groups, inline and attached policies, trust relationships, Cognito identity pools, S3 bucket policies, and roles attached to EC2, ECS, EKS, Lambda, Glue, SageMaker and more.
    • Data model: Edges like awsAssumeRoleAllowed, iamPassRoleAllowed and action edges such as ec2RunInstances; s3:* is remapped to s3AllAccess.
    • Setup: go install github.com/VirtueSecurity/IAMhounddog@latest; optional -setup with a BloodHound API key installs icons and bundled Cypher queries.
    • Options: -profile, -regions (or -regions describe), -onlyroles, -onlyservices, -output.
    • Requirements: A principal with SecurityAudit or ReadOnlyAccess; results are limited to what that principal can read and the listed services.
    • AI use: The JSON can be fed to an AI agent with a sample prompt to enumerate escalation paths in plain language.

    Takeaway: Run IAMhounddog with a read-only audit role during AWS assessments to find indirect escalation via modifiable compute resources, then remove write access to resources carrying privileged roles.

  • AWS Detection Engineering: Mastering Log Sources for Threat Detection
    summary

    TL;DR: A detection engineer’s walkthrough of rebuilding AWS threat detection after a purple team found two weeks of undetected persistence, covering which log sources matter and sample Sigma-style rules.

    • Trigger: A purple team exercise showed an attacker persisting nearly two weeks undetected despite logging being “enabled”.
    • Foundation: CloudTrail and VPC Flow Logs come first; GuardDuty adds fast value but should not be the only layer.
    • Other sources: ALB access logs for web attacks, AWS Config for changes like disabled S3 public access blocks.
    • Cost: VPC Flow Logs and S3 Access Logs can generate massive volumes, so budget for them.
    • Sample rules: Suspicious CreateRole with external principals, bursts of ListAccessKeys/CreateAccessKey, root usage, console logins from new countries, DNS tunneling on port 53.
    • Tiering: Critical alerts get immediate response, high within 1 hour, medium within 4 hours.
    • Correlation: Python examples score matches across CloudTrail, flow logs and GuardDuty by time, source IP and resource.
    • Response: Automated playbooks attach AWSDenyAll and deactivate access keys for critical alerts.
    • Lessons: Context enrichment, automation, false-positive tuning, detection-as-code and continuous rule validation against true and false positive datasets.

    Takeaway: Prioritize CloudTrail plus VPC Flow Logs, manage detections as code, and continuously test rules against known attack data to prevent detection decay.

  • AWS CloudTrail Event Cheatsheet: A Detection Engineer’s Guide to Critical API Calls
    summary

    TL;DR: A cheatsheet mapping high-risk AWS CloudTrail event names to MITRE ATT&CK tactics, with attacker CLI commands and SQL detection queries for junior detection engineers.

    • Structure: Each tactic lists key events, the aws CLI commands an attacker runs, and a matching CloudTrail SQL query.
    • Initial access: ConsoleLogin, PasswordRecoveryRequested, AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity; alert on more than 5 failed logins per IP.
    • Execution: SendCommand, Invoke, RunInstances; flag SSM commands containing curl piped to bash.

    • Exfiltration: ModifySnapshotAttribute, ModifyImageAttribute, ModifyDBSnapshotAttribute sharing to foreign accounts, PutBucketPolicy.
    • Impact: PutBucketVersioning suspended, DeleteAccountPublicAccessBlock, DeleteDBInstance, large c5 instance launches for mining.

    Takeaway: Alert on logging tampering and cross-account snapshot or AMI sharing first, and use the per-tactic event lists as a baseline for CloudTrail detection coverage.

  • Implementing granular failover in multi-Region Amazon EKS
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS shows how per-application Route 53 health checks let apps on shared multi-Region EKS clusters fail over independently instead of moving every app when one breaks.

    • Problem: Route 53 “Evaluate target health” on an ALB alias marks the whole ALB unhealthy if any target group fails, moving healthy apps too.
    • Architecture: Two Regions (eu-west-1, eu-central-1), EKS Auto Mode, AWS Load Balancer Controller with IP targets, latency-based Route 53 alias records.
    • How it works: Create an HTTP health check per app per Region against its own path, attach it to the alias, and set “Evaluate target health” to No.
    • Numbers: Four health checks for two apps, 30-second interval, failure threshold of 3; failover takes several minutes with default timers.
    • Test: Scaling app1 to zero replicas moves only app1 traffic to the other Region while app2 stays put.
    • Limits: ALB rule quotas cap apps per ALB, each app adds health check cost, and it does not work for path-based routing on one domain.
    • Caveats: HTTPS health checks do not validate TLS certificates; Route 53 checkers cannot reach private IPs, needing CloudWatch alarms or Lambda instead.
    • Gotcha: The console defaults “Evaluate target health” to Yes; the CLI requires setting it explicitly.

    Takeaway: On shared multi-Region clusters, attach app-specific health checks to Route 53 aliases and disable ALB-level target health evaluation so one failing app does not drag others cross-Region.

    summary

    TL;DR: A new Headlamp UI plugin gives real-time visibility into Karpenter node provisioning, scaling decisions and pending pods, aimed at debugging autoscaling behavior.

    • Context: Headlamp is a Kubernetes SIG UI project; Karpenter is a SIG Autoscaling node provisioner that launches and removes nodes.

    • Config editor: Live edits to Karpenter resources with diff previews and validation.
    • Pending pods: Dashboard highlights unmet scheduling requirements and FailedScheduling reasons.
    • Providers: Tested only on AWS and Azure; AlibabaCloud, GCP, Cluster API, Proxmox, OCI and Bizfly are untested.
    • Origin: Built as an LFX mentorship project; install steps live in plugins/karpenter/README.md.

    Takeaway: If you run Karpenter on EKS or AKS, add the Headlamp plugin to debug why pods stay pending and why specific instance types were chosen.

  • Kubernetes Gateway API in action
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS demonstrates Kubernetes Gateway API on EKS for ingress, weighted canary in a mesh, and default-deny egress, using Envoy Gateway and Linkerd instead of vendor annotations.

    • Problem: Ingress and Services need third-party CRDs or annotations for canaries and egress, and blur operator versus developer ownership.
    • Stack: Amazon EKS, Envoy Gateway, Linkerd, ExternalDNS with Route 53, and an internet-facing NLB via AWS Load Balancer Controller.
    • Role split: Gateway defines infrastructure owned by operators; HTTPRoute attaches via parentRefs and is owned by developers.
    • North-south: An HTTPRoute routes www.cal-llm.com to the web-app service on port 8080.
    • East-west: A GRPCRoute on the calendar Service splits 40% to calendar and 60% to calendar-v2 (GAMMA).
    • Egress: A Linkerd EgressNetwork named deny-all blocks outbound traffic, and a TLSRoute allows only api.mistral.ai:443.
    • Caveats: Egress is Linkerd-specific and enforced at proxies, so it can be bypassed; add NetworkPolicy as a second layer.
    • Versions: GRPCRoute and TLSRoute use v1alpha2 here and may change; no controller versions given.

    Takeaway: Use Gateway API to separate platform and app routing ownership, but back mesh-level egress allow-lists with Kubernetes Network Policies.

  • The GhostAction Campaign: 3,325 Secrets Stolen Through Compromised GitHub Workflows
    summary

    TL;DR: GitGuardian’s write-up of GhostAction, a GitHub supply chain campaign that injected malicious workflows into 817 repositories and stole 3,325 CI/CD secrets.

    • Technique: Compromised accounts pushed .github/workflows/github_actions_security.yml (“Add Github Actions Security workflow”) triggered on push and workflow_dispatch.
    • Exfiltration: A curl step POSTed secrets to an attacker host; secret names were harvested from existing workflows and hardcoded.
    • Numbers: 327 GitHub users, 817 repositories, 3,325 secrets; 24 packages (9 npm, 15 PyPI) at immediate risk.
    • Secrets stolen: Mostly DockerHub credentials, GitHub tokens and npm tokens, plus PyPI, AWS, Cloudflare and database credentials.
    • IOCs: bold-dhawan.45-139-104-115.plesk.page, carte-avantage.com, objective-hopper.45-139-104-115.plesk.page, IP 45.139.104.115.
    • Timeline: First found via the FastUUID maintainer on September 5, 2025; PyPI set the project read-only the same day.
    • Impact: No malicious packages published at disclosure, but victims confirmed active use of stolen AWS keys and database credentials.
    • Follow-up: A later update reports a second wave of about 500 commits, mostly to previously hit repos; the initial access vector remains unknown.

    Takeaway: Rotate any CI secret exposed to unreviewed workflow changes, require review on .github/workflows/, scope secrets per environment, and block the listed IOCs.

  • Release Tampering PoCs: How to Tamper with Releases built with GitHub Actions
    summary

    TL;DR: Proof-of-concept research showing how a malicious maintainer can tamper with GitHub Actions-built releases while hiding traces, plus what defenders and consumers can check.

    • Scope: Assumes the attacker already has maintainer access; focuses on hiding tampering across SLSA Source, Build and Distribution phases.
    • Origin: Accompanies the fwd:cloudsec Europe 2025 talk “Continuous Integration / Continuous Deception”.
    • Path 1: GitHub Releases are mutable by default, so assets can be swapped after publishing; ReleaseEvent still records the original hash.
    • Path 2: A typosquatted Action (actlons/checkout) runs after the real checkout and alters files, then is hidden via mutable tags and repo deletion.
    • Path 3: A job container from an attacker-controlled Docker Hub image (hardenrunner/ubuntu) wraps gh to tamper during release, then the image is swapped clean.
    • Further paths: Self-hosted runners labelled ubuntu-latest and orphaned commits in the actions/checkout network are listed but were not in the content read.
    • Detection: Compare Events API digests with current assets, within its 300-event / 30-day window; check lookalike repo creation dates.
    • Mitigations: Enable immutable releases and verify immutable: true, pin Actions to full SHAs, monitor image digests, and scan pipelines with poutine or zizmor.

    Takeaway: Consume only immutable releases, pin every Action and job container by digest, and treat recently created or deleted Action repos in your pipelines as red flags.

  • pull_request_nightmare: Exploiting GitHub Actions for RCE and Supply Chain Attacks
    summary

    TL;DR: Orca Research shows how insecure pull_request_target workflows let a single fork PR execute code with secrets and write tokens in repos at Google, Microsoft and other large organizations.

    • Root cause: pull_request_target runs in the base repo context with secrets and a typically write-enabled GITHUB_TOKEN, without manual approval by default.
    • Exploit: The workflow checks out github.event.pull_request.head.sha and runs attacker-controlled scripts on a privileged runner.
    • Mistake 1: Write-enabled tokens such as contents: write and pull-requests: write let untrusted contributors push code.
    • Mistake 2: Secrets reachable by the job can be exfiltrated.
    • Mistake 3: Self-hosted runners exposed to external contributors through auto-approved workflows.
    • Mistake 4: Legacy repos relying on broad tokens created before GitHub’s 2021 permission changes.
    • Impact: RCE on runners, secret theft, pushes to main, PR manipulation and publishing to org-level GitHub packages.
    • Caveats: No vulnerable repo counts or CVEs given; affected projects are unnamed and findings were responsibly disclosed.

    Takeaway: Never check out or run PR head code in pull_request_target jobs, set least-privilege permissions: explicitly, and keep self-hosted runners away from fork-triggered workflows.

  • GitHub Actions policy now supports blocking and SHA pinning actions
    summary

    TL;DR: GitHub’s allowed actions policy can now block specific actions or versions and enforce full-SHA pinning, giving admins a direct response to malicious third-party actions.

    • Blocking: Prefix an action or version entry with ! to block it in the allowed actions and reusable workflows policy.
    • Precedence: The blocklist is evaluated last and overrides any rule that would otherwise allow the action.
    • SHA pinning: A new checkbox requires actions to be referenced by full commit SHA, stopping silent pulls of new code via tags or branches.
    • Enforcement: Any workflow using an unpinned action fails once pinning is required.
    • Scope: Settings apply at enterprise, organization and repository levels.
    • Limit: The pinning checkbox is unavailable where actions are disabled at that level.
    • Roadmap: Immutable releases, whose assets and tags cannot be changed or deleted, with release attestations and Dependabot support.
    • Docs: Allowed actions docs and the GitHub Well-Architected Actions security guidance were updated to match.

    Takeaway: Pin actions to SHAs with Dependabot updates first, then enable the enforcement checkbox at org level and use ! blocks for known-compromised actions.

  • A Security Engineer’s Guide to MCP
    summary

    TL;DR: Semgrep’s primer on the Model Context Protocol for security engineers: its architecture, transports, MCP-specific attacks, and server and client hardening checklists.

    • What it is: A JSON-RPC 2.0 tool interface where the caller is an LLM, built from resources, tools, prompts, roots, elicitation and sampling.
    • STDIO transport: Local child process; audit for command injection and privilege escalation across tools with different privileges.
    • HTTP transport: All classic web and API bugs apply, including request smuggling, path traversal and injection.
    • Tool poisoning: Hidden instructions in the tools/list response influence the model before any tool is called.
    • Shadowing and rug pulls: Duplicate tool names across servers, and approved servers silently changed later, since the spec does not require re-approval.
    • Sampling abuse: No isolation is required, so a malicious server could learn other servers’ tool names and register collisions.
    • Real bug: Semgrep found a path traversal in its own early MCP server via improper root checking, limited by a low-privilege container user.
    • Server checklist: Validate Origin, rate-limit tool calls, validate paths against roots, and use OAuth 2.1 with audience validation for HTTP auth.
    • Client checklist: Treat tool descriptions and outputs as untrusted, require approval for sampling and first run, and use fully qualified tool names.

    Takeaway: Inspect MCP servers deterministically with MCP Inspector before trusting them, require auth on HTTP servers, and treat every tool description as untrusted input.

  • MCP Scanner: Scan MCP servers for potential threats and security findings
    summary

    TL;DR: Cisco AI Defense’s MCP Scanner is an Apache-2.0 Python tool that scans MCP servers, tools, prompts and packages for poisoning, malware and vulnerable dependencies.

    • Analyzers: YARA rules, LLM semantic analysis, Cisco AI Defense API, readiness heuristics (20 rules) and prompt-defense regex checks for 12 attack vectors.
    • Code analysis: A behavioral analyzer compares docstring claims to implementation with cross-file dataflow across languages including Python, Go, Rust and TypeScript.
    • Supply chain: VirusTotal hash lookups, pip-audit CVE checks, and Docker-sandboxed pypi-scan / npm-scan that never execute package code.
    • Commands: remote, stdio, config, known-configs (Cursor, Claude, VS Code, Windsurf), static for offline JSON in CI, and protocol probes.
    • Modes: CLI, REST API via mcp-scanner-api, and a Python SDK (mcpscanner).
    • Install: uv tool install cisco-ai-mcp-scanner; requires Python 3.11+.
    • LLMs: Tested with GPT-4o, GPT-4.1 and Bedrock Claude 4.5 Sonnet; local endpoints like Ollama work but need a placeholder key.
    • Limits: API analyzer needs a Cisco subscription; VirusTotal free tier allows 4 requests per minute and 500 per day.
    • Caveats: Heuristic checks flag missing defenses, not confirmed attacks; protocol probes do not call tools.

    Takeaway: Run mcp-scanner known-configs on developer machines and the static mode in CI to catch poisoned or vulnerable MCP servers before agents use them.

  • MCP Tools: Attack Vectors and Defense Recommendations for Autonomous Agents
    summary

    TL;DR: Elastic Security Labs catalogs attack vectors against MCP tools, from classic injection bugs to tool poisoning and orchestration attacks, and offers defensive recommendations.

    • Traditional bugs: MCP servers inherit command and SQL injection; a cited March 2025 study found high rates of command injection among public servers.
    • Tool poisoning: Hidden instructions in descriptions or parameters override behavior or pull system prompts, history and environment details.
    • Obfuscation: Invisible Unicode or Base64 hides instructions from human reviewers and keyword filters.
    • Implicit manipulation: A never-called tool’s description can alter how the model uses another tool, such as a payment tool.
    • Rug pulls: Tools approved in benign form are later redefined without triggering re-approval.
    • Name collision: Lookalike tools from different servers, such as a fake file reader that skips path validation.
    • Orchestration injection: Hidden instructions in GitHub issues or Jira tickets drive agents to leak private data, especially with “always allow” enabled.
    • Detection: A zero-shot LLM review of tool definitions flagged several examples but is explicitly a demo, not production-ready.
    • Defenses: Sandbox clients and servers in containers, apply least privilege, require human approval for sensitive actions and log every tool call.

    Takeaway: Disable auto-run and “always allow” in privileged agent contexts, sandbox MCP servers away from local credentials, and audit tool invocation logs regularly.

  • Can AI weaponize new CVEs in under 15 minutes?
    summary

    TL;DR: Researchers built an LLM agent pipeline that turns newly published CVEs into validated exploits in about 10-15 minutes for roughly $1 each, threatening defenders’ patch windows.

    • Thesis: With about 130 CVEs published daily, automated exploit generation could shrink the patch-to-exploit gap from weeks to minutes.
    • Stage 1: Pulls GitHub Advisories and NIST data, clones the repo, extracts the patch and writes a research report as agent context.
    • Stage 2: Separate agents write a vulnerable test app and an exploit, iterating against each other in Dagger sandboxes.
    • Validation: Exploits run against both vulnerable and patched versions to filter false positives.
    • Models: Claude Sonnet 4.0 worked best; Opus gave negligible gains at about five times the cost; local qwen3:8b and gpt-oss:20b were used for experiments.
    • Guardrails: Hosted models that first refused exploit work stopped refusing once a long step-by-step prompt chain was used.
    • Examples: CVE-2025-54887 (Ruby JWE bypass), GHSA-w2cq-g8g3-gm83 (JS prototype pollution), GHSA-9gvj-pp9x-gcfr (Python pickle sanitizer bypass).
    • Results: 10 working exploits at publication; no overall success rate or attempt count reported.
    • Limits: Small sample, author-reported speed and cost, hand-written environments, and complex CVEs likely harder.

    Takeaway: Assume public exploits can appear within minutes of a CVE advisory and shorten patch SLAs for exposed services, backed by compensating controls like WAF rules.

  • SSH LLM Honeypot caught a real threat actor
    summary

    TL;DR: A Beelzebub SSH honeypot backed by an LLM fooled a real attacker into staging a Perl IRC botnet, exposing its download host and C2 channels.

    • Setup: Beelzebub configured by one YAML file (ssh-2222.yaml) on port 2222, OpenSSH banner, weak passwords like root, 123456, jenkins accepted.
    • How it works: Command regex ^(.+)$ routes every command to the LLMHoneypot plugin, which asks gpt-4o (or local Llama) to fake shell output.
    • Attack: Login from 45.175.100.69 with admin/123456, recon via uname -a, uptime, nproc, then downloads into /tmp and /var/tmp.
    • Payloads: A Perl script disguised as sshd (about 84 KB) and emech.tar.gz (about 100 KB), fetched from a compromised old Joomla site, deep-fm.de.
    • Malware: “rootbox PerlBot v2.0” joins ix1.undernet.org:6667, channels #rootbox and #c0d3rs-TeaM, for remote command execution and DoS.
    • Follow-up: Author joined the IRC channels, saw the attacker active, and reported them to Undernet for takedown.
    • Caveats: Single incident; LLM-generated output may explain repeated failed executions; no hashes published; takedown outcome not confirmed.
    • Bias: Published by the vendor of Beelzebub, so framing is promotional.

    Takeaway: LLM-backed honeypots are cheap to deploy and can extract attacker tooling and C2 details; block weak SSH credentials and watch for downloads into /tmp and /var/tmp.

  • Detecting and countering misuse of AI: August 2025
    summary

    TL;DR: Anthropic’s August 2025 threat intel report shows criminals using Claude agentically for extortion, North Korean job fraud, and ransomware-as-a-service, lowering the skill bar for cybercrime.

    • Vibe hacking: One actor used Claude Code to automate recon, credential harvesting and intrusion against at least 17 organizations, including healthcare and government.
    • Extortion model: No encryption; threatened data leaks, with ransom demands sometimes over $500,000 set by Claude’s analysis of stolen financials.
    • DPRK fraud: North Korean operatives used Claude to build fake identities, pass coding tests and do the actual work at US Fortune 500 tech firms.
    • Why it matters: The scheme previously required years of training; AI lets operators with no coding or English skills keep jobs.
    • Ransomware-as-a-service: A low-skill actor built and sold ransomware variants with evasion and anti-recovery features for $400 to $1,200 on forums.
    • Response: Account bans, a tailored classifier, new malware-generation detection, and indicators shared with authorities and partners.
    • Key finding: Agentic AI now performs attacks, not just advises, and is used across every stage from victim profiling to data analysis.
    • Caveats: Actors unnamed; displayed ransom note and “profit plan” are simulations; claims about actor dependence on Claude are Anthropic’s assessment.

    Takeaway: Assume low-skill attackers can now run multi-stage intrusions and extortion; tighten remote-hire identity verification and treat data-theft extortion without encryption as a primary scenario.

  • Good CISO / Bad CISO
    summary

    TL;DR: Phil Venables and Mike Aiello contrast good and bad CISOs, arguing that ownership, strategy and business framing matter more than budget or tools.

    • Core thesis: A good CISO is a business executive managing technology risk; a bad one is an IT manager running security tools and making excuses.
    • Ownership: Good CISOs own resilience outcomes; bad ones blame funding, developers, users or the board.
    • Strategy: A plan or vendor list is not a strategy; good strategy sets the “what” and generates work for other teams.
    • Scaling: Build flywheels that lower the unit cost of control and make the secure path easy, instead of running a reactive “fire station”.
    • Vendors: Push vendors to build security in and reduce demand through risk avoidance, rather than buying more tools.
    • Communication: Speak in risk and capital terms, publish written positions, and treat risk as “Hazard + Outrage”.
    • Culture: Reward bad news early, develop successors and security champions, and avoid becoming a single point of failure.
    • Board: Teach the board the right meta-questions to create shared accountability, not status reports that defend budget.
    • Caveats: Opinion piece adapted from a16z’s “Good Product Manager, Bad Product Manager”; no data or statistics.

    Takeaway: Audit your security program against the table: replace ticket-count metrics with secure-by-default flywheels and write down your positions in business terms.

  • Multi-VPC Load Balancing for Amazon EKS Workloads
    summary

    TL;DR: Walkthrough for running EKS in one VPC while the AWS Load Balancer Controller provisions NLBs and ALBs in a separate, peered VPC.

    • Problem: The controller defaults to the cluster VPC, but perimeter isolation, compliance or a central network team may need load balancers elsewhere.
    • Architecture: VPC A holds the cluster in private subnets, VPC B holds public load balancers, joined by VPC peering (Transit Gateway mentioned only).
    • Key setting: Install Helm chart eks/aws-load-balancer-controller with vpcId set to VPC B’s ID.
    • IAM: Uses the eks-pod-identity-agent add-on and a Pod Identity association with the awsLoadBalancerController well-known policy.
    • Routing: Every route table in both VPCs needs a route to the other CIDR via the peering connection.
    • NLB: LoadBalancer Service with aws-load-balancer-type: "external", nlb-target-type: "ip", scheme: "internet-facing".
    • ALB: Ingress with ingressClassName: alb and alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/target-type: ip.
    • Requirements: Target type must be ip; VPC B subnets need kubernetes.io/role/elb or kubernetes.io/role/internal-elb tags.
    • Caveats: Mixes us-west-2 and us-east-1 in commands; skips CIDR planning, cross-VPC security groups and Transit Gateway details.

    Takeaway: To centralize ingress in a network VPC, set vpcId on the controller, use IP targets, tag subnets, and verify with aws elbv2 describe-load-balancers.

  • GitHub - hunvreus/devpush
    summary

    TL;DR: /dev/push is an MIT-licensed, self-hosted, Vercel-like deployment platform that builds and deploys any Docker workload from GitHub pushes.

    • What: Open-source alternative to Vercel, Render and Netlify, supporting Python, Node.js, PHP and anything that runs in Docker.
    • Features: Git push deploys, zero-downtime rollouts, instant rollback, branch-mapped environments and encrypted environment variables.

  • Lefthook: Fast and powerful Git hooks manager for any type of projects
    summary

    TL;DR: Lefthook is a fast, single-binary Git hooks manager from Evil Martians that runs per-file-filtered commands in parallel across any language ecosystem.

    • What: MIT-licensed Git hooks manager written in Go, usable in Node.js, Ruby, Python and other projects.

  • What is a Detection Engineer? - by Zack Allen
    summary

    TL;DR: Zack Allen’s Field Manual opener explains why Detection Engineering exists: finding attackers who bypassed preventive controls, which depends entirely on telemetry from known assets.

    • Definition: A blue-team specialist who detects threat actors infiltrating and moving through a network, successor to classic “threat detection”.
    • Framing: Mapped onto NIST CSF functions; the role focuses on Detect, sometimes Respond.
    • Core principle: “There is no detection without telemetry”, and no telemetry without identified assets, citing Locard’s Exchange Principle.
    • Loop 1: Ideal state where known assets and controls eliminate whole attack classes.
    • Loop 2: Hedge for when Loop 1 fails; detection lives here and aims to find malicious activity fast.
    • Loop 3: When containment fails to limit business impact; lessons feed back into Loops 1 and 2.
    • Examples: Credential phishing, malicious downloads from pirated-software searches, exploitation of internet-facing vulnerabilities.
    • Hiring: Production-grade coding, deep expertise in one area (OS, cloud, network, IR, threat intel), and a customer mindset toward IT, vuln management and SOC.
    • Caveats: Opinion based on the author’s experience leading nearly 50 people; loop model is his own framing; rules and alerting deferred to later posts.

    Takeaway: Start detection work with an asset inventory and telemetry coverage, then push lessons from incidents back into preventive controls.

  • DNS rebinding attacks explained: The lookup is coming from inside the house!
    summary

    TL;DR: GitHub Security Lab explains how DNS rebinding lets a malicious web page reach unauthenticated local services, demonstrated by reading secrets from the Deluge BitTorrent WebUI.

    • Root cause: Browsers bind origin to hostname, not IP, so an attacker domain can be re-resolved to 127.0.0.1 or a LAN address while staying same-origin.
    • Limit: Cookies for the real local app are not sent, so unauthenticated endpoints are the main target.
    • Case study: Deluge WebUI’s unauthenticated /js endpoint had a path traversal via /js/known_keyword/../, fixed in v2.2.0.
    • Impact: Reading web.conf exposed a salted SHA1 admin hash and sessions; with a session, CVE-2017-7178’s plugin technique via /json gives code execution.
    • Technique: Iframes on subdomains, DNS alternating between 0.0.0.0 and the real server with short TTL, retrying until rebinding succeeds on port 8112.
    • Tools: rbndr by Tavis Ormandy and NCC Group’s Singularity of Origin.
    • Browser defenses: DNS caching and draft Local Network Access (CORS-RFC1918) help but leave bypasses such as 0.0.0.0 on Linux and macOS.
    • Mitigations: Serve local apps over HTTPS, require authentication, and validate the Host header against a strict allow list.
    • Advisories: GHSL-2024-188 and GHSL-2024-191.

    Takeaway: Treat every localhost or intranet HTTP service as reachable from a victim’s browser; enforce Host header allow lists and authentication even on internal tools.

  • High Leverage Security Decisions
    summary

    TL;DR: Five foundational choices made early (IdP, security keys, IaC, managed infrastructure, MDM) give startups more security than bolt-on tools or bigger security teams.

    • Thesis: Biggest gains come from operational excellence, not buying tools; decisions get cheaper and more effective the earlier they are made.
    • Identity provider: Google Workspace, Okta or Entra ID for SSO, MFA, SCIM and audit logs, roughly $6 to $15 per user per month.
    • IdP caveats: Avoid self-hosted open source unless you can staff a specialist (about $150k a year); budget for vendors’ “SSO tax”.
    • Security keys: Issue two YubiKeys per employee (about $50 to $75 each) as the default phishing-resistant MFA for sensitive systems.
    • IaC: Terraform or OpenTofu in Git for history, review and drift reversion, scanned with Semgrep, tfsec or Snyk.
    • Managed infra: AWS Fargate, RDS and Aurora offload patching, backups and multi-AZ, aligning with the DIE triad (Distributed, Immutable, Ephemeral).
    • MDM: Intune for Windows, Apple Business Manager with Kandji or Jamf Pro for Macs, so devices enroll on first boot.
    • Caveats: Managed services cost more, Fargate has limits, MDM enrollment alone adds no security, and most advice is general IT practice.

    Takeaway: At any new company, lock in an IdP with hardware-key MFA and IaC-only changes before headcount grows and retrofitting becomes debt collection.

  • Security for High Velocity Engineering
    summary

    TL;DR: Jason Chan, ex-Netflix security VP, argues security must match engineering velocity through guardrails, sublinear team growth and paved-road solutions, not SDL-style gates.

    • Origin: Adapted from his LocoMocoSec 2024 keynote; contrasts VMware’s monthly deploys with 12-18 hours downtime against Netflix shipping dozens of times daily.
    • Constraint 1: Align with developer experience: maximize flow, give fast feedback, minimize cognitive load.
    • Constraint 2: Grow headcount sublinearly versus the company, like legal or HR.
    • Constraint 3: Prefer high-leverage reusable artifacts over one-on-one engagements.
    • Principles: Change is constant; change systems, not people (Netflix tenure was 18-24 months); guardrails, not gates.
    • Core processes: Asset discovery and enrichment, vulnerability finding and fixing, and incident response such as log4j.
    • Paved road: Optional, well-supported solutions like a central crypto API; grow width (offerings) and smoothness (ease of adoption).
    • Measurement: Treat adoption as a hypothesis; Netflix withheld secrets-in-code tickets until a secrets solution existed, then ran adoption campaigns before alerts.
    • Caveats: Anecdotal, no quantitative evidence; assumes central teams have resources to build and maintain paved roads.

    Takeaway: Before filing findings against teams, ship a secure-by-default paved road for the problem, then measure adoption and replace manual review gates with continuous guardrails.

  • IAM Lens: Google Maps for AWS IAM
    summary

    TL;DR: iam-lens is an AGPL-3.0 Node.js CLI that evaluates real collected AWS IAM policies locally to answer who can do what across accounts and organizations.

    • What: “Google Maps for AWS IAM”, evaluating identity policies, boundaries, SCPs, RCPs and resource policies together.
    • Input: Needs data from companion iam-collect (iam-collect init, iam-collect download), stored on disk or in S3.
    • Install: npm install -g @actsecurity/iam-collect @actsecurity/iam-lens.
    • simulate: Returns Allowed, ImplicitlyDenied or ExplicitlyDenied; --expect sets a nonzero exit code on mismatch, useful in CI.

    summary

    • MCP servers: About 25 entries, including Nuclei, Ghidra, x64dbg, BloodHound, Burp Suite, Shodan and VirusTotal.
    • Tools: Largest section (about 50) with agent security scanners, runtime guardrails and AI pentest platforms like pentagi, Vigil and Pipelock.
    • Research: Papers and projects on autonomous CTF and pentest agents, such as AutoCTF, CAI, D-CIPHER and Microsoft’s CyberBattleSim.
    • Frameworks: LangChain, LangGraph, CrewAI, AutoGen, Semantic Kernel, plus the Cloud Security Alliance MAESTRO threat-modeling framework.
    • Datasets: CICIDS 2017/2018, UNSW-NB15, DARPA Transparent Computing data and CTF write-up collections.
    • Learning: Newsletter, AI security podcast and PromptTrace hands-on training; communities include AI Cyber Alliance.
    • Caveats: A link list only; entries are not vetted or benchmarked, and counts are approximate.

    Takeaway: Use it as a starting index for security MCP servers and agent guardrails, but review each project’s maturity before wiring it into tooling.

  • kubectl-ai: AI powered Kubernetes Assistant
    summary

    TL;DR: kubectl-ai from GoogleCloudPlatform turns natural-language requests into Kubernetes operations via CLI, kubectl plugin, web UI or MCP server, supporting many LLM providers.

  • Automatically prioritize security issues from different tools with an LLM
    summary

    TL;DR: Plerion shows three ways to have an LLM re-rank a mixed-scanner security backlog, arguing that enriched issue context matters more than prompt wording.

    • Problem: Scanner severities conflict across tools and vendors inflate them; the example backlog has 742 issues with 61 marked “Critical”.
    • Input enrichment: Feed the model the affected resource, account, asset criticality, exposure (e.g. a bucket policy with "Principal": "*"), owner tags, and CVE and IP lookups.
    • Naive scoring: score_sort.py gives each issue a 1-100 score with reasoning; linear cost, but scores cluster at round numbers. Took 29.52s for 10 issues.
    • Bubble sort: bubble_sort.py runs pairwise LLM comparisons, O(n²): 45 for 10 issues, 4,950 for 100. Took 193.75s for 10 issues.
    • Elo rating: elo_sort.py starts every issue at 1200 and compares random pairs; the run with --max-comparisons 0.8 took 171.15s and kept comparison history.
    • Prompt: Tells the model to ignore source severity and weigh impact, likelihood, resource importance, reachability, fix availability, age, time to fix and environment; returns JSON.
    • Models: Claude 3.5 Haiku, 3.5 Sonnet and 3.7 Sonnet on AWS Bedrock trade speed and cost against reasoning depth.
    • Caveats: Not fully deterministic, tested on one 10-issue dataset with no ground truth, has inconsistent example scores, and pitches the vendor’s Pleri product.

    Takeaway: Before you ask an LLM to triage findings, enrich each finding with asset, exposure and ownership context. Use pairwise or Elo ranking for small top-N lists and accept non-deterministic output.

  • The Vulnerable MCP Project: Comprehensive Model Context Protocol Security Database
    summary

    TL;DR: The Vulnerable MCP Project is a searchable database of Model Context Protocol vulnerabilities, exploits and research, useful for tracking risk in AI agent tool integrations.

    • What it is: A catalogue maintained by Vineeth Sai, now at vulnerablemcp.info, with stats, taxonomy and a reporting form.
    • Numbers: 50 vulnerabilities, 13 rated Critical, 32 credited researchers, with entries from March 2025 to February 2026.
    • Categories: Input Validation (17) and Prompt Injection (13) lead, followed by Auth Failures, Integrity, Trust Model, Network Security, Session Management and Credentials.
    • Critical RCEs: MCP Inspector (CVE-2025-49596, chainable with DNS rebinding), mcp-remote command injection (CVE-2025-6514), and MCPJam Inspector unauthenticated on 0.0.0.0 (CVE-2026-23744).
    • Anthropic servers: Three chained flaws in mcp-server-git (CVE-2025-68143/68144/68145) reach RCE when combined with the Filesystem MCP server.
    • Attack classes: Tool poisoning via hidden instructions in tool descriptions, rug pulls, line jumping, and zero-click prompt injection through shared Google Docs in Cursor.
    • SDK and client bugs: DNS rebinding in the official SDKs (CVE-2025-66414/66416), a TypeScript SDK cross-client data leak (CVE-2026-25536), and Cursor MCPoison (CVE-2025-54136).
    • Other issues: SSRF in Fetch MCP and MarkItDown reaching EC2 metadata, plaintext API key storage, session IDs in URLs, and a registry-hijacking study of 67,057 servers.

    Takeaway: Check every MCP server and client you run against this list. Bind local MCP tools to localhost with authentication, and treat tool descriptions and fetched content as untrusted input.

  • Hardening GitHub Actions: Lessons from Recent Attacks
    summary

    TL;DR: Wiz updated its GitHub Actions hardening cheat sheet for 2026 after the tj-actions, trivy-action and Axios incidents. It covers org settings, secrets, workflow authoring, third-party actions and runners.

    • Threat pattern: A compromised workflow or action leaks credentials, and the attacker then moves laterally. In tj-actions (March 2025), a vulnerable workflow leaked PATs that were used to poison actions across thousands of repos.
    • Org settings: Make the default GITHUB_TOKEN read-only, since orgs created before February 2023 may still default to read-write. Turn on SHA pinning enforcement (August 2025) and block actions during incidents with a ! prefix.
    • PPE vectors: Pwn Requests through pull_request_target or workflow_run, script injection from issue titles or branch names into run:, GITHUB_ENV/GITHUB_PATH abuse (for example LD_PRELOAD), and living-off-the-pipeline tools.
    • Workflow authoring: Set permissions: {} at the top and grant per job. Use persist-credentials: false on actions/checkout, and pass untrusted values through env, never inline interpolation.
    • Secrets: Scope secrets to step-level env. Avoid toJson(secrets) and secrets: inherit, prefer OIDC, and keep release credentials in GitHub Apps.
    • Third-party actions: Pin to commit SHA, because tags can be force-pushed. Add a 7-14 day cooldown with pinact --min-age 7 or Renovate minimumReleaseAge.
    • Branch protection: Dismiss stale approvals on new commits, and require approval from someone other than the last pusher to stop PR hijacking.
    • Runners: Never attach self-hosted runners to public repos. Use ephemeral runners with default-deny egress.
    • Tools: zizmor for static analysis, octoscan, OpenSSF allstar, and trajan/Gato-X for self-testing. GitHub’s roadmap adds a workflow dependencies: lockfile that pins transitive actions.

    Takeaway: Run zizmor across all workflows and enforce SHA pinning at org level. Set read-only default tokens and permissions: {}, then replace long-lived cloud secrets with OIDC.

  • Building Uber’s Multi-Cloud Secrets Management Platform to Enhance Security
    summary

    TL;DR: Uber describes how a 10-person team centralized, scanned, scoped and auto-rotated 150,000+ secrets across clouds, and why it is now moving toward secretless authentication.

    • Scale: 5,000+ microservices, 5,000 databases, 500,000+ daily analytics jobs, 150,000+ secrets and 400+ vendor integrations.
    • Consolidation: Uber cut 25 HashiCorp Vault deployments to 6, limiting blast radius per environment; the Kubernetes External Secrets Operator enabled migration without code changes.
    • Metadata: Each secret records provider, deployment platform and impact level, backfilled by heuristics and owner crowdsourcing.
    • Scanning: A pre-commit Git CLI blocks secrets; PRs, pushes, Slack and build logs are scanned in real time; repos, filesystems and images are scanned on schedule.
    • Scoping: Monitoring which pipelines (Piper, Buildkite) read secrets inside containers cut the secrets distributed to workloads by up to 90%.
    • Rotation: A Cadence-based Secret Lifecycle Manager drives provider Generate/Revoke/Rollback APIs and rotates about 20,000 secrets per month with no human involvement.
    • Vendor exchange: SSX gives vendors a one-time passcode so they enter secrets straight into the vault, with sanity tests and rollback on deploy.
    • Limits: Full rotation coverage is impractical across many secret types; single-password credentials risk downtime during rotation; SaaS rotation was still in progress.
    • Direction: Uber is shifting to workload identity federation with SPIRE; the 90% figure comes with little methodology.

    Takeaway: Inventory secrets with owner and impact metadata first, then scan every channel and scope each secret to the workloads that use it. Prefer workload identity over rotation wherever a provider supports it.

    summary

  • apple/container: A tool for creating and running Linux containers using lightweight virtual machines on a Mac
    summary

    TL;DR: apple/container is Apple’s Swift CLI that runs OCI Linux containers on Apple silicon Macs, each one inside its own lightweight virtual machine.

    • What it is: A command-line tool, written in Swift, that creates and runs Linux containers on a Mac.
    • Isolation: Each container runs in its own lightweight Linux VM, not in a shared VM.
    • Images: Pulls and produces OCI-compatible images, so it works with standard registries and other OCI tools.
    • Internals: Built on Apple’s separate Containerization Swift package for container, image and process management.
    • Requirements: Apple silicon and macOS 26, because it needs virtualization and networking features new in that release.
    • Setup: Install the signed package from Releases into /usr/local, then run container system start; test with container run --rm alpine echo hello.
    • Lifecycle: update-container.sh upgrades or downgrades with -v; uninstall-container.sh -k keeps user data and -d removes it.
    • Caveats: Under active development; releases are not semver; experimental features such as the k8s subcommand may break; data is compatible only within one major version.

    Takeaway: On Apple silicon with macOS 26, try container as a Docker Desktop alternative when you want per-container VM isolation. Pin versions, because CLI and data compatibility can change between major releases.

  • This Shit is Hard: Inside the Chainguard Factory
    summary

    TL;DR: Chainguard gives a high-level tour of the automated “factory” that builds, patches, tests and ships thousands of open source packages and images hundreds of times a day.

    • Build system: Builds and tests thousands of upstream projects hundreds of times daily; it moved from GitHub Actions to a custom Kubernetes-based system over about a year.
    • Humans: Dozens of specialist engineers debug build failures, patch packages such as gcc and curl, apply hardening flags and track upstream breaking changes.
    • Bots: Detect upstream releases and open PRs within minutes, retire EOL packages from Wolfi, bump Go dependencies, and auto-merge PRs that pass all checks.
    • Security feeds: Bots update Advisories feeds that let scanners suppress fixed CVEs, and scan updates for malware with Malcontent.
    • AI: Failed build logs go to an LLM that suggests fixes in GitHub comments; engineers rate them, and the author calls this work “still early”.
    • QA: Package tests run on every update; image integration tests use K3s with Helm, or full EKS clusters for images such as the AWS EBS CSI Driver.
    • Distribution: Images ship with short-lived, scoped OIDC tokens through chainctl instead of long-lived credentials.
    • Numbers: The stated SLA is 7 days for critical CVEs and 14 days for high, medium and low; the platform took about three years to build.
    • Caveats: Promotional, self-reported figures; the claim of “fewer CVEs” comes with no data; the post gives little internal detail.

    Takeaway: Copy the pattern for your own base images: bot-driven upstream update PRs, required tests before publish, and auto-merge when checks pass. Use OIDC short-lived tokens for registry pulls.

  • EKS vs GKE Security
    summary

    TL;DR: Jason Umiker compares EKS and GKE security across IAM, network firewalling, posture, vulnerability and supply-chain management, and runtime detection, including defaults and paid add-ons.

    • AuthN: Both use cloud IAM via aws eks update-kubeconfig or gcloud container clusters get-credentials; minimum rights are eks:DescribeCluster or Kubernetes Engine Cluster Viewer.
    • AuthZ: EKS uses only the Access Entries API, replacing the deprecated aws-auth ConfigMap; GKE mixes GCP IAM with Kubernetes RBAC, which rbac-lookup helps audit.
    • Network: AWS Security Groups for Pods exist but are off by default; GKE tags and service accounts secure only nodes, not pods.
    • NetworkPolicy: EKS does not enforce policies out of the box; GKE Dataplane V2 and Autopilot enforce them by default.
    • GKE quirk: FQDNNetworkPolicy needs GKE Enterprise; GKE Warden blocks L7 rules in CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy.
    • Posture: AWS has no managed Kubernetes posture tool; GKE Enterprise Policy Controller (managed OPA Gatekeeper) can also govern attached EKS clusters.
    • Vulnerabilities: Inspector scans ECR images and pipelines but not running workloads; GKE Enterprise scans images actually running; Bottlerocket is not the EKS default, Container-Optimized OS is on GKE.
    • Supply chain: AWS Signer and Binary Authorization are mutually incompatible, which ties signed images to one cloud.
    • Runtime: GuardDuty covers the Kubernetes API and node syscalls; GCP SCC Container Threat Detection covers workloads; both have hard-to-predict costs.

    Takeaway: On EKS, enable NetworkPolicy enforcement and SG for Pods, use Bottlerocket, and add Kyverno or Gatekeeper plus GuardDuty Runtime Monitoring. On GKE, audit IAM and RBAC together.

  • Understanding the threat landscape for Kubernetes and containerized assets
    summary

    TL;DR: Microsoft Threat Intelligence maps the main Kubernetes and container attack paths, shows a password-spray-to-cryptomining case, and lists mitigations and AKS hunting queries.

    • Stat: Microsoft data shows 51% of workload identities were completely inactive over the past year, which leaves unused attack surface.
    • Threat sources: Compromised cloud accounts, vulnerable images, exposed or weakly authenticated Kubernetes APIs, app-level exploits, node attacks and pod escape, and unrestricted network traffic.
    • Case study: Storm-1977 used AzureChecker.exe to password-spray education tenants, then a guest account created a resource group and ran 200+ cryptomining containers.
    • Build-time controls: Scan images, keep secrets in a store, harden DevOps workstations, and enforce PR approval and branch policies.
    • Runtime controls: Keep containers immutable, use admission controllers and Pod Security Standards, block high or critical CVE images, and monitor API calls and Syslog.
    • Identity: Use Entra ID for AKS, disable local admin and kubeconfig reliance, close kubelet read-only port 10255, and avoid wildcard RBAC and cluster-wide bindings.
    • Network: Restrict API server access with IP allowlists and JIT access; limit exposed Kubeflow, Argo, Weave Scope and NiFi; set loadBalancerSourceRanges.
    • Hunting: Three Defender XDR CloudAuditEvents queries find privileged pods, exec into kube-system, and new cluster-admin bindings.
    • Caveats: Advice is heavily Azure and Defender centric; some alerts are in preview; the queries are AKS-specific and need tuning.

    Takeaway: Alert on privileged pods, exec into kube-system, and new cluster-admin bindings in your audit logs. Remove dormant workload identities and lock down API server exposure.

  • Cloud IR Infrastructure: Critical Readiness Steps
    summary

    TL;DR: Invictus IR lists the logging, analysis and access setup to prepare in Azure, AWS and Google Cloud before an incident, so responders can start work immediately.

    • Azure logging: Send the “Big 3” (Entra Sign-in, Entra Audit, Azure Activity) to Log Analytics; storage accounts are cheaper but need Data Explorer or Synapse to search.
    • Azure alerting: Alert on breakglass sign-ins and new Global Administrator or User Access Administrator assignments; put a Delete lock on the workspace and audit queries.
    • Azure containment: Pre-build an isolated IR subscription, a Conditional Access lock-out policy, and PIM for privileged roles; both require Entra P1/P2.
    • AWS logging: Create an organization-wide CloudTrail trail and store it in a dedicated, tightly restricted Log Archive account.
    • AWS analysis: Query CloudTrail in S3 with Athena and Glue using the official org-trail table; broad SELECT * queries get expensive.
    • AWS access: Pre-create cross-account IAM roles for the IR provider, with a permissions policy beyond the trust policy.
    • GCP logging: Route logs to logging buckets and enable Workspace log sharing (Admin, Login, OAuth Token, SAML audit) for Log Explorer.
    • GCP analysis: Upgrade buckets for SQL-based Log Analytics, but the upgrade cannot be reversed; pre-grant IAM roles to external responders.
    • Caveats: Ingestion and retention costs, lock-out risk if Trusted Locations IPs change, and high-level guidance without full configs.

    Takeaway: Set up org-wide trails, protected log stores, breakglass alerts and pre-provisioned responder access now. Test these steps before an incident.

  • Securing a SaaS Company’s AWS Environment After a Breach
    summary

    TL;DR: A consultant’s case study shows how one leaked admin IAM key let an attacker exfiltrate data and destroy a SaaS company’s AWS production, and what fixed it.

    • Initial access: A leaked access key for an IAM user with AdministratorAccess.
    • Attack chain: Recon of all regions, a pivot from staging to prod, security group changes for VPN IPs, RDS master password resets, and exfiltration with aws s3 sync.
    • Evasion and destruction: The attacker stopped CloudTrail, deleted its logs, disabled deletion protection, then deleted EC2, RDS and backups.
    • Root causes: Prod and backups lived in the Organizations management account; CloudTrail logs were in the same account; VPC Flow Logs were missing; CI used prod credentials from non-prod.
    • Process gaps: Shared IAM credentials and a shared SSH Linux user with no session recording; RDS sat in public subnets.
    • Detection miss: GuardDuty raised only a low-severity “CloudTrail stopped” alert; the attacker IPs were later listed as C&C on VirusTotal and Abuse.ch.
    • Numbers: 1,000+ developer hours lost, about one week of outage; CloudTrail LookupEvents allows 2 req/s per region and 50 events per call, over 90 days.
    • Fixes: Multi-account split, org trail with S3 Object Lock, SCPs that block CloudTrail stops and bucket deletes, RCPs, Cloudsplaining, Teleport, and private-subnet databases.
    • Caveats: Anonymized, undated and self-reported; the article does not say how the key leaked.

    Takeaway: Move logs and backups into separate accounts with Object Lock, and add SCPs that deny cloudtrail:StopLogging and deletes. Replace IAM user keys with SSO and short-lived roles.

  • Root in prod: The most important security analysis you will never do on your AWS accounts
    summary

    TL;DR: Plerion’s Daniel Grzelak shows that “root in prod” covers every principal with a path to admin through IAM and trust policies, and gives a method to map them.

    • Thesis: The root user is only one route; admin roles, permission sets, inline policies and trust relationships make many more principals effectively root.
    • Method: Inventory accounts with Organizations and boto3, define prod by data and cost rather than labels, then find root-equivalent principals.
    • Root equivalents: AdministratorAccess holders, SSO admin permission sets with groups expanded, PowerUserAccess, iam:PutRolePolicy, and broad s3:* or rds:*.
    • Graphing: Export all trust policies, build an assume-role graph, mark admin roles as targets, and trace back every principal with a path.
    • Trust risks: :root trusts let any account principal with sts:AssumeRole in; external full-account trusts and forgotten vendor roles also grant access.
    • CI/CD: Admin deploy pipelines and GitHub Actions OIDC trusts move the boundary to whoever can merge code or change runners.
    • Examples: A StackSet execution role trusting :root with admin, and a role named “public” trusting "*" with AdministratorAccess.
    • Numbers: The sample graph had 3,291 nodes, 6,418 edges and three admin target roles.
    • Caveats: The analysis ignores SCPs and resource policies and only partly checks sts:AssumeRole; the scripts are starting points from a vendor.

    Takeaway: Graph every role trust path to your prod admin roles. Remove :root and wildcard principals, and treat CI/CD, DNS and email owners as part of prod’s admin set.

  • Setting Up a Cloud Security Roadmap for Your Startup
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS Security Digest explains how startups should scope and customize a cloud security roadmap, and compares three public roadmaps by fit and gaps.

    • Why: A roadmap ties security work to business goals and stops teams chasing “urgent” findings while they miss quick wins like backups.
    • Scoping questions: Ask which clouds you use, your top 3 priorities, which services link to those priorities, and whether scope is misconfigurations or all 4Cs (cloud, cluster, container, code).
    • Customizing: Add regulatory needs only if non-compliance is existential, prioritize services linked to past incidents, and pair preventive controls with detective ones.
    • Scott Piper’s roadmap: 10 AWS-only stages that start with public resources and leaked keys; last updated in 2021, so no RCPs; best for early startups.
    • Marco Lancini’s roadmap: 94 multi-cloud items across 7 categories, aligned to CSA CCM, with tools such as Cartography and Falco; best for 250+ engineers or regulated firms.
    • AWS Security Maturity Model: 4 phases across 9 categories; good with AWS credits, but criticized for pushing AWS products.
    • Others: IANS Cloud Security Maturity Model, SANS CISO Scorecard, and a draft NIST CSF-based multi-cloud roadmap.
    • Cadence: Review every two quarters, plan four quarters ahead, and get leadership buy-in per phase because costs rise with maturity.
    • Caveats: No stage-by-stage control lists and no budget figures.

    Takeaway: Pick a base roadmap that matches your size and cloud footprint, then trim it to your top three risks. Review it every six months.

  • AWS Security Maturity Model
    summary

    TL;DR: The AWS Security Maturity Model v2 orders AWS security controls into four phases by effort and impact, so teams know what to implement first.

    • Purpose: Prioritized sequencing guidance that complements Well-Architected and the CAF Security Perspective; it covers the whole organization, not one workload.
    • Quick Wins: Security contacts, region blocking, MFA and root protection, SSO, GuardDuty, CloudTrail, Security Hub, billing alarms, Block Public Access, IAM Access Analyzer, and WAF managed rules.

    • Audience: Startups can focus on the first two phases; single-account experimenters need only MFA, security contacts and billing alarms.

    • Caveats: It is the AWS Security team’s opinionated view and leans on AWS services.

  • Getting Started with CloudTrail Security Queries
    summary

    TL;DR: Rich Mogull’s hands-on lab for writing Athena SQL queries against an organization CloudTrail trail, pivoting from action, resource or identity to investigate a terminated EC2 instance.

    • Thesis: CloudTrail entries share a structure, but each service team emits its own fields, so useful security queries start from an action, a resource or an identity.
    • Setup: Requires Athena configured over the org trail (cloudtrail_logs.organization_trail); launch and terminate an EC2 instance named “delete” to generate events.
    • Action query: WHERE eventname = 'TerminateInstances' ordered by eventtime DESC reveals who terminated the instance, from which sourceipaddress.
    • Resource query: Match the instance ID in both requestparameters and responseelements; RunInstances only returns the ID in its response.
    • Identity queries: Filter on useridentity.arn for one account, or LIKE '%/username' to cover every account in the org at lower precision.
    • Signals: Changes in source IP or user agent within one identity’s activity often indicate stolen credentials or sessions.
    • Caveats: The resource field is often empty, useridentity subfields vary, console navigation adds many Describe calls, and org-wide queries are slow without partitioning.
    • Reuse: The same queries serve both as threat detectors and as incident analysis tools.

    Takeaway: Keep a small library of action, resource and identity Athena queries for CloudTrail, always search both request and response fields, and plan partitioning before an incident forces it.

  • Roles Here? Roles There? Roles Anywhere: Exploring the Security of AWS IAM Roles Anywhere
    summary

    TL;DR: Unit 42 shows that AWS IAM Roles Anywhere’s default trust setup is too permissive, letting any certificate from a regional trust anchor assume roles it was never meant to.

    • How it works: External workloads authenticate with X.509 certificates via trust anchors (ACM Private CA or uploaded PEM bundle), profiles and IAM roles, calling the /sessions endpoint.
    • Tooling: aws_signing_helper signs requests and can emulate an EC2-style IMDS locally.
    • Root cause: The default role trust policy has no condition, so nothing ties a trust anchor to a specific profile or role.
    • Attack 1: A stolen certificate and key plus ARNs, found via ListTrustAnchors, ListProfiles or CloudTrail CreateSession events, grants access.
    • Attack 2: With rolesanywhere:CreateTrustAnchor or UpdateTrustAnchor, an attacker registers their own CA and keeps persistence while the anchor exists.
    • Fixes: Restrict sts:AssumeRole with aws:SourceArn to one trust anchor, and map certificate attributes like aws:PrincipalTag/x509Subject/CN.
    • Hardening: Prefer ACM-PCA trust anchors, since attackers cannot upload a CA to them; cap permissions with session policies on profiles.
    • Detection: Trust anchors and profiles rarely change, so treat any creation or modification as suspicious.
    • Caveats: Attribute conditions can be bypassed if certificate details leak; no prevalence data is given, and the post promotes Cortex Cloud.

    Takeaway: Audit every role trusting rolesanywhere.amazonaws.com for a missing Condition block, add aws:SourceArn and subject conditions, and alert on trust anchor changes.

  • The many ways to obtain credentials in AWS
    summary

    TL;DR: Scott Piper (Wiz) catalogs the many places AWS workloads expose IAM credentials beyond 169.254.169.254, from SDK chain sources to SSM, IoT, Cognito and DataSync.

    • SDK chain: Hardcoded keys win over everything, then env vars like AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID, then ~/.aws/credentials, /etc/boto.cfg and ~/.boto.
    • IMDS: EC2 roles at /latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/; IMDSv2 adds a PUT token; IPv6 endpoint is [fd00:ec2::254].
    • Containers: AWS_CONTAINER_CREDENTIALS_RELATIVE_URI with 169.254.170.2 covers ECS, SageMaker, App Runner, CodeBuild and Batch; FULL_URI serves CloudShell and Greengrass.
    • EKS: Pod Identity uses 169.254.170.23 plus a token file; IRSA uses AWS_WEB_IDENTITY_TOKEN_FILE and sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity.
    • DHMC: A second SSM role per EC2 at identity-credentials/ec2/security-credentials/ec2-instance, needing the key in /var/lib/amazon/ssm/Vault/Store/EC2RegistrationKey.
    • SSM hybrid: Read access to RegistrationKey and InstanceFingerprint under the SSM Vault is enough to obtain credentials on on-prem hosts.
    • Certificates: iot:AssumeRoleWithCertificate and IAM Roles Anywhere (PKCS#11 since September 2023) use generic-looking X.509 files that are hard to classify.
    • Others: Cognito GetCredentialsForIdentity returns session credentials; DataSync uses undocumented cert.pem and keypair.pem under /usr/local/aws-storage-gateway/var/.
    • Caveats: No concrete detection rules; some details come from third-party research; written by Wiz to motivate its sensor.

    Takeaway: Extend credential-theft detection and file monitoring beyond IMDS to container endpoints, token files, SSM Vault paths and certificate files, and remember one host can carry several principals.

  • The dark cloud around GCP service accounts
    summary

    TL;DR: Dave Bogle shows that deleting a GCP service account key does not cut off access, because issued tokens cannot be revoked and can mint fresh keys for persistence.

    • Problem: The key only signs a JWT exchanged for an access token lasting about one hour; that token is not revocable.
    • Storage: gcloud keeps tokens in ~/.config/gcloud/access_tokens.db and key credentials in ~/.config/gcloud/credentials.db.
    • Failed revocation: Key deletion and gcloud auth revoke (which only clears local entries) did not stop a replayed raw token.
    • Persistence: A service account with iam.serviceAccountKeys.create creates a key for itself and recreates it whenever defenders delete it.
    • Detection: Alert on google.iam.admin.v1.CreateServiceAccountKey where authenticationInfo.principalEmail equals the account in request.name.
    • Risky roles: roles/editor, roles/owner, roles/iam.serviceAccountKeyAdmin, roles/securitycenter.admin and roles/assuredoss.admin allow key creation.
    • Mitigation: Least privilege, block keys with org policy, and enforce iam.automaticIamGrantsForDefaultServiceAccounts so default accounts lose Editor.
    • Caveats: Google considers it addressed by the May 2024 default-grant change; the author disagrees, and detection was tested in one environment only.
    • Source note: The Red Canary URL now redirects to a Zscaler blog copy of the post.

    Takeaway: Treat a compromised service account as needing disablement, not just key deletion, and alert on any service account creating its own key.

  • Things we learned about LLMs in 2024
    summary

    TL;DR: Simon Willison’s year-in-review of LLMs in 2024: GPT-4 was surpassed widely, prices crashed, reasoning models arrived, and agents and prompt injection remained unsolved.

    • GPT-4 beaten: 18 organizations had 70 models ranking above original GPT-4 on Chatbot Arena, starting with Gemini 1.5 Pro.
    • Local models: GPT-4-class Qwen2.5-Coder-32B and Llama 3.3 70B run on a 64GB M2 MacBook Pro.
    • Prices: GPT-4 was $30 per million input tokens in December 2023; GPT-4o mini is $0.15 and Gemini 1.5 Flash 8B $0.0375.
    • Numbers: Describing 68,000 photos with Flash 8B costs about $1.68 total.
    • Reasoning: o1 launched in September; o3 posted strong ARC-AGI results, likely costing over $1,000,000 in compute.
    • DeepSeek v3: 685B parameters trained on 2,788,000 H800 GPU hours (about $5,576,000) versus 30,840,000 hours for Llama 3.1 405B.
    • Agents: Still not real; LLM gullibility and prompt injection, discussed since September 2022, block them.
    • Other: Evals matter, synthetic training data works, Apple Intelligence is weak while MLX excels, and “slop” names unrequested, unreviewed AI content.
    • Caveats: Arena is a vibes-based evaluation; datacenter buildout is worsening environmental impact even as per-prompt cost falls.

    Takeaway: Assume cheap, capable models are everywhere, build evals before shipping LLM features, and treat any agent that reads untrusted input as prompt-injectable.

  • Implementing Security Invariants in an AWS Management Account
    summary

    TL;DR: Chris Farris proposes enforcing security invariants in the AWS Organization management (payer) account, where SCPs and RCPs do not apply, using one universal IAM permission boundary.

    • Problem: SCPs and RCPs skip the management account, yet workloads often live there and can endanger the whole Organization.
    • Approach: Attach one shared permission boundary to every user and role; it grants nothing and only caps maximum permissions.
    • Invariants: Nine rules, covering CloudTrail, member account closure, Organization services, SCP/RCP changes, sts:AssumeRoot, billing, Identity Center, StackSets and Control Tower.
    • Control Tower: Nobody may enable or use it; tagline “Control Tower: Not even Once!”
    • Policy design: A broad Allow with NotAction excludes restricted actions, then conditional allows on aws:PrincipalArn restore them for Cloud Admin, Finance, Identity Center and Terraform roles.
    • Self-protection: Statements deny sts:AssumeRole on OrganizationAccountAccessRole and block edits to the boundary policy itself except by admin and enforcer roles.
    • Enforcement: A Lambda fired by EventBridge on iam:CreateRole, iam:CreateUser and boundary delete/put events reattaches the boundary nearly instantly; a Steampipe query audits coverage.
    • Caveats: A race window exists between principal creation and attachment; AWS-managed Identity Center roles cannot get boundaries; existing boundaries are not handled.
    • Scope: A design pattern with placeholder role names, not a fully tested implementation.

    Takeaway: If workloads run in your payer account, deploy an auto-enforced permission boundary protecting CloudTrail, Organizations, root and billing actions, and move workloads out where possible.

  • Confidential computing at 1Password
    summary

    TL;DR: 1Password explains how it uses AWS Nitro Enclaves to process some decrypted secrets server-side while aiming to keep end-to-end encryption guarantees.

    • Problem: E2EE limits features; enterprise vault-usage reports are too large to decrypt and aggregate on-device.
    • Approach: Confidential computing runs plaintext processing in hardware-isolated enclaves shielded from the OS, cloud provider and 1Password operators.
    • Current use: Powers detailed reporting in 1Password Extended Access Management; other features are under development without a timeline.
    • Attestation: Each Nitro Enclave produces a signed attestation document proving what code it runs.
    • Transparency: Every enclave release is published to Sigstore’s Rekor log so auditors can verify every version that ran.
    • Channel: Clients connect via the Noise Protocol, using a public key trusted through attestation.
    • Client enforcement: Apps refuse enclaves whose code is not signed by 1Password and logged in Rekor.
    • Implementation: Written in Rust, reusing client crypto and safe-logging libraries; an external audit reported zero major vulnerabilities.
    • Caveats: Applies only to certain secrets; trust rests on AWS Nitro and Rekor; auditor unnamed and deeper technical docs promised later.

    Takeaway: When evaluating vendors that process secrets server-side, ask for enclave attestation, a public transparency log and client-side verification rather than trusting policy claims.

  • Avoiding mistakes with AWS OIDC integration conditions
    summary

    TL;DR: Wiz reviews 24 SaaS vendors’ AWS OIDC integration docs and shows that missing aud or sub trust-policy conditions can let any vendor customer assume your role.

    • Root cause: IAM role trust policies for OIDC providers often lack conditions, so anyone authenticating to the same vendor can assume the role.
    • History: In 2023 researchers found GitHub Actions roles without sub; AWS now blocks creating such GitHub policies, but old ones may remain.
    • Spread: The same flaw appeared in Terraform Cloud, Microsoft Defender and GitLab integrations.
    • Shared values: GitHub Actions, Gitpod, Harness, Shisho and Upbound use sts.amazonaws.com as aud, which restricts nothing by itself.
    • Odd vendors: Microsoft Defender relies on sts:RoleSessionName; DoIT uses aws:RequestTag/DoitEnvironment; Env0 adds aws:RequestTag custom claims.
    • Partial guidance: GitLab, Bitbucket, DVC and OpenShift recommend only sub; Teleport, DoIT and sandboxes.cloud recommend only aud.
    • EKS: OIDC providers should also check aud (sts.amazonaws.com) and sub, though aud is often omitted.
    • Tools: IAM Access Analyzer warns about missing condition keys; broad StringLike on sub can still admit every repo and branch.
    • Caveats: Based on public docs only; some vendors publish none, and customer-specific provider URLs reduce risk.

    Takeaway: Audit every OIDC-trusting IAM role for both aud and a tight sub condition, verify which values are truly unique to you, and run Access Analyzer.

  • Securing Grafana on Kubernetes with GCP IAP, Gateway API, and Terraform
    summary

    TL;DR: A Terraform-driven guide to putting Grafana on Kubernetes behind GCP Identity-Aware Proxy with Gateway API, using IAP’s signed JWT as Grafana’s only login path.

    • Why: IAP reduces exposure to Grafana CVEs and adds context-aware access, DDoS protection and audit logging.
    • Architecture: User to IAP to a GCP load balancer from the Gateway to the Grafana service on port 80 in monitoring; PostgreSQL backs Grafana.
    • IAP wiring: A GCPBackendPolicy enables IAP with OAuth secret grafana-oauth-secret; a separate grafana-gateway chart covers limited Helm route support.
    • JWT auth: auth.jwt reads X-Goog-Iap-Jwt-Assertion, verifies against Google JWKs, requires iss of https://cloud.google.com/iap, and maps the email claim.

    • Manual steps: OAuth consent screen (Internal) once per project; re-apply if the annotation is not yet present.
