    Takeaway: Front internal dashboards with IAP and trust only its verified JWT header, but plan a break-glass admin path and expect the brittle backend-service lookup.

  • Passkeys: they’re not perfect but they’re getting better
    summary

    TL;DR: The UK NCSC says passkeys are the future of authentication and worth using now, but unresolved usability, recovery and standards gaps block recommending them for mass adoption.

    • Problem: Most cyber harm to citizens involves abuse of legitimate credentials via phishing or weak and reused passwords.
    • Benefits: Passkeys cannot be guessed or phished and are unique per site.
    • Numbers: Microsoft data shows about 8 seconds per passkey sign-in versus 69 seconds for password plus second factor.
    • User issues: Device-bound versus synced passkeys confuse users; device loss, vendor migration and inconsistent terminology remain hard.
    • Recovery risk: Attackers may shift to weak account recovery and reset flows, including phishing recovery keys.
    • Access gaps: Shared devices, people without personal devices and those for whom biometrics fail lack private credential manager access.
    • Relying parties: Multi-domain services may need several passkeys; no consensus on assurance level or whether passkeys count as MFA.
    • NCSC plans: Work with FIDO and vendors, explore passkeys for GOV.UK One Login, review GPG 44, and later push passkeys as default.
    • Caveats: NCSC guidance still defaults to password plus factor; the timing figure is Microsoft’s, not independently tested.

    Takeaway: Offer passkeys as an option now, but harden account recovery first, since it becomes the weakest link once phishing of passwords stops working.

  • How AI enhances static application security testing (SAST)
    summary

    TL;DR: GitHub explains how it pairs CodeQL with Copilot to model library sources and sinks and auto-generate fixes for SAST alerts directly in pull requests.

    • Problem: Shift-left moved security work onto developers without better tools; roughly one security expert per 100 developers.
    • Survey: In a 2023 GitHub survey, developers ranked fixing vulnerabilities (31%) second only to writing code (32%).
    • Source/sink modeling: GitHub experiments with AI to model open source frameworks for CodeQL, since manual modeling is laborious; no accuracy figures given.

  • How we built an AppSec AI that security researchers agree with 96% of the time
    summary

    TL;DR: Semgrep explains how it benchmarked and tuned its LLM-based Assistant Autotriage until it matched its security researchers on 96% of true-positive SAST findings.

    • Problem: SAST backlogs are huge; 100,000 findings at 15 minutes each would keep 3 people busy around the clock for about a year.
    • How it works: Each finding gets a binary verdict, likely true or false positive, using rule metadata, prior triage, rule examples, Assistant Memories and RAG-supplied dataflow context.
    • Outputs: True positives get a fix and remediation steps as a PR comment or Jira ticket; false positives are auto-tagged and the developer is notified.
    • Benchmark: Over 2,000 researcher-triaged findings, evaluated with promptfoo across gpt-4o, gpt-4o-mini and claude-3-5-sonnet; gpt-4o gave the best balance.
    • Numbers: Initial agreement was about 55% overall (91% true positive, 25% false positive); final was 96% true positive and 41% false positive.
    • Techniques: Prompt refinement and feeding the rule confidence value made Assistant disagree more with low-confidence rules.
    • Design choice: Tuned deliberately to minimize false negatives; improving false-positive detection tended to cost true-positive accuracy.
    • Caveats: Agreement is measured against Semgrep’s own researchers and dataset; Assistant sees only the dataflow trace, not the whole project, and never closes findings.

    Takeaway: When adopting AI triage, benchmark it against your own labelled findings and keep suppressed findings in a backlog that AppSec still reviews.

  • AWS EKS Access Management & Permissions
    summary

    TL;DR: A comparison of the four overlapping EKS auth mechanisms that recommends EKS Pod Identities plus RoleBindings for a platform cluster that manages pods in a separate workload cluster.

    • Problem: AWS permissions (pods changing AWS resources) and Kubernetes permissions (managing K8s objects, even cross-cluster) are separate needs that are often confused.
    • aws-auth (2018): A ConfigMap in kube-system maps IAM principals to RBAC in one cluster only; it is deprecated and gives pods no AWS access.
    • IRSA (2019): OIDC provider plus service account annotations; flexible and cross-cluster, but heavy to configure, with a 100 OIDC providers per account limit and trust policy size limits.
    • Pod Identities (2023): Generic trust on pods.eks.amazonaws.com allowing sts:AssumeRole and sts:TagSession, so one role can be reused across clusters unchanged.
    • Setup: Create role and least-privilege policy, install the pod-identity add-on, then add aws_eks_pod_identity_association with cluster, namespace, service account and role ARN.
    • Gotcha: Pod Identities does not create the service account; you create it and bind its Kubernetes permissions yourself.
    • Cluster Access Management (2023): Access entries and access policies map AWS principals to K8s rights and let you revoke the cluster creator’s cluster-admin.
    • Limits: The 15 AWS-managed access policies such as AmazonEKSClusterAdminPolicy cannot be customized; granular access needs groups on the access entry plus your own RoleBindings.

    Takeaway: For new EKS workloads, use Pod Identities for AWS permissions and explicit RoleBindings for Kubernetes permissions, and plan to retire aws-auth.

  • THE RISK YOU CAN’T AFFORD TO IGNORE: AWS SES AND EMAIL SPOOFING
    summary

    TL;DR: Verifying a whole domain in AWS SES lets anyone with SES send rights spoof any address in that domain, and SES does not log sent mail by default.

    • Root cause: A verified domain identity allows sending from any address on that domain, unlike email identities that allow only confirmed addresses.
    • Who can abuse it: Holders of SES SMTP credentials, or IAM principals with ses:SendEmail or ses:SendRawEmail on the domain identity.
    • Deliverability: Because AWS acts as the domain’s mail server, the author estimates “99.9+%” inbox delivery; the figure is unsourced.
    • Logging gap: SES sends are not logged by default; auditing needs SNS notifications routed to S3, for example via Kinesis Data Firehose, at extra cost.
    • Classification: Sits between CSPM (missing logging) and CIEM (identities can impersonate users); SES is outside benchmarks such as CIS.
    • Mitigation 1: Prefer individually verified email identities; a Custom MAIL FROM domain resolves the DMARC concern that pushed teams to domain verification.
    • Mitigation 2: Attach a Sending Authorization policy that denies ses:SendEmail and ses:SendRawEmail unless ses:FromAddress matches an allowlist.
    • Caveats: No test data is provided, and SPF and DKIM are not discussed.

    Takeaway: Audit SES for verified domain identities, restrict ses:FromAddress with a sending authorization policy, and turn on SES event logging.

  • Connect your on-premises Kubernetes cluster to AWS APIs using IAM Roles Anywhere
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS walkthrough for giving on-premises Kubernetes pods short-lived AWS credentials via IAM Roles Anywhere, cert-manager and AWS Private CA, with no long-term access keys.

    • Problem: Long-term IAM keys need rotation and tracking, and service account issuer discovery requires a publicly reachable API server.
    • How it works: Pods exchange X.509 certificates for temporary credentials through the IAM Roles Anywhere CreateSession API, signed with SigV4a.
    • CA: AWS Private CA in SHORT_LIVED_CERTIFICATE mode issues certificates valid for 7 days or fewer and backs the trust anchor onprem-k8s-issuer.
    • cert-manager: v1.16.2 with cert-manager-approver-policy, cert-manager-csi-driver for per-pod ephemeral certs, and aws-privateca-issuer providing AWSPCAIssuer.
    • Sidecar: aws_signing_helper serve exposes an IMDSv2-compatible endpoint on port 9911, so application images stay unchanged.
    • Least privilege: Trust policies on rolesanywhere.amazonaws.com are scoped by aws:PrincipalTag/x509Subject/CN and trust anchor ARN; use one role and profile per workload.
    • Bootstrap: The issuer’s first certificate is made manually with openssl, then replaced by a 168h cert renewed 24h before expiry.
    • Caveats: The aws-privateca-issuer Helm chart must be rendered and edited by hand to add the sidecar; not meant for EKS, which should use IRSA or Pod Identity.
    • Hardening: Revocation works through CRL checks, and the post advises a hardened base image instead of plain Alpine.

    Takeaway: For hybrid clusters, replace static AWS keys with short-lived certificate-based credentials, scoped per workload through CN conditions.

  • Four ways to grant cross-account access in AWS
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS explains four ways to write resource-based policies for cross-account access and the trade-off each makes between granularity and availability.

    • Baseline: Cross-account access needs both an identity-based policy in the caller’s account and a resource-based policy on the target resource.
    • Method 1, role ARN in Principal: Most granular, but IAM stores the role’s unique ID, so a deleted and recreated role silently loses access.
    • Method 2, account in Principal: Account ID or :root ARN (which does not mean the root user); survives role recreation but delegates decisions to the other account’s admins.
    • Method 3, account plus aws:PrincipalArn: An ArnEquals condition pins one role yet survives recreation, because conditions are not converted to unique IDs.
    • Method 3 caveat: Any future role with the same name in that account will also match.
    • Method 4, organization: Principal "*" plus aws:PrincipalOrgId, since an organization cannot be named as a principal.
    • Owner exclusion: Adding StringNotEquals on aws:PrincipalAccount against ${aws:ResourceAccount} forces same-account principals to also need an identity-based allow.
    • Choosing: Use Method 1 when you own both accounts’ role lifecycles, Method 3 when you do not, and Method 4 for organization-wide sharing.

    Takeaway: Prefer aws:PrincipalArn conditions over role ARNs in Principal when IaC may recreate roles, and audit resource policies regularly.

    • Audience: Aimed at pentesters new to AWS and experienced cloud hackers wanting practical, engagement-ready skills.

    Takeaway: Watch it as an IAM-first primer before an AWS engagement, and use its detection-evasion topics to check what your own monitoring would miss.

  • Ephemeral values in Terraform
    summary

    TL;DR: HashiCorp explains Terraform 1.11 ephemeral values, which let secrets flow through a run without being written to the plan or state file.

    • Problem: Before ephemerality, sensitive values were always persisted to plan and state, so state had to be treated as secret.
    • Ephemeral resources: Temporary resources that read a secret or open a connection; their attributes can be referenced but are never persisted.
    • Lifecycle: Ephemeral resources are opened when needed, renewed if they outlive remote expiry, and closed after dependent work; not every resource implements renew or close.
    • Write-only arguments: Provider-implemented arguments such as password_wo or data_json_wo that are never stored in plan or state.
    • Change detection: Each write-only argument pairs with a stored version argument, such as password_wo_version, which you bump to push a new value.
    • Example: ephemeral "random_password" writes to Vault via data_json_wo, is read back ephemerally, and is fed to aws_db_instance password_wo.
    • Ordering: Ephemeral resources with unknown inputs are deferred to apply; ones that managed resources depend on are opened first.
    • Caveats: They run on every plan and apply; a newly generated ephemeral secret is lost unless written to a secrets manager.

    Takeaway: On Terraform 1.11+, move database passwords and tokens to ephemeral resources and write-only arguments, and manage version arguments to trigger rotations.

  • IssueOps: Automate CI/CD (and more!) with GitHub Issues and Actions
    summary

    TL;DR: GitHub’s guide to IssueOps: driving automation such as approvals and team changes from issue forms, comments and labels, modelled as a state machine in GitHub Actions.

    • Concept: Issues, comments, labels and PRs act as triggers and audit trail for Actions workflows; anything with an API can be automated this way.
    • State machine: Requests move through opened, submitted, approved, denied and closed, with events, guards and actions on each transition.
    • Guards: Comment commands such as .submit, .approve and .deny; approve and deny check that the commenter is in the admins team.
    • Example: A team-membership issue form is parsed to JSON by issue-ops/parser, validated with Octokit teams.getByName, then the user is added on approval.
    • Tools: issue-ops/validator, issue-ops/labeler, actions/github-script, actions/create-github-app-token and peter-evans/create-or-update-comment.
    • Auth: A GitHub App via ISSUEOPS_APP_ID and ISSUEOPS_APP_PRIVATE_KEY reaches resources outside the repo.
    • Safety: Workflows declare minimal permissions, re-validate the body on submit, and reset labels when an issue is reopened.
    • Caveat: One trigger type such as issue_comment serves many events, so every workflow must check comment content.

    Takeaway: IssueOps gives self-service with an audit trail; enforce admin checks in guards, re-validate input at each step, and start with a small workflow.

  • Understanding RCPs and SCPs in AWS: Choosing the Right Policy for your Security Needs
    summary

    TL;DR: Fog Security compares AWS Resource Control Policies and Service Control Policies and explains when to use each, or both layered, across an organization.

    • Core idea: SCPs limit what your principals can do; RCPs limit what can be done to your resources, including by outside principals.
    • History: SCPs date from early 2017; RCPs were released in late 2024.
    • RCP coverage: Only S3, STS, KMS, SQS and Secrets Manager, extending to IAM role trust via sts:AssumeRole and DynamoDB via KMS.
    • Numbers: Both cap documents at 5,120 characters and 5 attachments per entity; 2,000 SCPs and 1,000 RCPs per organization.
    • Use RCPs for: Standardizing resource policies, saving SCP space, and NotResource, which SCPs do not support.
    • Use SCPs for: Services outside the RCP list and NotAction, which RCPs do not support, such as locking admin accounts to iam:* and sts:*.
    • Migration example: Moving an S3 KMS-encryption deny from SCP to RCP needs "Principal": "*" and changes who is covered inside and outside the org.
    • Hardening: Add confused deputy protection with aws:SourceAccount or aws:SourceOrgID.
    • Caveats: NotResource and NotAction logic is easy to get wrong; test organization policies carefully.

    Takeaway: Add RCPs to block external access to S3, KMS, STS, SQS and Secrets Manager, and keep SCPs for principal-side guardrails.

  • A hard look at GuardDuty shortcomings
    summary

    TL;DR: Tracebit tests Amazon GuardDuty and finds narrow service coverage, unpredictable cost, and zero alerts for common data-exfiltration techniques, arguing for canary resources alongside it.

    • Coverage: Covers about ten services such as EC2, EKS, Lambda, RDS and S3, out of nearly 250 AWS services, with 176 finding types.
    • Cost: Billed on CloudTrail, VPC Flow Logs, DNS, EKS audit and S3 data event volume; one S3 Protection bill rose from $2.5k to $6.5k per month.
    • Efficacy: Static severity ignores resource sensitivity, Low findings are noisy, and ML struggles with low-volume, high-impact theft.
    • Stratus Red Team test: Six exfiltration techniques, including Secrets Manager mass reads and shared EBS or RDS snapshots, produced zero alerts.
    • guardduty-tester: 11 of 12 runs worked; Recon:EC2/Portscan, Recon:IAMUser/TorIPCaller and PenTest:S3/KaliLinux never fired.
    • Latency: Median detection took 15 minutes; the best was 5:08 for UnauthorizedAccess:S3/MaliciousIPCaller.Custom.
    • Blast radius: With s5cmd at 316 MB/s and 1,781 deletes/s, an attacker could move about 1.3 TB before the fastest alert.
    • Caveats: Small informal tests by one author; the vendor sells canary infrastructure, so the conclusions favor its product.

    Takeaway: Keep GuardDuty on, but do not rely on it for data exfiltration; add canary credentials and resources and forecast add-on costs during the trial.

  • New Vulnerability in GitHub Copilot and Cursor: How Hackers Can Weaponize Code Agents
    summary

    TL;DR: Pillar Security’s “Rules File Backdoor” hides invisible-Unicode instructions in AI assistant rule files so Cursor and GitHub Copilot silently generate malicious code.

    • Vector: Rule files such as those in .cursor/rules steer code generation, are shared widely, trusted implicitly and rarely reviewed.
    • How it works: Payloads hide in zero-width joiners, bidirectional markers and Unicode tag characters that humans cannot see but models still read.
    • Manipulation: Jailbreak-style storytelling frames the payload as a security requirement and tells the AI not to mention the change.
    • Demo: In Cursor agent mode, “Create a simple HTML only page” produced a script tag loading from an attacker site, with no mention in the reply.
    • Persistence: A poisoned rule file affects every later session for the team, survives forks, and stays invisible in GitHub PR review.
    • Impact: Insecure crypto, auth bypasses, disabled validation, and exfiltration of env vars, credentials and API keys.
    • Vendor response: Cursor (Feb-Mar 2025) and GitHub (Mar 2025) called it user responsibility; GitHub added a hidden-Unicode warning on 1 May 2025.
    • Mitigations: Audit rule files for invisible characters, review AI config like code, and check generated code for unexpected external references.

    Takeaway: Add CI checks that reject hidden Unicode in rule files such as .cursor/rules, and review them as strictly as executable code.

  • MCP (Model Context Protocol): Simply explained in 5 minutes
    summary

    TL;DR: Jordan Cutler’s beginner explainer of the Model Context Protocol (MCP): a standard interface that lets LLM clients like Cursor call external tools through adapter servers.

    • Problem: Each third-party API has its own types and patterns, so wiring assistants to Sentry, Slack, JIRA or Gmail is tedious.
    • Core idea: MCP makes providers expose tools through one standard interface; the author frames servers as the adapter pattern.
    • Components: An MCP client (e.g. Cursor) speaks the protocol; MCP servers wrap third-party APIs and translate requests into real API calls.
    • How it works: The Slack server defines tools (name, description, inputSchema), creates the server, and handles CallToolRequestSchema with a switch on tool name.
    • Transport: Client and server talk over standard I/O via StdioServerTransport, not HTTP.
    • Examples: AgentDesk BrowserTools MCP let Cursor read console logs and fix a tooltip bug; Puppeteer MCP let Cursor drive a browser.
    • Setup: Create .cursor/mcp.json, add a command like npx -y @modelcontextprotocol/server-puppeteer, enable it in settings; Slack needs SLACK_BOT_TOKEN and SLACK_TEAM_ID.
    • Limits: Setup grows harder with extra API keys and permissions; the article does not cover security, credential protection or scoping tool access.

    Takeaway: Use this as a mental model of MCP, then separately assess the tokens and permissions each server holds, since the article skips that entirely.

  • Effectively implementing resource control policies in a multi-account environment
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS Security Blog guide to rolling out Resource Control Policies (RCPs) across an AWS Organization in five phases, with an sts:AssumeRole org-perimeter example and exception patterns.

    • What RCPs are: Coarse-grained, always-on resource boundaries attached to the root, OUs or accounts; not for fine-grained least privilege.
    • Control objectives: Data perimeters, cross-service confused deputy mitigation, and identity-independent rules such as requiring TLS 1.2+ for S3.
    • Five phases: Examine objectives, design guardrails and exceptions, anticipate impact, implement via CI/CD, then monitor continuously.
    • Core policy: Deny sts:AssumeRole for Principal: "*" using StringNotEqualsIfExists on aws:PrincipalOrgID and BoolIfExists on aws:PrincipalIsAWSService false.
    • Exceptions: Exclude accounts with aws:ResourceAccount, tagged roles with aws:ResourceTag/partner-access-exception plus aws:PrincipalAccount, or ARNs via NotResource.
    • Tag governance: A companion SCP restricts who can change the exception tag (aws:TagKeys, aws:PrincipalArn) and blocks passing it as a session tag.
    • Impact analysis: Use IAM Access Analyzer for externally shared roles and query CloudTrail with Athena for STS calls from outside the org.
    • Deployment: CloudFormation AWS::Organizations::Policy in pipelines or Control Tower customizations (rcp deploy method); test in lower OUs, roll out in waves.
    • Caveats: Overly strict RCPs break partner integrations; tag exceptions are only as strong as tag governance; uncovered services need separate controls.

    Takeaway: Analyze external STS access with Access Analyzer and CloudTrail first, then ship an org-perimeter RCP through IaC in waves, locking down exception tags with an SCP.

  • Master architecture decision records (ADRs): Best practices for effective decision-making
    summary

    TL;DR: AWS ProServe engineers share ten practices for running Architecture Decision Records (ADRs), drawn from 200+ ADRs over 3+ years on teams from under 10 to about 100 people.

    • Problem: Teams lose 20-30% of time coordinating across teams, per the authors’ experience, causing slow delivery and repeated refactoring.
    • Meetings: Keep ADR reviews to 30-45 minutes, readout style: 10-15 minutes silent reading with written comments.
    • Participants: Cross-functional but lean; keep attendees under 10 even on large projects.
    • Scope: One decision per ADR; explore alternatives in a separate design document referenced from the ADR.
    • Feedback: Resolve every comment by editing or reaching consensus; the author owns the document.
    • Timeliness: Expect 1-3 readouts; if more, narrow scope or cut invitees; most decisions are reversible “two-way doors”.
    • Lifecycle: Treat accepted ADRs as “team law”; when superseded, link the new ADR from the old one; store centrally.
    • Adoption: Pilot with one team, use clear templates, track time to decision, rework reduction and team satisfaction.
    • Caveats: Experience-based, no controlled data; irreversible decisions may need more deliberation than suggested.

    Takeaway: Adopt a single-decision ADR template with timeboxed silent-read reviews under 10 people, and keep superseded ADRs linked in one shared repo.

  • Patterns and Techniques for Writing High-Performance Applications with Go
    summary

    TL;DR: The Go Optimization Guide is a free, benchmark-driven series on making production Go services faster, covering memory, concurrency, networking and per-version runtime performance.

    • Approach: Emphasizes measured results over folklore and explains what the runtime does and where costs come from.
    • Memory patterns: Object pooling, preallocation, struct field alignment, avoiding interface boxing, zero-copy, GC behavior, escape analysis and stack allocation.
    • Concurrency patterns: Worker pools, atomics and sync primitives, lazy initialization, immutable data sharing and context management.
    • Nuance: Recommends sync.Pool only “where it actually helps, not everywhere”.
    • Networking: net/http, net.Conn, UDP, 10K+ concurrent connections, GOMAXPROCS with epoll/kqueue tuning, TCP vs HTTP/2 vs gRPC, QUIC with quic-go.
    • Operations: Profiling with pprof, load testing, socket options, DNS and TLS tuning, leak prevention in long-lived connections.
    • Version tracking: 76 benchmarks across Go 1.24-1.27 with an interactive tool comparing deltas by category.
    • Benchmark setup: Linux amd64 (Ice Lake), Linux arm64 (Graviton3) and macOS arm64 on dedicated EC2 with variance retry logic.
    • Source: Personal project in astavonin/go-optimization-guide, built with Material for MkDocs.

    Takeaway: Profile with pprof first, then consult the matching pattern and the version-tracking benchmarks before tuning Go services or upgrading Go versions.

  • A collection of MCP servers.
    summary

    TL;DR: punkpeye/awesome-mcp-servers is a large curated GitHub list of production and experimental Model Context Protocol servers, organized by category and synced with Glama’s web directory.

    • Purpose: Catalogs MCP servers that give AI tools file access, database connections, API integrations and other context.

    • Legend: Icons mark official implementations, language (Python, TypeScript, Go, Rust, C#, Java, C/C++, Ruby), scope and OS.

  • curl-like access to AWS resources with AWS Signature Version 4 request signing
    summary

    TL;DR: awscurl is a curl-like CLI that signs HTTP requests with AWS Signature Version 4, letting you call AWS APIs and IAM-protected endpoints without writing signing code.

    • Problem: Raw curl cannot call SigV4-protected AWS endpoints such as API Gateway with IAM auth, S3 or EC2 query APIs.
    • Install: pip install awscurl, brew install awscurl, or Docker images okigan/awscurl and ghcr.io/okigan/awscurl.
    • Usage: awscurl --service execute-api -X POST -d @request.json https://<prefix>.execute-api.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/<resource>.
    • Credentials: Flags override env vars (AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID, AWS_SESSION_TOKEN), which override ~/.aws/credentials with --profile or AWS_PROFILE.
    • Defaults: --service defaults to execute-api and --region to us-east-1; set both for other services or regions.
    • Curl-like flags: -X, -d, --data-binary, -H, -i, -v, -k, -L, -o and --fail-with-body.
    • Docker: Mount ~/.aws into /root/.aws or pass keys; the README offers a shell alias wrapping this.
    • Alternatives: Go ports (allthings/awscurl, legal90/awscurl) and signing proxies like awslabs aws-sigv4-proxy.
    • Caveat: Forgetting --service signs for the wrong service; passing secrets as flags can leak them into shell history.

    Takeaway: Keep awscurl in your toolbox for testing IAM-authorized API Gateway, OpenSearch or raw AWS APIs, using profiles rather than key flags.

  • GitHub’s official MCP Server
    summary

    TL;DR: GitHub’s official MCP server lets AI agents read code, manage issues and PRs, monitor Actions and query security data, with configurable toolsets and a read-only mode.

    • Remote option: Hosted at https://api.githubcopilot.com/mcp/ for hosts supporting remote MCP, such as VS Code 1.101+, Claude Desktop, Cursor and Windsurf.
    • Local option: Docker image ghcr.io/github/github-mcp-server or a Go binary run with the stdio subcommand.
    • Auth: OAuth browser login (token kept in memory, Docker callback on 127.0.0.1:8085) or GITHUB_PERSONAL_ACCESS_TOKEN, which takes precedence.
    • Default toolsets: context, repos, issues, pull_requests, users; select others via --toolsets or GITHUB_TOOLSETS.
    • Other toolsets: actions, code_security, dependabot, secret_protection, orgs, projects, gists and more; all enables everything.
    • Least privilege: --read-only skips write tools even if named in --tools; README suggests minimal PAT scopes like repo, read:packages, read:org.
    • Enterprise: --gh-host or GITHUB_HOST for GHES and ghe.com; HTTPS required; the remote server does not support GHES.
    • Caveats: Invalid names in --tools stop startup; some hosts like Windsurf need hardcoded tokens in config files.
    • Go module: The v2.0.0 tag is unusable as a module; use a pseudo-version until a fixed release.

    Takeaway: Run it with --read-only, a narrow toolset list and a minimally scoped token, and avoid hosts that force plaintext tokens in config.

  • What Do Hackers Know About Your AWS Account?
    summary

    TL;DR: Plerion argues exposed AWS resources are only exploitable if attackers know their identifiers, and launches Awseye, a free OSINT service mapping publicly discoverable AWS account IDs and resources.

    • Thesis: AWS is API-driven, so attacks need account IDs, ARNs, hostnames or names; attackers collect these while defenders mostly do not.
    • CDK example: Asset buckets follow cdk-{qualifier}-assets-{account_id}-{region} with default qualifier hnb659fds, so knowing account IDs enables takeover research.
    • SQS example: Guessing queue URLs from account IDs plus wordlists across billions of candidates found 209 readable and writable queues.
    • Other examples: ECR policies allowing any AWS account to push or pull, and CloudFront distributions pointing to deleted S3 buckets.
    • Awseye: Enter an account ID to see up to 100 discovered resources, activity status and public exposure; opt-in alerts via a temporary deny-all role.
    • Discovery techniques: Scraping GitHub, NuGet and Pastebin; listing public resources like shared EBS snapshots; guessing names; S3 HTTP HEAD checks.
    • Hostnames tracked: amazonaws.com, on.aws, cloudfront.net, awsapps.com, elasticbeanstalk.com, awsapprunner.com, amplifyapp.com.
    • Rationale: Cites Orca’s 2023 honeypot report of leaked keys used within minutes; defenders rarely build such datasets themselves.
    • Caveats: Vendor content; dataset admitted incomplete, mentions include false positives, and no accuracy metrics are given.

    Takeaway: Look up your account IDs in Awseye, change predictable defaults like the CDK qualifier, and fix resource policies or dangling DNS that only obscurity protects.

  • How to use AWS Resource Control Policies
    summary

    TL;DR: Wiz explains AWS Resource Control Policies (RCPs), the org-wide resource-side counterpart to SCPs that also constrains principals outside your Organization, with example perimeter policies and rollout caveats.

    • What RCPs are: AWS Organizations policies that grant nothing and cap who can access resources, overriding even a bucket policy that makes data public.
    • Supported services: Five at writing: S3, STS, SQS, Secrets Manager and KMS.
    • vs SCPs: SCPs only bind principals in your Organization, so an attacker granting an external account via resource policy bypasses them; RCPs do not.
    • Identity perimeter: Deny sts:AssumeRole unless aws:PrincipalOrgID or a listed aws:PrincipalAccount matches, exempting aws:PrincipalIsAWSService.
    • GitHub OIDC fix: Deny sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity when token.actions.githubusercontent.com:sub is null or not repo:<org>/*, closing wildcard trust policies.
    • Other uses: Enforce TLS 1.2+ on all S3 access once, block external sharing, and deny actions like s3:DeleteObject even to compromised admins.
    • Caveats: No dry-run or audit mode; denials return a generic AccessDenied; RCPs do not follow accounts moved to another OU or Organization.
    • Visibility gaps: Access Analyzer shows who has external access but not how; CloudTrail data events are off by default.
    • Rollout: Review logs first, test in a sandbox, promote dev to staging to production in groups, and review logs after.

    Takeaway: Deploy an RCP blocking AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity from GitHub repos outside your org, after checking Access Analyzer and CloudTrail for legitimate external access.

  • Kube-Policies: Guardrails for Apps Running in Kubernetes
    summary

    TL;DR: Square describes Kube-Policies, its in-house OPA-based Kubernetes admission layer that replaced Pod Security Policies with staged, guardrail-style validating and mutating policies.

    • Problem: Kubernetes defaults favor speed over security, and PSPs and Pod Security Admission did not fit Square’s varied platforms.
    • Alternatives rejected: K-Rail, Gatekeeper and Kyverno each missed some of seven requirements, including dry runs, exceptions and observability.
    • How it works: OPA in server mode evaluates Rego policies behind Kubernetes validating and mutating admission webhooks.
    • Staged modes: Validating policies go monitor, warn (HTTP Warnings header), deny; mutating policies go dry run, then patch.
    • Example policies: Deny privilege escalation, hostIPC and hostNetwork; mutate to readOnlyRootFilesystem, drop capabilities and runAsNonRoot.
    • Testing: Rego unit tests, e2e tests on ephemeral kind clusters checking audit logs, plus opa check --strict, regal and CodeQL.
    • Observability: OPA Prometheus metrics, audit-log annotations and apiserver_admission_webhook_rejection_count; denial spikes flag attacks or bugs.
    • Safety nets: Critical allowlist for aws-node and kube-proxy, a breakglass bypass, and an explicit fail-open vs fail-closed choice.
    • Limits: Policies are baked into the image, so changes need staged rollouts; exceptions need a ticket, PR and two-team approval.

    Takeaway: Roll out admission policies as monitor, warn, then deny, prefer safe mutations, and build breakglass plus system-component allowlists before enforcing.

  • How to Harden GitHub Actions: The Unofficial Guide
    summary

    TL;DR: Wiz’s updated GitHub Actions hardening cheat sheet, revised after tj-actions (2025) and the 2026 trivy-action and Axios attacks, covering settings, workflow pitfalls, secrets and runners.

    • Incidents: tj-actions cascaded from a vulnerable workflow to leaked PATs to poisoned actions; attackers force-pushed 75 of 76 trivy-action tags.
    • Axios: Malicious 1.14.1 and 0.30.4 were live about three hours, via an inserted plain-crypto-js dependency.
    • Pwn requests: pull_request_target and workflow_run run with secrets; checking out fork code is dangerous even after GitHub’s December 2025 change.
    • Injection: Interpolating issue titles, branch names or labels into run: enables command injection; writes to GITHUB_ENV or GITHUB_PATH can inject LD_PRELOAD.
    • Credentials: actions/checkout persists tokens in .git/config that can leak via artifacts; set persist-credentials: false.
    • Third-party actions: Only SHA pinning guarantees the same code; transitive weakly pinned actions still expose you; use SHA-pin enforcement.
    • Cooldowns: Wait 7-14 days before adopting new versions, estimated to catch 80-90% of supply chain attacks; pinact --min-age 7 or Renovate minimumReleaseAge.
    • Settings: Read-only default GITHUB_TOKEN, permissions: {} per workflow, no Actions PR approval, avoid toJson(secrets) and secrets: inherit.
    • Runners: Use ephemeral runners grouped by trust, default-deny egress, never self-hosted on public repos; prefer OIDC and GitHub Apps.

    Takeaway: Run zizmor in CI, SHA-pin actions with a 7-day cooldown, set permissions: {}, and audit every pull_request_target workflow now.
